#3079. Figs Stock Quietly Jumped 53.8% in October. Here's Why.

Figs barely made any news last month. Its stock jumped 53.8% anyway.

Figs (FIGS +5.38%) had a fantastic October. The scrubs maker's stock jumped 53.8% during the month, according to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence. Meanwhile, the S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) held steady with a minuscule 0.9% drop. The funny thing is, Figs barely said a word. No earnings report, no big deal, no splashy acquisitions. Wall Street simply woke up and noticed how good the company's last quarter really was.

The company's October press releases were limited to seasonal product launches, including a fall color collection and a line of children's scrubs timed for Halloween. Searches for Figs news during the month turned up more recipes and pantry tips than corporate announcements.

The real spark came on Oct. 12, when an industry review of second-quarter results named Figs the best performer among apparel and accessories companies. Looking back, that quarter was a beauty. Revenue hit $196.6 million, 5.6% above expectations, and earnings of $0.15 per share more than doubled the $0.07 consensus. Analysts had raised full-year earnings estimates by roughly 23% over the past quarter, and several firms lifted their price targets.

Investors noticed all at once. Figs jumped 12.4% that day on more than triple its usual trading volume, and Investor's Business Daily crowned it the top stock in the apparel industry. Momentum investors love that kind of signal.

The options market tells a fun story, too. On Oct. 7, traders loaded up on put options, betting the stock would fall. Within a week, the crowd flipped and started buying calls instead. Add a short interest of 13.3% of the float, and you get a cluster of bears suddenly scrambling for the exits. The combo is a great recipe for fruity volatility.

This wasn't a sectorwide party, either. Some apparel peers posted double-digit gains in October, but none came close to Figs.

After a run like that, Figs isn't cheap anymore. The stock trades at about 68 times trailing earnings and 58 times next year's expected profits. It even sits above Wall Street's average price target of $21.12, which tells you analysts are struggling to keep up with the market momentum.

The company reports third-quarter results after the closing bell on Thursday, Nov. 5, and the bar is now sky-high. Investors want more of the same: strong sales, fatter margins, and fewer discounts. Anything less could let some air out of this balloon. That may be a weird thing to say about a maker of boring work clothes, but it's all true.

There's also a question mark around the supply chain. Since August, Figs has been working around a U.S. import restriction on one of its suppliers in the country of Jordan. For what it's worth, Jordan also has a proud dried-fig tradition. I have no idea what to make of that coincidence.

On the bright side, Figs has a healthy balance sheet with about $240 million in net cash and very little debt.

I'm not saying this stock can't keep climbing. But before buying in after a 53.8% month, I wouldn't let my attention wander from Thursday's earnings report. For better or worse, the report is likely to move Figs' stock price.

Anders Bylund has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Figs. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3078. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#3077. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 3: Still Moving Lower

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased again today, giving buyers modest relief from rates above 7%.

Mortgage rates are continuing to cool off as we head into November.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 6.93% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

A significant drop is eye-catching, but bear in mind that you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. Since September, mortgage rates have been hovering around 7%. That’s a full percentage point higher than they were at the beginning of 2026.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.43% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3076. How Much Is SpaceX's AI Business Really Worth? Here's the Math.

The "super intelligence" segment could be worth $11 trillion in several years.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -3.81%) is one of the most ambitious and aggressive companies on the planet. Among CEO Elon Musk's more hyperbolic assertions is that it could eventually be worth more than the entire economy of Earth. It has huge goals that extend beyond this planet into space, eyeing ventures such as deploying a fleet of data center satellites, mining asteroids, and colonizing Mars.

While those more ambitious undertakings will likely be further out, the company's biggest growth driver in the nearer term will be its artificial intelligence (AI) businesses, which now fall under its newly named "super intelligence" (SI) segment. This includes its AI infrastructure business, AI software engineering platform Cursor, its Grok large language model (LLM), and social media platform X.

In the third quarter, that segment's revenue climbed 830% from $813 million a year ago to $7.56 billion, making it the largest segment by revenue. Meanwhile, the segment reported an operating profit of $431 million and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) of nearly $5 billion.

The bulk of the SI segment's value will come from its AI infrastructure business. SpaceX is on track to become the world's largest neocloud. It ended Q3 with compute capacity of 1.7 gigawatts, as it continues to build out its Colossus II data center cluster. It plans to end the year with between 2.5 gigawatts and 3 gigawatts of compute power.

However, that is small potatoes compared to where Musk and company plan to take SpaceX's computing capacity next year. After originally saying the company would likely end 2027 with total computing capacity closer to 10 gigawatts of power than 5 gigawatts, he upped his forecast: He now is setting a target for the company to reach 13 gigawatts by the end of next year. Meanwhile, Musk said his moonshot goal was to reach 20 gigawatts of capacity in 2027.

SpaceX has been renting some of its computing capacity on short-term monthly leases, and getting great rates for it. It has said that its chip and networking investments are reaching breakeven within one year and thinks it can push that down to around six months in the future.

Earlier this year, Musk announced that SpaceX would use Nvidia's newest Vera Rubin platform in its data centers going forward, as it was the best on the market. One gigawatt of Vera Rubin computing capacity, meanwhile, costs around $50 billion, and it said it was getting just above $50 per watt. That implies that 1 gigawatt of capacity is generating a little more than $50 billion for SpaceX in annual revenue.

As such, it is easy to map out how much revenue SpaceX could generate at current rates if it can add 10 gigawatts of computing capacity a year going forward. Management has talked about renting out half its capacity and using 50% internally, although we'll assume both can generate similar revenue. Place a sales multiple of 5 on this revenue, and its AI infrastructure business alone would be worth $10.75 trillion in 2030.

Meanwhile, you also have Cursor, which SpaceX recently acquired for $60 billion. Morgan Stanley projected that Cursor could end 2030 with annual recurring revenue (ARR) of $33 billion. Place a multiple of 10 times sales on that, and it would be worth $330 billion.

Data source: Author AI infrastructure, Grok, and share estimates, with Morgan Stanley estimates for Cursor. *These are speculative numbers.

Combined, that would value the company's AI business at around $11 trillion. Assuming that the shares undergo some dilution as it issues additional stock to pay for its AI infrastructure build-out, the value per share of the AI business alone could be worth $585 in five years; that doesn't include its rocket launch arm or the Starlink satellite broadband business.

That's a lot of potential for the stock, and while there are certainly risks, such as market prices per watt falling, it looks like the stock has a lot of upside from here over the next several years.

Geoffrey Seiler has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nvidia. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Both cards are useful for American Airlines flyers, but one offers more flexibility.

Airline miles, hotel points, travel insurance

This page includes information about these cards, currently unavailable on NerdWallet. The information has been collected by NerdWallet and has not been provided or reviewed by the card issuer.

Both the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard®  and Citi Strata Premier® Card can help you rack up AAdvantage miles. The former earns them directly; the latter transfers points to American Airlines at a 1:1 ratio. So which one is better for American Airlines flyers?

For earning rewards, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the winner. Although it's not an American Airlines credit card, it can make it easier to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases with its higher earning rates and the option to transfer ThankYou points to American.

Still, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® can help you achieve AAdvantage elite status and offers perks when flying American Airlines, including a free checked bag and preferred boarding. In 2027, the annual fee will increase, but the card will add new bonus categories and other benefits.

The Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better fit for travelers who want to earn American Airlines miles without committing all their rewards to a single airline program.

Although the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is an American Airlines credit card, the Citi Strata Premier® Card can be a faster way to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases.

That's because Citi Strata Premier® Card holders can transfer ThankYou points to American Airlines AAdvantage — and several other loyalty programs — at a 1:1 ratio.

Currently, the Citi Strata Premier® Card offers more rewards than the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® in every bonus category. On Jan. 24, 2027, when the benefits on the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® change, that will change — but only in a couple of narrowly defined categories: select streaming services and eligible Rides and Rails purchases, which include taxis, rideshares, subways and trains. Unless those categories make up a significant portion of your spending, the Citi Strata Premier® Card will likely yield more points overall, even if your ultimate goal is American Airlines miles.

» Learn more: 11 Benefits of the Citi Strata Premier® Card

The Citi Strata Premier® Card also offers a $100 annual hotel benefit when you book a single hotel stay of $500 or more, excluding taxes and fees, through Citi Travel. Using the full benefit can more than offset the card’s annual fee, and hotel bookings through Citi Travel earn 10 ThankYou points per dollar.

Just keep in mind that booking through Citi Travel may mean giving up hotel loyalty program benefits you would receive by booking directly. Whether the $100 hotel perk offers enough value to justify booking through Citi Travel will depend on the property, the rates available and how much you value those hotel loyalty benefits.

» Learn more: Is the Citi Strata Premier® Card worth its annual fee?

One of the biggest advantages of the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the ability to earn ThankYou points, which can be transferred to several airline and hotel loyalty programs. If you want the option to transfer points to American Airlines but also see value in the card’s other airline and hotel partners, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better choice.

Transfer ratios will vary depending on which card you have.

Although you can transfer points from the Citi Strata Premier® Card to American Airlines, you won’t get any Loyalty Points.

» Learn more: Guide to American Airlines elite status

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is a strong choice if you want American Airlines benefits. Keep in mind that the card is getting revamped in 2027 with a higher annual fee and new benefits. See NerdWallet's coverage for more details.

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® comes with several benefits that can significantly offset the annual fee:

Free checked bag: Get a free first checked bag on domestic American Airlines itineraries for the primary cardholder and up to four companions traveling on the same reservation. If you check bags with American, this benefit alone could make the card worth getting.

Preferred boarding: The primary cardholder and up to four companions on the same reservation receive preferred boarding.

American Airlines Vacations discount: Save 10% on eligible American Airlines Vacations packages.

Inflight discount: 25% off inflight food and beverage purchases. This perk is being replaced on Jan. 24, 2027, with an inflight statement credit.

$125 flight discount: Spend at least $20,000 during your card membership year and renew the card to receive a $125 American Airlines flight discount. This perk is also changing in 2027; see below.

Starting Jan. 24, 2027, the card will offer:

Inflight statement credits: Receive up to $100 back in statement credits annually on inflight purchases. This replaces the old 25% off inflight food and drink purchases.

$150 flight discount: Spend at least $20,000 during your card membership year and renew the card to receive a $150 American Airlines flight discount. This replaces the $125 flight discount; the spending requirement is the same.

The inflight statement credits, in particular, will be easy to maximize and could potentially offset a meaningful portion of the card’s annual fee. Combined with the ability to earn Loyalty Points toward elite status and the card’s other perks, that can make the card more valuable for American’s flyers.

» Learn more: Is an American Airlines credit card worth it?

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® offers Turo credits. You receive up to $180 annually in statement credits on eligible Turo rentals, with up to $30 back per eligible rental.

On Jan. 24, 2027, the card will add several more perks:

Walmart+: Primary cardholders receive a complimentary annual Walmart+ membership, valued at $98.

Global Entry or TSA PreCheck: Receive up to a $120 application fee credit every four years.

FOX One: Get 50% off an annual FOX One subscription.

Travel protections: Get trip delay protection, trip cancellation and interruption protection, worldwide car rental insurance and lost baggage protection.

It’s unusual to see a Global Entry or TSA PreCheck credit on a card with an annual fee under $200. When that perk kicks in, the card could become a good option for travelers who prioritize AAdvantage miles but also want a credit card that fully reimburses the application fee for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck.

» Learn more: American Airlines credit card benefits guide

Cardholders receive 1 Loyalty Point for every eligible AAdvantage mile earned from purchases. That means everyday spending can move you closer to AAdvantage elite status, in addition to earning miles you can redeem for flights. American’s entry-level elite status requires 40,000 Loyalty Points, so putting significant spending on the card can help you reach that threshold.

If you want to earn rewards on everyday spending and have the flexibility to move those points to American Airlines — and you don’t need the airline-specific benefits — the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the best choice.

That said, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® makes more sense for frequent American Airlines flyers who can take advantage of benefits such as a free checked bag and preferred boarding, or for travelers who want to earn Loyalty Points toward American Airlines elite status through credit card spending.

#3072. ​Houston, We Have A Communications Problem: Why Silence After A Breach Is Killing Trust

By Ian Weisberger, co-founder and CEO of CoinRoutes.

In financial services, 2026 has been the year of the constant cyberattack. Whether it is wealth managers losing clients’ Social Security numbers to stolen logins, employees tricked into revealing customer data or botnets hammering firms with AI-generated password attempts, these threats are hitting companies on a near-weekly basis.

With AI making attacks easier, the pace is unlikely to slow. Financial services companies understand that breaches are becoming a fact of life. What they cannot accept, though, is being left in the dark. In financial services, especially in fintech and fast-growing areas such as decentralized finance, communication after a breach has too often been lacking.

​To understand the problem, it’s worth starting with what good looks like. In September, Revolut was reported to have suffered a data breach involving fraudulent government data requests. The firm acknowledged the incident, said it had contacted affected customers directly and described steps it took to block the fraudulent requests and notify relevant authorities.

Later that month, when Bitget reportedly suffered a hack involving more than $350 million, CEO Gracy Chen publicly addressed the impact, explaining the withdrawal suspension and stating that the company would cover the losses.

Neither company had every answer at the outset, but these examples illustrate an important principle of incident communications: acknowledge what is known, explain what remains uncertain and give customers clear information about the steps being taken. That approach can help preserve trust even when the full picture is still emerging.​​

Not every incident is accompanied by the information clients need to understand the risks. A few weeks ago, we began receiving questions about a cyberattack involving a portfolio management service provider we work with. Our systems were not affected, but we share several clients with the provider, leaving us with questions we could not yet answer.

At the time of writing, we were still seeking details about the incident, including what information may have been exposed and how the attackers gained access. Although the provider had indicated that it would share a post-incident review, we did not yet have the information needed to address our clients’ concerns.

Many clients came to us looking for answers, and we had concerns that trading histories and portfolio data could have been exposed. For a trading firm, that can be enough to work out its strategies, among the most valuable things it owns. We asked for details that would have put those concerns to rest, but they did not arrive.​

In financial services, a provider’s clients can collectively manage hundreds of billions of dollars, and they have their own obligations to investors, shareholders and regulators. If providers cannot explain what data was taken, they cannot tell their own clients, and one firm’s silence puts everyone in a difficult position.

This matters most in the newer corners of finance. Sectors such as decentralized finance are still earning the trust of businesses and consumers, with far less reputation to fall back on than an established bank. When something goes wrong, silence costs them more than anyone.

In our own operations, we’ve made timely incident reporting a contractual obligation, committing to investigate incidents and provide clients with a post-mortem within 24 to 48 hours. Because we run a trading system around the clock, we write one even for five-minute outages. For consumer-facing businesses, I would expect a first message closer to an hour. The principle is simple: Say what you know, admit what you don’t yet know and come back as soon as you know more.

As an industry, we need to agree on what accountability looks like and stop pretending breaches don’t happen. At a minimum, every firm should commit to acknowledging breaches within hours, keeping open lines with key partners and providing continuous updates until the picture is complete.

Holding providers accountable starts before anything goes wrong. These are the questions I’d ask any technology provider handling your data before a breach occurs:

1. If something goes wrong, when will I first hear from you? Not when the investigation is finished, but when you first tell me there is a problem, followed by regular updates as the picture becomes clearer.

2. What exactly will you tell me? Telling me it has been fixed is not enough. I want to know what was taken, how it happened, what has changed and whether it could happen again. Above all, I want to know what the stolen information now allows someone to do.

3. If I ask whether my data was affected, can you give me a straight answer? When one of our clients had a messaging account phished, we pulled the IP addresses that had accessed their account with us, traced what had happened and gave them a clear answer. That is the standard I expect.

4. How are passwords, API keys and other secrets protected? Sensitive credentials should be protected through appropriate key-management systems, with access tightly restricted and unnecessary exposure in application memory minimized. Ask whether keys are protected by dedicated key-management hardware or an equivalent, and who has access.

5. If someone gets into one client’s account, can they reach anyone else’s? Each client’s data and credentials should be kept separate, and logins, credentials, trading and client-facing services should be walled off from one another, so that if one area is compromised, the damage stops there.

6. What can someone do with a stolen key, login or access token? Credentials do get stolen. What matters is how much access they provide, how long it stays valid, whether its use would be detected and whether connections are restricted to approved IP addresses. Stolen customer read-only keys should not allow the attacker to access funds or other clients’ information.

When I first came into this industry in 2017, what attracted me was its openness and the competitive energy that came with it. Today, that openness matters more than ever. As cyberattacks become more sophisticated and easier to carry out, breaches will keep happening, and no firm can promise they won’t. What every firm can promise is to communicate honestly and quickly when they do.​

#3070. Prediction: Here's What a $10,000 Investment in Vanguard's Tech ETF Could Be Worth in 25 Years

AI is currently driving high growth in the tech sector.

Artificial intelligence (AI) has been driving double-digit market gains for four years, and one of the best ways to play the trend is to buy the Vanguard's Information Technology ETF (VGT +0.23%). It's beating the S&P 500 this year by more than double, 25% to 12%, and it's beaten the market by a wide margin over many years. Here's what a $10,000 investment might be worth in 25 years.

The information technology ETF is Vanguard's highest-gaining ETF over the past 10 years, with an exceptional annualized gain of 23.9%. If you had invested $10,000 10 years ago, you'd have $85,910 today. That's more than double what you'd have if you'd invested in the standard Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. Since inception in 2004, though, the ETF's gains have been more modest at 14.6%.

The ETF has 318 components today and is anchored by the world's biggest companies, Nvidia, Apple, and Microsoft.

Stocks don't maintain the same growth they have delivered in the past, because as they get bigger, they slow down. However, ETFs work differently because they track indexes. Stocks get swapped out as necessary to maintain standards and growth levels, which means the index can continue to gain at similar levels.

For this exercise, let's assume that the ETF can continue to gain at an annualized rate of 15% over the next 25 years. That would turn a $10,000 investment into $329,190.

It's important to keep in mind that this is just an estimate, and the results over such a long period could vary.

Mortgage rates jumped after stronger-than-expected jobs data raised expectations for a Fed rate hike next month.

Mortgage rates are higher today on the heels of strong labor market data released yesterday.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points higher than yesterday but 25 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates are always on the move, so if you're tracking rates day-to-day, you're going to see a lot of volatility.

As for today’s jump: Job openings hit a four-month high of 7.5 million in September, according to the Labor Department’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey released yesterday. Hiring picked up, too, while layoffs declined. Friday’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other.

To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3066. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

Technology deployments across sales and administrative teams support client retention and long-term organic revenue expansion.

#3062. Why Individual AI Gains Don’t Add Up To Company Value

Dr. Son Nguyen is Orient Software’s CTO & co-founded Neurond AI, companies specializing in software development, AI, & data science services

​A few months ago, I asked our DevOps and finance teams the same question: What does it cost us each month to run the cloud infrastructure for one of our larger projects? Using different AI tools and data, they returned with different answers.

I couldn’t tell which answer to trust, where the information came from, whether it was current and whether correcting it would help others.

Now, imagine this across thousands of employees and AI tools.

Individual productivity is clearly improving with AI. A Gallup survey from May 2026 found that 65% of employees at organizations that have implemented AI reported a positive effect on their productivity and efficiency.​

But do thousands of assistants, each holding its own fragment of context, add up to a smarter company? I don’t think it necessarily does. While individual AI use can make each employee faster, only a company brain makes the company smarter.

McKinsey’s August 2026 report on the state of AI found that 89% of organizations are now using AI, and 80% of respondents say AI has improved their individual productivity. However, only 37% attribute at least some earnings before interest and taxes impact to AI, which was unchanged over the previous year.

We have become quite good at giving employees access to AI, but what happens to everything they learn from using it?

Each AI chat is its own island. Employees ask questions, refine answers and share business context. Yet that learning often stays within one interaction or workflow, and the next employee starts from scratch.

MIT’s NANDA initiative calls this the “learning gap.” Its preliminary study of 52 organizations found that most enterprise AI systems struggle to retain feedback, adapt or improve over time. The result? Thousands of employees become more productive without the organization becoming proportionally smarter.

Based on my experience, four issues explain why personal gains rarely become organizational gains:

1. Knowledge is scattered and often leaves with people. An employee develops a useful prompt, workflow or understanding of a customer issue, but that knowledge rarely becomes part of the company’s shared memory.

2. Companies lack a shared definition of what their information means. Without it, AI spreads existing inconsistencies faster.

3. Answers lack evidence. If AI doesn’t show the source of the information, it can produce an answer that sounds right without giving the organization a reliable basis for acting on it.

4. There is no shared learning loop. An employee discovers that an AI answer is wrong and corrects it, but that correction stays in their conversation. The next person can encounter the same error.

This is why I believe the quality of the underlying system matters at least as much as the model’s intelligence.

The BEAVER benchmark found that state-of-the-art AI agent frameworks using GPT-5.2 scored only 10.8% on questions drawn from real private data warehouses. As I discussed in my earlier article on AI system design, the model is only one layer. The surrounding architecture determines how information is retrieved, interpreted, governed and used.

A smarter model does not automatically create a smarter company.

Companies need a system that can turn individual interactions into shared organizational intelligence. I call this a company brain. The goal is to make organizational knowledge usable in decision-making. ​

A company brain is neither a chatbot on top of a data lake nor traditional knowledge management. It is not an off-the-shelf product or a platform project.

Instead, it is an operating model. Vendors can provide technology, but cannot decide what information is authoritative, who can access it or who is accountable when AI gets something wrong. When one person learns something valuable, that knowledge should become available to the right people and AI systems, with appropriate governance and evidence.

Before calling an AI system a company brain, I would test it against five requirements.

• Evidence: Every answer should show its source, owner and last update. I’ve explained before why every AI agent decision requires strong evidence. Without evidence, an AI answer may be useful, but not accountable. This matters most in regulated work, where every figure must be traceable. For high-risk uses such as hiring, credit and education, the EU AI Act requires high-quality data and traceability logs from December 2, 2027, for any company offering such systems in the EU. For those uses, the evidence standard is about to become a compliance standard.

• Meaning: Departments must share consistent definitions. If sales and finance mean different things by “revenue,” connecting their data won’t resolve the inconsistency.

• Permissions: People and AI agents should access only what they’re authorized to see and do, especially as AI moves from answering questions to taking actions.

• Learning: When an error is corrected once, the correction should improve the system for everyone. Outdated knowledge should also have a way to expire rather than remain available forever.

• Ownership: Someone at the executive level must own the outcome, with a dedicated budget and clear accountability. Otherwise, a company brain risks becoming everyone’s priority but nobody’s responsibility.

You don’t need a platform decision to begin building the brain. Start with questions your sales, finance, operations, customer success or engineering teams need to answer every week.

Measure how long it takes to get a trustworthy answer with a verifiable source, owner and date. Call this your time to trusted answers.

Then ask: Who corrects errors? Does that learning reach others? Can AI agents act safely within their permissions? These questions reveal more about AI maturity than license counts or daily usage.

To achieve success with enterprise AI, turning what one person learns into organizational intelligence is more important than making individuals faster. Your job isn’t to ask which model to buy, but what your AI knows about your company, and whether it can prove it.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3061. Piedmont Realty Trust: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Refinancing debt at lower rates while shifting capital to high-growth markets sets up durable earnings growth without balance sheet strain.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Piedmont Realty Trust (PDM +5.33%) announced a third consecutive increase to its full-year earnings outlook as landlord pricing power expands. Commercial tenants face shrinking options for premium workspace, which lets property owners push net effective rent, defined as rent earned after accounting for concessions and capital expenditures, to record levels.

Tight commercial office inventory allows Piedmont Realty Trust to achieve record rental pricing across major Sunbelt markets, defined as warm-weather southern U.S. regions. Existing tenants are requesting early renewals to lock in space before availability vanishes entirely.

Refinancing high-cost debt improves cash flows by cutting overall interest expense across the business. Piedmont Realty Trust replaced high-coupon bonds with lower-rate convertible obligations, leaving zero debt maturities until late 2028.

Selling noncore assets provides capital to fund targeted property acquisitions without adding debt. Piedmont Realty Trust is exiting slower-growth markets to focus resources on higher-yielding commercial hubs.

Leasing activity continues accelerating across key regional markets. High customer retention and scarce competitor construction give Piedmont Realty Trust durable pricing discipline.

A signed pipeline will flow into core funds from operations, a standard earnings metric for real estate trusts, as free rent periods end while refinancing lowers annual debt costs.

If your pet is young and healthy, the cost of coverage may not seem worth it. But that math can change fast.

Senior Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

It feels natural to protect your pet’s health with insurance in the same way you protect your loved ones with health insurance. After all, fur babies are part of the family.

But if you’ve shopped for pet insurance recently, you might have cringed at the cost. Should you really pay hundreds of dollars every year for something you might not use? For some pet parents, the answer is yes, even if their pet is young and healthy. And that’s partly because of how pet insurance handles pre-existing conditions.

A pre-existing condition is any illness or injury that shows symptoms before your pet insurance policy takes effect. Pet insurers usually won’t cover these conditions.

Dr. Rebecca Greenstein, chief veterinarian at Kleinburg Veterinary Hospital and veterinary expert for rover.com, says she’s seeing an increase in claim denials based on pre-existing conditions. “The whole point of insurance is you want broad spectrum coverage for a variety of things,” she says. “By the time they're excluding major organ systems and anything related to that, you may be paying monthly for coverage that doesn't actually help you when you need to go make a claim.”

If you’re considering pet insurance, it’s best to buy a policy before your pet needs it.

Your pet’s pre-existing conditions will determine which treatments and procedures insurers cover down the road.

Say your dog has a history of allergies. If you buy a policy after your four-footed friend has shown symptoms, pet insurers typically won’t pay for allergy shots or other treatments. The exclusion might also apply to related problems like itchiness or ear infections, says Dr. Michael Goldstein, medical director at Billerica Veterinary Hospital and Thrive Pet Healthcare.

He offers the example of a dog who goes to the vet for a hotspot one year and an ear infection the next. If you got pet insurance for your dog between those visits, the insurer might deny your claim because both issues could be caused by underlying allergies.

The best way around this type of scenario is to buy coverage when your pet is young, before any symptoms develop.

» MORE: The best pet insurance for pre-existing conditions

It’s tricky to figure out if pet insurance will actually save you money in the long run. The most common type of plan covers accidents and illness. It costs an average of $836 for dogs and $435 for cats each year, according to the North American Pet Health Insurance Association (NAPHIA). You could pay these premiums for years before an emergency surgery or chronic illness makes its way to your financial doorstep.

It’s impossible to know for sure whether your pet will stay healthy or run up sky-high vet bills. But you can assess the potential value of pet insurance by looking at common chronic conditions that pets develop with age and how much it costs to treat them.

For instance, “cats generally, as younger animals, don't have a lot of problems,” Goldstein says. “But as they get older, there are definitely common issues. Older cats in particular get chronic kidney disease.”

In cats over age 10, the Cornell Feline Health Center estimates that as many as 40% have chronic kidney disease. The annual cost to treat this condition can top $1,200.

Another example is atopic dermatitis from skin allergies, a common ailment affecting as many as 15% of dogs, according to the Cornell Richard P. Riney Canine Health Center. A single vet visit to address these itchy, persistent symptoms can cost $250 or more. And while that’s a fraction of what you might pay for pet insurance, costs of chronic conditions can add up over your pet’s lifetime.

Vet bills can also balloon quickly if chronic conditions require surgery. One example of this is hip dysplasia, a genetic disorder that can cause arthritis and pain in large-breed dogs. Depending on the procedure, you could pay up to $10,000 for hip surgery.

Many pet insurance plans cover treatments for hip dysplasia, including surgical costs, as long as it's not a pre-existing condition. However, some policies have waiting periods of six to 12 months for orthopedic issues like hip dysplasia. If symptoms develop during that waiting period, the condition will be considered pre-existing. That’s another argument for buying your pet’s coverage earlier rather than later.

Most importantly, having insurance means you can choose the best treatment for your pet no matter the cost. “It's really tragic when it's a life and death decision that is needed and it’s based on someone's financial constraints,” Greenstein says. “What is best and what's affordable often are not on the same side of the spectrum.”

One option to keep pet insurance affordable is to buy an accident-only plan. This type of coverage is usually much cheaper, costing about $190 annually for dogs and $112 for cats, according to NAPHIA. These plans cover injuries resulting from accidents, which include not just wounds and broken bones but also objects or toxins your pet might swallow.

However, an accident-only plan won’t help with some of the most common vet expenses. Illnesses, not injuries, are behind most claims submitted to pet insurer Lemonade, the company said in an email. In the past year, more than 60% of Lemonade's pet insurance claims stemmed from illnesses. That’s twice as many as the next-largest claim category, which was preventive care. A similar trend can be found in data from Nationwide, where chronic diseases make up a clear majority of pet insurance claims submitted to the company in 2025.

If you opt for an accident-only plan or no coverage at all, it’s still smart to set aside some savings for those inevitable pet emergencies and illnesses. This pool of funds can keep you from having to make difficult decisions about your pet’s care based solely on the cost of treatment.

#3057. Stock Market Today, Nov. 4: Oracle Falls on Canceled $7 Billion Tencent Chip-Leasing Deal

On Nov. 4, 2026, Oracle shares slid in premarket trading as reports of a scrapped Tencent deal renewed focus on how it funds its AI buildout.

Oracle (ORCL -1.95%), an enterprise cloud software and AI infrastructure provider, closed at $123.77, down 1.92%. In premarket trading, reports that it canceled a $7 billion Tencent chip-leasing deal and renewed attention on AI funding plans weighed on the shares.Trading volume reached 111.4M shares, coming in about 241% above its three-month average of 32.7M shares.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) closed at 7,689, up 1.59%, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.44%) ended at 25,737, up 1.44%. Among enterprise software and cloud infrastructure peers, Microsoft closed at $555.80, up 6.68%, and SAP closed at $240.05, up 4.23%, keeping Oracle's relative weakness in focus.

Although Oracle stock is down today, it seems to have fared well in its Wednesday performance, given the circumstances it faced. Aside from the cancellation of the Tencent deal, many data center stocks plunged as several politicians opposed to data center development won in the 2026 Midterm elections.

However, the issues that might affect companies like CoreWeave or Nebius appear to not apply directly to Oracle, as it has legacy businesses that should bolster the company. Investors may notice the 1.9% stock price decline was far less than the neocloud companies.

Moreover, its condition presumably makes it easier to raise cash, as Oracle also completed a $20 billion stock and debt offering. Although debt and coupons sold at a yield as high as 8.65%, that is much lower than some neocloud stocks, whose debt yields have risen well into the teens.

Additionally, Oracle expressed an intention to limit additional capital-market funding to $20 billion through fiscal 2030, implying no additional fundraising for the next few years. That may offer relief to some investors in the cloud stock who feel its debt burden has become too high.

For now, Oracle stock trades near a 52-week low. However, it has shown a continued ability to raise funds, and assuming it keeps its word not to raise additional funds until after fiscal 2030, it is in a stronger position to eventually win back the confidence of investors.

#3055. The Future Of AI Will Be Decided In The Physical World

Daisuke Okanohara is CEO of Matlantis Coporation, and Co-founder and CTO of Preferred Networks, Inc.

​Every conversation about the future of AI circles back to the same assumptions: bigger models, more data, more compute. We treat progress as a contest of intelligence. Yet the constraint that will decide how far AI goes over the next decade has little to do with intelligence and a great deal to do with physics.​

AI is running into two physical walls that no amount of clever software can talk its way past.

In a modern AI system, far more energy goes into moving data around than into performing the calculations themselves. The calculations inside a chip are remarkably cheap. The real expense lies in shuttling enormous volumes of data between memory and processor, billions of times every second. By some engineering estimates, that movement can consume ten to a hundred times more energy than the computation it serves.​

For two decades, processing power has raced ahead while the speed of feeding data to it has crawled, and today’s large language models are limited less by how fast they can think than by how fast they can be fed.​

The bill lands on the balance sheet and on the power grid. Running the AI we expect to deploy across the economy could require tens of gigawatts of additional electricity. The ceiling on AI’s growth is becoming an energy ceiling, and that energy is spent in a place few people think to look: the distance data must travel.

The second wall sits one level deeper, in the materials our hardware is built from.​

Better chips, better batteries, better energy systems: nearly every hardware advance the AI era depends on eventually comes down to discovering or improving a material. Materials discovery has always been agonizingly slow. The number of possible combinations is effectively limitless, while the number humanity has tested remains vanishingly small.​

That mattered less when hardware progress had room to run elsewhere. It matters enormously now, because materials are precisely where both walls have to be broken: new architectures to shorten the distance data travels, new materials to move electricity with less waste, new chemistries to store energy more densely.

The instinct is to spend through these walls, buying more chips and standing up more data centers. That buys time without changing the trajectory, because it keeps optimizing the very designs that created the bottleneck.​

The more durable path is using AI to redesign the physical layers beneath AI itself.​

For most of computing history, those layers were designed in isolation. Materials scientists handed properties to device engineers, who handed components to chip designers, who handed hardware to system builders. When the binding constraints are physical and cut across every layer, the layers have to be designed together. Having spent years building both AI chips and the AI tools that design materials, I see this cross-layer approach as the defining shift of the coming decade.​

Three changes follow in practice. Organizationally, teams that once optimized their own slice hold a shared goal and work across team boundaries, so a system-level question like halving energy per query gets answered partly at the materials level. In process, sequential handoffs become joint development, with materials treated as design variables from the start and simulation as a standard gate before capital commits to hardware. In budget, funding follows the genuine bottleneck on the path to the goal.​

Materials discovery is where this change is furthest along. Understanding how a material behaves once meant either a physical experiment or a simulation too slow to model anything realistic. AI has broken that trade-off by learning the rules of how atoms interact, reaching near-laboratory accuracy at speeds that were recently impossible. At Matlantis, we have watched work that would once have demanded roughly 20 years of conventional calculation finish in about a week. Companies, including Volkswagen and Panasonic, now use this kind of simulation to explore materials at a pace no traditional lab could match.​

Speed of that magnitude changes decisions by moving materials questions earlier. Screen candidates in parallel with product design instead of ahead of it. Verify early whether materials that are scarce, regulated, or exposed to supply chain risk can be replaced, and whether promising but untested ones hold up. Make go or no-go roadmap calls on simulation evidence in weeks instead of lab evidence in years.

Picture where this leads. AI agents survey the research, propose candidate materials and run thousands of simulations in parallel around the clock, sending only the rare promising candidate to an automated lab for physical confirmation. The bottleneck shifts from human hours to the genuine limits of physics and experiment, a far better problem to have.​

Leaders can prepare now with three moves. First, build an in-house team that can read AI results critically, because the value of ten thousand simulations depends on the judgment applied to the ten that matter. Second, restructure the development cycle around that speed, with simulation running from day one rather than after prototypes exist. Third, choose partners that span layers: the suppliers, foundries and research institutions that own the parts of the stack you don’t.​

A caveat: even far more capable AI will never fully solve the physical world, because reality holds layers of complexity that cannot be predicted from the rules beneath them. The useful question is where irreversibility begins. Trust AI to explore, rank and explain at a scale humans cannot. Reserve for humans every decision that cannot be undone: synthesizing a material, committing capital, taking a product to market. Experiment keeps the final word. AI changes how fast and how intelligently we can run it.​

The decisive edge over the next decade will belong to whomever understands that AI’s progress now runs through the physical world, and who designs across every layer of the stack rather than optimizing a single one. Where physics sets the limit, AI is becoming the way through.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3053. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3052. Pharming: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

A rare-disease player with a stabilized core product is now targeting a market 26 times larger, fueled by cash generation that needs no external financing.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Pharming (PHAR -3.64%) announced total revenue grew 21% to $117.6 million. Operating profit increased 89% to $29.9 million as the business expanded sales of its primary rare disease treatments. Strong cash generation gives the business funding to expand its clinical drug pipeline without needing extra financing.

RUCONEST, a treatment for hereditary angioedema (HAE, a rare genetic condition causing severe swelling), returned to revenue growth after earlier quarterly declines. Management maintained over 90% of active patients despite new oral competition, stabilizing demand among high-volume users.

Joenja, a therapy for activated phosphoinositide 3-kinase delta syndrome (APDS, a rare primary immunodeficiency causing immune system dysfunction), expanded through new international launches and U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) pediatric approvals. International sales now generate roughly one-third of total Joenja revenue.

Management's advancing leniolisib into common variable immune deficiency (CVID, a primary immunodeficiency disorder affecting immune function), a market 26 times larger than APDS in patient prevalence. Positive phase 2 data in related primary immunodeficiencies (PIDs) provides proof of concept for the broader indication.

Operating profit is surging as commercial sales expand, and management isn't hiding its satisfaction. High patient retention and specialized manufacturing create durable competitive moats. Management is strategically watching channel inventory timing and pediatric adoption.

Disciplined execution and clinical expansion set up long-term growth across broader immunology markets.
