#3050. Management Mistakes That Throttle Innovation On Tech Teams

Tech leaders often say they want their teams to experiment and pursue new ideas. But even seemingly reasonable management practices can make creative risks feel too costly or time-consuming. When those pressures become part of everyday work, employees may become less willing to test unfamiliar approaches or challenge established ways of working.

The disconnect between encouraging innovation and making room for it isn’t always obvious to leaders. Here, members of Forbes Technology Council explore the management habits that can quietly hold innovation back and explain what leaders can do differently to support experimentation.

The mistake is making every experiment a delivery commitment. Having managed program managers and engineers, I’d separate learning goals from ship dates. Only 13% of AI users in Microsoft’s 2026 survey said reinvention was rewarded when results fell short. Set a question, a budget and a stop date; disproving the idea can still be a useful result. - Swapnil Upganlawar, Amazon

Blanket bans and strict restrictions on AI tools kill innovation. Blocking doesn’t stop adoption. It creates an AI lockdown paradox, where employees move their work to personal devices and accounts. Leaders end up with all of the security risk and none of the visibility. Instead of acting as the “Department of No,” leaders should put automated guardrails in place that let teams experiment safely. - Rick Caccia, WitnessAI

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Only rewarding safe goals undermines innovation. If people are judged only on goals, they make conservative plans and stop proposing big bets. We want people who set goals they might miss and run their area like owners. We judge them on how far they moved the business and what they learned. - Chris Szymansky, Fieldguide

Rushing teams into execution before giving them space to understand, experiment and learn stifles experimentation. Innovation suffers when leaders don’t take the time to deeply understand the problem, connect it to a meaningful business outcome, and bring the right cross-functional teams together. Release in small, meaningful increments; test, learn and iterate. Great innovation needs both freedom to experiment and discipline around outcomes. - Benu Aggarwal, Milestone, Inc.

Punishing failed experiments while celebrating innovation holds teams back. When leaders demand certainty before approving new ideas or judge every experiment by immediate returns, teams learn that playing it safe is rewarded. Innovation requires disciplined experimentation, clear boundaries and permission to learn. The real test of leadership is not how it celebrates success but how it responds when a well-designed experiment fails. - Maman Ibrahim, EugeneZonda

Overengineering experimentation quietly kills innovation. Discipline matters, but every experiment doesn’t need to become a project. Give teams a clear problem, guardrails and the autonomy to test, learn and iterate quickly. The goal isn’t less process. It’s making sure the process serves innovation—not the other way around. - Abhijit Kakhandiki, BMC Software

Filling every sprint to 100% quietly kills innovation. Leaders say they want experiments, then plan the roadmap so tightly that there is no room to try anything. People will not test a new idea when it means missing a committed deadline. If experimentation matters, it needs space on the calendar. Keep some capacity unplanned and protect it when delivery pressure builds. - Mudit Singh, TestMu AI (LambdaTest)

Starting with features instead of user problems kills innovation. When epics and features are defined before the team understands user needs, experimentation is compressed into implementation. UX and delivery teams are left optimizing a predetermined solution instead of using discovery to jointly design a better one. Engage UX strategists from the start. This gives teams the greatest autonomy to design the best solution. - April Ho-Nishimura, Infineon Technologies AG

Prioritizing trends and buzzwords over how comfortable employees are with adopting new technologies can undermine innovation. Leaders may see a new tool or tech stack as an opportunity to innovate, but forcing it on a team without considering their experience can have the opposite effect. Innovation works best when people have the time, support and freedom to explore technology rather than simply follow trends. - Artem Lalaiants, RiskSeal, Inc.

Treating the existing workflow as untouchable quietly kills innovation. In recent banking discussions, leaders emphasized redesigning the process before deciding where AI belongs. Asking teams to experiment while preserving every approval, handoff and metric limits what they can discover. Give teams permission to question how work gets done and test a better way with clear guardrails and outcomes. - Gregg Aldana, Appian

Demanding outcomes without fostering the culture that supports innovation is counterproductive. Leadership needs to put more emphasis on people as half of the factors in successful outcomes. Providing your people with a culture that fosters creativity, open debate and collaboration is necessary for experimentation and for turning that experimentation into commercial products. - Susan Odle, StorMagic

Giving teams a destination when what they need is a direction limits innovation. A list of approved projects only contains what leadership has imagined, so teams never get past those ideas. With AI, we set clear limits on data access, then handed every department a blank page and one direction: Try things and find your own use cases. Ideas came from every function, not from the plan we drew up in advance. - Alex Saric, Ivalua

Requiring a business case before anyone is allowed to try something can shut down experimentation. Leaders say they want experimentation, then ask for projected ROI on a two-week test, which guarantees that only safe, incremental ideas get proposed. Give teams a small preapproved budget and time box for experiments, and ask for a business case only after the test produces evidence. - Agung Dwi Sandi, rankpillar Group

Judging experiments by the same metrics as established products quietly kills innovation. Teams learn to protect quarterly targets instead of testing bold ideas. A space mission needs ground tests that expose weaknesses before launch; tech teams need that same permission to discover what fails. The space mindset: Reward disciplined learning, with clear hypotheses, bounded resources and evidence that informs the next decision. Make learning a performance goal. - Shelli Brunswick, SB Global LLC

Roadmaps and dates can constrain innovation. Often, management is trying to forecast the impact of things on the business, and because of this, they put expectations around dates, delivery and impact. But innovation requires a lot of experimentation, and the first version is often not correct or impactful in the way you expect. It is the constant iteration that drives real results, and that is hard to quantify and predict. - Mark Beare, Malwarebytes

Having to report to a CFO to justify market demand when the technology has not even been prototyped, tested or taken to market is a sure way to kill innovation. While innovation teams need to work in a resource-disciplined manner, finance should not have a seat at the table during the early stages of innovation, concepting and user testing. - Mark Francis, CaregiverZone

Innovation must not be limited by a long list of constraints. It also must be presented to the right audience. Wheeled luggage was patented in 1970 but did not become widely popular until two decades later. How management delivers their message when an innovative idea presented does not meet current needs is critical. For example, “That’s not what I am looking for” versus “How do you see this idea resolving the issues in front of us?” create two different impacts. - Irene Thong, Oglethorpe Power

Requiring too many layers of approval before anyone can test an idea discourages innovation. Having worked through several major technology shifts, I’ve learned that you rarely know exactly what will work until you try it. When every experiment requires multiple meetings and executive sign-off, people stop bringing forward ideas. Give teams room to test things without making them defend every idea first. - Nada Usina, NU Advisory Partners

Rewarding people for knowing rather than learning stifles innovation. When teams are expected to have the right answer before they experiment, they stick to familiar problems and safer solutions. Leaders need to make it easier to be wrong, test assumptions and learn quickly. The point of experimentation isn’t always to succeed. Sometimes it’s simply to find out what doesn’t work and why. - Dhiraj Rajaram, Mu Sigma

#3049. Why Wayfair Stock Is Up Today

Shares of Wayfair (W +10.99%) leaped 11% on Wednesday after the home goods-focused e-commerce leader delivered stronger-than-expected profits despite a tepid overall economic environment.

Wayfair's net revenue rose 9.7% year over year to $3.4 billion in the third quarter.

The gains were fueled by an 11.4% jump in U.S. net revenue to $3 billion, marking the online shopping platform's fastest pace of growth since the early stages of the pandemic.

"At the start of this year, we told you we expected to take share in the U.S. at a pace we had not seen in several years," CEO Niraj Shah said. "With U.S. revenue growing double digits against a roughly flat category here in Q3, we've done exactly that."

Wayfair's active customers grew 4.7% to 22.2 million as of Sept. 30.

Although average order value declined to $311 from $317 the prior-year quarter, customers are ordering more often and spending more overall.

Orders delivered in the third quarter jumped 12.2% to 11 million, while last 12-month net revenue per active customer increased 3.1% to $596.

All told, Wayfair's adjusted earnings per share surged 33% to $0.93. That was well above Wall Street's estimates, which had called for per-share profits of $0.79.

Looking ahead, Wayfair sees "high single-digit growth" in revenue in the fourth quarter.

"The momentum we have on orders and customers gives us confidence that our share gains are structural, and we are still in the early innings," Shah said.

#3045. Crypto Market Today, Nov. 4: Bitcoin Stages Sharp Recovery as Wall Street Rallies and Regulatory Hopes Return

Bitcoin reclaimed $80,000 as cryptocurrencies rallied alongside Wall Street following the midterm elections, with Ethereum leading gains among major digital assets.

As of early evening on Nov. 4, Bitcoin (BTC +3.32%) rose 3.64% to $80,156.15, Ethereum (ETH +5.89%) rose 6.58% to $2,421.50, and Solana (SOL +2.63%) rose 2.19% to $94.94.

Cryptocurrency markets staged a broad recovery on Wednesday as the conclusion of the U.S. midterm elections helped ease political uncertainty that had weighed on risk assets in recent weeks. Bitcoin, which had fallen as low as $75,700 amid sustained selling pressure, rebounded above $80,000, while Ethereum led gains among major cryptocurrencies, climbing more than 6% to a two-week high above $2,400.

The recovery coincided with a broader rally across U.S. equities, as investors responded positively to the election results and the prospect of greater policy stability. Declining oil prices and easing Treasury yields provided additional support for risk appetite, helping lift cryptocurrencies alongside traditional financial markets.

Among key crypto funds, iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT +2.91%) rose 2.91%, Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC +2.84%) rose 2.87%, and iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA +5.72%) rose 5.72%.

The rebound follows an extended period of weakness that resulted in more than $1 billion of outflows from spot Bitcoin ETFs. Whether Wednesday's gains mark the beginning of a sustained recovery will likely depend on improving ETF flows.

In the wake of the Democratic sweep of Congress, crypto investors should be watching for a potential shift in the regulatory landscape. Democratic lawmakers are reportedly considering revisions to the stalled CLARITY Act that could expand the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission's (SEC) authority, strengthen investor protections, and impose additional restrictions on government officials' cryptocurrency activities. Although discussions remain preliminary, renewed legislative momentum could help resolve regulatory uncertainty that has weighed on the industry for months and establish a clearer framework heading into 2027.

In the near term, investors will be looking for confirmation that Wednesday's rebound has staying power. Bitcoin holding above the psychologically important $80,000 level and reclaiming $82,000 would be encouraging signs of renewed buying momentum.

Ultimately, the combination of regulatory developments and reclamation of key levels could provide a foundation for a more sustained recovery. However, macroeconomic uncertainty, shifting interest rate expectations, and lingering weakness across the broader cryptocurrency market suggest investors should remain prepared for continued volatility.

#3041. SunCoke Energy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Debt paydown and rising cash flow give the company room to reward shareholders while maintaining operational momentum.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, SunCoke Energy (SXC -2.70%) announced that it expects to modestly exceed the high end of its full-year 2026 guidance for consolidated Adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Reliable operational performance across both operating divisions and reduced debt levels bolster long-term capital balance.

Three key takeaways from management's discussion stand out for investors.

Management reached a settlement regarding a 2025 contract dispute with steel maker Algoma Steel. This resolution eliminates working capital friction and helps boost expected 2026 free cash flow, which is cash generated after funding capital expenses, to between $180 million and $185 million.

The Domestic Coke segment, which produces high-carbon coal fuel for steel production, continues to see steady structural demand. Solid customer contracts and completed spot market sales mean the division locked in its full operational capacity for the year.

The Industrial Services handling and logistics segment experienced elevated river and terminal throughput. Higher volumes and integrated operations from the Phoenix terminal acquisition drove results above previous full-year projections.

Operations run at full capacity across core facilities, and SunCoke Energy is capturing higher terminal throughput. Long-term customer contracts provide stable revenue while management monitors debt payoff and market demand.

Capital allocation stays focused on strengthening the balance sheet and rewarding shareholders. Paying down the revolving credit line, a flexible borrowing facility, remains the primary debt target alongside ongoing dividends.

#3039. Why Opendoor Stock Jumped 13.8% Today

Opendoor jumped as a Democratic sweep pushed oil and Treasury yields lower, but this is a rate-sensitive stock with a model I'm still skeptical of.

Opendoor Technologies (OPEN +13.79%) stock finished Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, up 13.8% after a Democratic sweep in the midterms set off a rally in housing stocks.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.44%) finished up 1.7% and 1.5%, respectively.

Democrats took both chambers of Congress on Tuesday night in a sweeping victory. The party has said that affordability is a major policy priority, including the cost of housing.

Beyond that, the results led oil prices lower as investors hoped for a quicker resolution to the Iran war now that Democrats control the legislature. That could help cool inflation, which in turn may mean long-term Treasury yields retreat -- exactly what the 10-year Treasury yield did today.

Opendoor is in the business of buying and selling homes, so the more active the housing market is, the better the company's sales. If yields move lower, mortgage rates follow suit, which typically means more buying and selling.

Opendoor also sits on a large, financed inventory. Higher rates eat into its bottom line as it is forced to pay more in interest.

The company reports Q3 results after the close on Thursday, with Wall Street looking for sales growth of about 17%. We'll see if they deliver.

Regardless, I'm still skeptical of the model long term. I'd be careful reading too much into today.

#3036. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3027. History Says This ETF Is a Fantastic Buy-and-Hold Investment -- and It Could Turn $100 per Month Into $359,492

This dividend ETF has delivered surprisingly strong growth for the past 15 years.

High-yield dividend stocks are often discussed as being "safer" or "steadier" than tech stocks -- but that relative sense of safety comes at a price. Dividend stocks often don't grow as fast as the best tech stocks. That's because companies that pay strong dividends tend to be mature, well-established companies. They have strong business models and solid balance sheets, but they're unlikely to see their share prices skyrocket as some of the best artificial intelligence (AI) stocks have in recent years.

However, if you're feeling nervous about AI stocks and want to diversify some of your investments into a different part of the market, buying high-yield dividend exchange-traded funds (ETFs) could be a good strategy. The Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF (SCHD +1.86%) is one of the best dividend index funds.

Let's look at why SCHD might be a good buy in the event of an AI stock sell-off, and how much money you could earn by investing $100 per month in SCHD.

The Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF holds 102 stocks and tracks the performance of the Dow Jones U.S. Dividend 100 Index. These are large-cap companies known for the quality and sustainability of the dividends that they pay to investors. SCHD charges a low expense ratio of 0.06%.

If you're worried that the AI trade has taken over the stock market, SCHD could give you some meaningful diversification away from AI stocks. Tech stocks make up only about 12.2% of the SCHD portfolio. The top stock holdings in SCHD include energy stocks like ConocoPhillips and Chevron, healthcare stocks like UnitedHealthcare and Merck, and consumer staples giants like Procter & Gamble and Coca-Cola.

These are not AI stocks. There's no guarantee that any ETF is "safe" from a bear market, but SCHD generally holds a different mix of companies than the tech ETFs that some investors are worried about.

The Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF has delivered annualized returns of about 9.5% over the past five years. That's a decent return, but less than that of the S&P 500.

But over the longer run, SCHD has done even better. The fund was established in October 2011, and since then it has delivered a 15-year track record of 13.11% annualized returns.

Let's say you invest $100 per month in SCHD, and although returns are not guaranteed, let's assume the fund continues to deliver that same 13.11% annualized return. After five years, your investment would grow to $7,793. After 10 years, you'd have $22,222. After 20 years, you'd have $98,394. And after 30 years, you'd have $359,492.

Ben Gran has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Chevron and Merck. The Motley Fool recommends ConocoPhillips and UnitedHealth Group. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3022. CenterPoint Energy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Capital trackers let CenterPoint fund $4 billion in grid expansion while limiting new equity issuance to just $1.6 billion, a financing efficiency that protects existing shareholders.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, CenterPoint Energy (CNP -3.50%) raised its full-year guidance range for non-GAAP EPS, which represents adjusted earnings per share after removing one-time accounting items, to $1.90 to $1.92. Electric customer demand in its Houston service territory is expanding rapidly across commercial and industrial operations.

Here are three key takeaways from management.

Industrial expansion in Greater Houston is driving commercial energy demand, pushing regional leasing activity up over 30% year over year. To support this demand, the utility plans to energize at least 14 gigawatts, or billions of watts of electrical capacity, by 2030.

Grid investments expand the utility's rate base, which is the total value of property on which regulators allow a company to earn a specified return. The company expects a compound annual growth rate (CAGR), or average yearly growth rate, of over 14% for its rate base through 2030.

Management plans to fund $4 billion in new capital investments while issuing only $1.6 billion in new common stock. The company utilizes capital trackers, which are regulatory mechanisms that allow rapid rate recovery on grid projects, to minimize financial lag.

Power demand in Greater Houston is accelerating rapidly across commercial and data center customers, and management is eager to capitalize on it. Existing system capacity and brownfield rights-of-way give the utility a distinct speed-to-market advantage, while leadership tracks capital allocation efficiency and customer bill affordability.

This long-term pipeline highlights how expanding regional power demand continues to reinforce the company's financial foundation.

#3017. Champion Homes (SKY) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

Captive retail now drives 39% of sales as backlog climbs to $500 million.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Champion Homes, Inc. (SKY +11.35%) reported top-line growth driven by expanded retail operations and increased manufacturing orders despite an environment of accelerating input cost inflation. Management reported that captive retail now accounts for 39% of consolidated sales, a strategic shift intended to improve average selling prices and enhance the customer experience. The company reported a sequential increase in backlog to $500 million, providing visibility into the third quarter of fiscal 2027. The company is actively integrating the Homes Direct acquisition and has reached a definitive agreement to acquire Timberline Homes to strengthen its Southeast U.S. footprint. Capital allocation remained focused on retail expansion and shareholder returns, supported by a cash balance of $702.8 million.

Operator: Good morning, and welcome to the Champion Homes Second Quarter Fiscal 2027 Earnings Call. My name is Brittany, and I will be coordinating your call today. [Operator Instructions] As a reminder, this conference is being recorded. I will now turn the call over to Ellen Kaleniecki, Director of Investor Relations. Ellen, please go ahead.

Ellen Kaleniecki: Good morning. Thank you for joining us for today's conference call and review of Champion Homes results for the second quarter ended September 26, 2026. Here to review the results are Tim Larson, CEO; and Dave McKinstray, CFO. Yesterday after the market closed, Champion Homes issued its earnings release. As a reminder, the earnings release and statements made during today's call include forward-looking statements within the meaning of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995. These statements are subject to risks and uncertainties that could cause actual results to differ materially from the company's expectations. Such risks and uncertainties include the factors set forth in the earnings release and in the company's filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Please note that today's remarks contain non-GAAP financial measures, which we believe can be useful in evaluating performance. Definitions and reconciliations of these measures can be found in the earnings release. I will now turn the call over to Tim Larson.

Timothy Larson: Thank you, Ellen, and good morning, everyone. Our second quarter reflects the momentum of the Champion Homes team as we continue to deliver year-over-year organic growth, gain share and advance our strategic initiatives. The strong results include sequential and year-over-year double-digit increases in manufacturing orders. The team's execution of our strategic priorities is enabling continued outperformance versus the broader housing market, reflecting the strength of our diversified channels and product portfolio. We continue to expand our retail channel as reflected in last month's announcement of our definitive agreement to acquire Timberline Homes. I'll discuss the acquisition in more detail shortly. First, let's turn to our second quarter results.

Net sales increased 6.2% compared to the prior year, in line with our expectation for mid-single-digit growth. Total homes sold during the quarter were 6,922, a 2.2% increase compared to the prior year. Our retail strategy continues to gain momentum. Captive retail represented approximately 39% of consolidated sales during the quarter compared to 37% in the prior year. We continue to see the benefits of our investments in retail capabilities and the customer experience. Manufacturing orders increased 17% versus the prior year quarter, resulting in a quarter-end manufacturing backlog of approximately $500 million. This represents an 18.6% sequential increase and $78 million of additional orders.

Backlog lead times averaged approximately 11 weeks at quarter end compared to 9 weeks last quarter. We strategically ramped production by location and geography, and we'll continue to manage production responsibly by balancing customer demand and seasonality. Manufacturing capacity utilization, including idled facilities, was 62% during the quarter and up from 60% in the same period last year. Turning to our strategic initiatives within our retail channel. Our integration of Homes Direct, which closed on August 1, is progressing well. The team is focused on expanding Champion products throughout the network and leveraging best practices across our retail platform.

As we've experienced with our prior retail investments, we anticipate the acquisition will be accretive to both our retail and manufacturing operations. On October 5, we announced a definitive agreement to acquire Timberline Homes, a leading independent retailer with 9 locations across Alabama, Mississippi and Florida. This planned acquisition reflects our commitment to align our capital to our strategy to drive long-term accretive value while continuing the diversified strength of our balance sheet. Timberline's presence in the Southeast provides opportunities to leverage the strength of our existing manufacturing and retail footprint while expanding our ability to serve customers in the region. Timberline's 9 locations have annualized revenue of over $100 million.

Subject to customary closing conditions, the transaction is expected to close in the fourth quarter of fiscal 2027. From a channel perspective, we achieved strong results across our portfolio, reinforcing the resiliency of our diversified go-to-market model in a dynamic environment. Sales to our independent retail channel continued to grow, delivering solid results in the quarter. We continue to support our independent dealers through investments in lead management tools in our dealer portal, digital initiatives that engage consumers and product offerings that are a fit for today's homebuyers. Our captive retail channel delivered another quarter of growth, benefited from continued execution across the network and contribution from 2 months with Homes Direct.

Our 95 captive retail stores represent approximately 39% of consolidated sales, up from 37% in the prior year period. Community customer demand remained stable in the quarter. We continue to work closely with community operators offering a variety of price points and products that fit with their priorities. As we noted on our last call, we remain encouraged by the engagement from our large community customers. Demand in our builder-developer channel remains strong, driven by our team's focus on delivering attainable homeownership and faster construction time lines. One recently launched development that illustrates the channel's breadth of opportunity and what's possible is the Cottonwoods project in Sterling, Colorado.

This previously stalled community has been revitalized by Champion, consisting of 3-bedroom, 2.5-bath homes that are priced below comparable site-built offerings. This project reflects the growing interest and pipeline we are experiencing from builders and developers. Each project becomes a proof point and a catalyst that demonstrates how offsite-built homes improve affordable housing supply with shorter construction time lines and faster return on capital. Turning to financing. Our joint venture with Triad continues to perform well. We remain agile on offering a range of financing solutions to our channel customers and homebuyers to ensure each can offer and purchase the right home at their target monthly payment. On the regulatory front, the encouraging momentum continues.

Our team remains actively engaged with HUD as rulemaking associated with the ROAD Act continues to evolve. We were pleased to recently host HUD Secretary Scott Turner and his team at our Chandler, Arizona facility, provided an opportunity to showcase firsthand our commitment to quality, innovation and our customer experience. We are so grateful for Secretary Turner's advocacy for affordable housing, and we appreciate his unwavering leadership as rulemaking proceeds. In September, Champion was featured in HUD's Innovative Housing Showcase on the National Mall in Washington, D.C. We were proud to debut the industry's first 2-story HUD code home with a second floor built without a chassis. The home plan is called New Horizon.

It's American's urban infill solution with a small building footprint for higher density housing. This 3-bedroom, 2.5-bath, 1,700-square-foot home is built to the HUD code. The response to our homes and operations from policymakers continues to be tremendously positive. While the updated code implementation time lines remain undefined, these developments reinforce our belief that manufactured housing will continue to play an increasingly important role in addressing the nation's housing affordability challenges. Looking ahead, while the macroeconomic environment remains dynamic, we are encouraged by customer engagement trends, order activity and the opportunities we see across our business.

Our broad product portfolio meets customers' needs with a range of options and price points that are increasingly important as inflation and rate dynamics persist. Our strong balance sheet, disciplined capital allocation and differentiated channel platform continue to position Champion well for growth and shareholder value creation. With that, I'll turn the call over to Dave.

David McKinstray: Thanks, Tim, and good morning, everyone. I'll begin by reviewing our second quarter financial results, followed by our balance sheet and capital allocation activities. I'll then conclude with our outlook for the third quarter of fiscal 2027. Overall, we delivered second quarter results largely in line with the expectations we provided last quarter. Revenue growth was supported by higher home shipments, increased average selling prices and continued strength in our retail operations. Net sales increased 6.2% compared to the prior year period. In the U.S., the number of homes sold increased 2.3% compared to the second quarter of fiscal 2026 to 6,724 units. Average selling price increased 4.1% to approximately $102,700 per home in the U.S.

As Tim noted, the increase in average selling price was primarily driven by a higher proportion of homes sold through our company-owned retail sales centers, which now represents approximately 39% of consolidated sales compared to 37% in the same quarter a year ago. Captive retail continues to be a strong channel for us and allows us to leverage efficiencies and scale across our nationwide portfolio of stores. Ultimately, this strengthens our ability to deliver a better customer experience. Canadian operations remained relatively stable with homes sold increasing modestly to 198 units compared to 196 units in the prior year period. Average selling price declined slightly due to product mix. Turning now to consolidated profitability.

Adjusted gross profit was $185.6 million, representing an adjusted gross margin of 25.5%, which is in line with our expectations. We continue to experience inflationary pressures across several key material categories. You'll recall on our last call, we noted that inflation appeared to be stabilizing. However, material cost inflation accelerated during the quarter at a faster rate than we had assumed at that time. A higher proportion of sales through our captive retail channel benefited gross margin and partially offset these pressures. Pricing execution and discipline, along with continued operational efficiencies also helped mitigate the impact. I'm proud of how our team responded and executed in this environment.

Adjusted SG&A expense represents 16.3% of net sales within our expected range of 16% to 17%. We continue to actively reallocate SG&A investment dollars towards our highest priority strategic initiatives. At the same time, we remain disciplined in managing costs across the rest of the organization, which allows us to invest in long-term growth while keeping SG&A within our targeted range. Adjusted EBITDA for the second quarter was $76 million, representing adjusted EBITDA margin of 10.5%. This was consistent with our expectations as we continue to balance growth and investment, retail expansion initiatives and disciplined expense management. Now looking at the balance sheet.

We ended the quarter with cash and cash equivalents of $702.8 million compared to $638.3 million at the end of the prior fiscal year. Operating cash flow totaled $43 million during the quarter, demonstrating the strong cash generation characteristics of the business. Together with cash on hand, this allowed us to fund the Homes Direct acquisition while also returning capital to shareowners. During the quarter, we repurchased and retired $50 million of common stock, consistent with our commitment to deploying capital in a manner that maximizes long-term shareowner value. This brings our total share repurchases since program initiation in fiscal 2025 to $380 million.

In line with our previously announced capital allocation framework, our Board also refreshed the company's share repurchase authorization back to $150 million. Our balance sheet remains strong and provides significant flexibility to continue executing on our capital allocation priorities, investing organically, pursuing strategic acquisitions and returning capital to shareowners. Now looking ahead to our third quarter, demand trends remain constructive across most of our channels. Manufacturing backlog remains healthy. Customer engagement continues to be positive and recent acquisition activity is progressing according to plan, which gives us good visibility into the quarter. That said, we remain mindful of the broader macroeconomic environment. Interest rates have moved higher, consumers continue to face affordability pressures and economic uncertainty remains elevated.

We are monitoring these factors closely as they could influence demand trends over time, and we will continue to manage the business with discipline as conditions evolve. On the cost side, we are seeing rising inflationary pressure across our input costs. As noted earlier, costs did not plateau as we had anticipated and inflation accelerated through late summer and early fall, most notably steel and petroleum-based materials. We expect these pressures to persist into the third quarter with steel costs, in particular, continuing to move higher. We are taking actions to mitigate their impact through strategic pricing, sourcing initiatives and operational efficiencies.

For the third quarter, we expect revenue growth to remain strong compared to the prior year period, supported by current backlog levels. We expect revenue growth in the 8% to 10% range compared to fiscal third quarter 2026. We anticipate adjusted gross margin will remain under some pressure from input cost inflation, though we continue to expect benefits from our mitigation actions to build over time. We expect adjusted gross margin to be in the 24% to 25% range for the coming quarter. We expect adjusted SG&A as a percentage of sales to remain within our historical operating range of 16% to 17% of net sales as we continue investing in strategic growth initiatives while maintaining disciplined cost management.

In summary, Champion continues to generate strong cash flow, maintain a highly flexible balance sheet and execute on a strategy that we believe will drive sustainable long-term shareowner value. I will now turn the call over to Tim for closing remarks.

#3012. Is Your Medicare Plan Still Right for You? Why Reviewing Your Coverage Now Matters

Every fall, Medicare provides millions of beneficiaries with a crucial window to review their healthcare coverage. But year after year, many people let Medicare's open enrollment period pass without making changes, simply assuming that their current plan is the best choice.

While sticking with what you know is comfortable, sitting out open enrollment is a decision you might regret. Here's why it's crucial to review your Medicare plan choices while you can.

Medicare Advantage and Part D drug plans are not set in stone. Each year, insurance providers adjust their coverage terms, premiums, and benefits.

Your current Medicare plan may be undergoing subtle changes for the upcoming year, such as shifting prescription tiers so your medications cost more. Monthly premium costs, annual deductibles, and maximum out-of-pocket limits can also increase.

Make sure to review your plan's annual notice of change so you don't gloss over any important updates. Having that information could help you find a more suitable plan for 2027.

One of the most compelling reasons to compare plan options during open enrollment is to take advantage of potential savings. By comparing available Medicare plans in your area, you may find an option that offers comparable coverage (or even better) for a lower monthly premium or reduced deductible.

You might also save money by virtue of expanded benefits. If your current Medicare Advantage plan, for example, doesn't offer a fitness club membership but another one does at the same cost, you could save yourself a gym fee each month.

Your health may not be exactly the same as it was a year ago. Maybe you've been diagnosed with a new condition, started a new prescription, or scheduled an upcoming surgery.

It's important to review your coverage choices, because a plan that served you well up until now may not be an ideal fit for 2027. A new diagnosis, for example, could mean that you'd benefit from extra perks that your current Medicare Advantage plan doesn't offer but another one might.

Medicare's open enrollment period runs through Dec. 7, so you still have about another month to make changes to your coverage for 2027. But don't wait until the last minute.

In the coming weeks, gather your current list of medications, contact your preferred doctors to confirm their network status, and use Medicare's Plan Finder online to explore the plans available in your ZIP code. Spending just a few hours reviewing your options could protect your health and wallet all year long.

#3008. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

(EXP +2.82%) reported record second quarter revenue of $652.7 million but experienced a decline in net earnings due to elevated diesel prices and higher interest rates impacting residential construction demand. Management reported that modernization projects at the Mountain Cement facility in Wyoming and the Duke Wallboard plant in Oklahoma remain on track, with the Laramie facility expected to be substantially complete by the end of the current calendar year. The company continues to prioritize its low-cost operating position and capital allocation through share repurchases and strategic investments despite macroeconomic headwinds and cost spikes in transportation and raw materials.

We generated solid EPS of $3.63, and we repurchased $62 million worth of shares while keeping our net leverage ratio at 2.1x. I'm consistently impressed by our team's operational focus. In this difficult operating environment, they stayed relentlessly focused on servicing customers and upholding our rigorous health and safety standards. In the second quarter, we saw continued progress across the organization with regards to safety.

We achieved reductions in recordable and lost time incident rates versus the prior year. This was the result of a sustained effort by our personnel at the plants to look out for each other and to operate safely. We continue to operate well below industry averages and will not stop until we meet our goal of 0 incidents. By our next quarterly earnings call, construction of our modernized cement plant in Laramie, Wyoming, should be substantially complete. Once construction is complete, commissioning of the new plant will take about 2 quarters.

During the commissioning phase, we'll be getting the plant up and running to full operating efficiency. Commissioning is not an easy task, and it will be an all-hands-on-deck effort across Eagle Materials. We have extensive experience with plant start-ups, and we'll be drawing on the collective expertise of our plant operators across Eagle. I will continue to report on our progress over the coming quarters. I did want to mention that the modernization of the Mountain Cement is critical and timely. The old plant continues to suffer from unplanned downtime given the age of its 1960s equipment. In October, our Mountain Cement plant experienced another disruption unrelated to the issues we resolved in July.

We are addressing the current operational issues and have been shipping cement from our broader network to ensure that our customers are supported. Importantly, throughout construction and commissioning, even with some operational issues with our old plant, there will be no commercial disruption for our customers, and we look forward to continuing to serve their product needs. By the later part of fiscal 2028, many of the cost savings we've highlighted before will be accretive to the margin profile of our Mountain Cement plant. Achieving the full 25% cost savings does require selling the full capacity of the new plant, which will take several years to complete.

Our Duke Wallboard plant modernization will follow a similar cadence with completion of both construction and commissioning occurring about 1 year after our Laramie, Wyoming, cement plant is complete. Once we finish the construction at Laramie, we'll see a significant step-down in capital expenditures for fiscal year 2028, resulting in increased free cash flow. It is important to reiterate that our capital allocation priorities remain unchanged. Over several decades, we have maintained the same strategic clarity and consistency. Our balance sheet health is the crucial starting point. Our net leverage profile will keep us ready to execute on attractive growth opportunities that meet our financial and strategic criteria.

We'll continue to look at high-impact, high-return organic projects, although securing cement plant upgrade permits remains extremely difficult and the rest of our Wallboard plant footprint is in good health. On the M&A front, we remain active exploring opportunities within our core businesses, and the pipeline for deals remains robust, especially in aggregates. Fragmentation in the U.S. aggregate industry creates opportunity for us as we have been -- as we can be patient and find differentiated value in the right deals at the right time and for the right price. Any investment we pursue must strengthen our footprint and low-cost position and meet our financial return threshold.

When there is no compelling investment on the immediate horizon, we will continue to opportunistically buy our shares back. That's a good spot for me to quickly comment on the macro environment we are seeing for our products. In our Heavy Materials sector, despite challenging weather conditions last quarter in some markets, volume held up, supported by public infrastructure and data center activity. Even with the continuing resolution between federal infrastructure bills and a higher public scrutiny of data centers, our customers believe the tailwinds for cement and aggregates will remain as we enter calendar year 2027.

As a result, in cement and aggregates, we have communicated price increases to our customers for most of our markets effective January 1, 2027. Wallboard demand will likely remain challenged in the near term since the current interest rate environment is keeping a low ceiling on residential construction. When the housing market does turn, we will have already lowered our low-cost position even more with the modernization of our oldest wallboard plant at Duke. In the near term, we also faced cost headwinds across all of our business lines given increased diesel prices and freight rates, which negatively affected our businesses both sequentially and year-over-year. Our low-cost position serves us well when we see cost spikes.

We know what levers to pull to control costs, operate efficiently and serve our customers. That is the Eagle way. We make the right investments regardless of where we are in the cycle, knowing that our commodity businesses are cyclical in nature. When we come out of the current trough, Eagle will be a stronger, better positioned and more profitable company than we were in prior cycles. Craig, I'll pause here and turn it over to you for more specifics on our quarterly financial results.

This strong performance was driven by record cement sales volume, along with higher sales volume in both Wallboard and Recycled Paperboard. However, this revenue growth was offset by higher operating costs, namely transportation costs due to elevated diesel prices across all businesses, which contributed to a 14% decrease in second quarter earnings per share. This decline was partially mitigated by a 5% reduction in our fully diluted shares as a result of our share repurchase program. Turning now to segment performance highlighted on the next slide.

In our Heavy Materials sector, which includes our Cement and Concrete and Aggregates segments, revenue was up 2%, driven primarily by increased cement sales volume, which continued to be supported by strong infrastructure spending as well as large private nonresidential projects. Operating earnings were down 12%, primarily because of higher freight costs from rising diesel fuel prices and raw material costs. Moving to the Light Materials sector on the next slide. Second quarter revenue in our Light Materials sector also increased 2% to $216 million, reflecting higher Wallboard and Recycled Paperboard sales volume, which was partially offset by a decline in Wallboard sales prices.

Operating earnings in the sector were down 13% to $68 million, primarily because of lower net Wallboard sales prices, which were significantly impacted by higher freight costs. Looking now at our cash flow. We continue to generate strong cash flow and allocate capital in a disciplined manner, consistent with our long-term strategic priorities. During the first 6 months of our fiscal year, operating cash flow increased 7% to $364 million, reflecting the strength of our businesses, the resilience of our operating model and the expected tax benefits from our capital spending program.

Capital expenditures totaled $250 million during the first half of the fiscal year, driven primarily by investments in the modernization and expansion of our Mountain Cement plant in Laramie, Wyoming, and the modernization of our Duke, Oklahoma, Wallboard facility. These are high-return projects that will enhance operating efficiency, improve reliability and further strengthen our competitive position. Looking ahead, we continue to expect fiscal 2027 capital expenditures of between $490 million and $525 million to fund these strategic growth initiatives as well as ongoing sustaining capital investments across the business segments.

Capital spending is expected to peak in fiscal 2027 with construction of Mountain Cement on track to be completed later this calendar year and at the Duke Wallboard plant in mid-fiscal 2028. At the same time, we continued returning capital to shareholders, a goal enabled by our strong balance sheet. During the first half of our fiscal year, we returned a total of $162 million through our dividend and the repurchase of approximately 711,000 shares. We ended the quarter with approximately 2.2 million shares remaining under our current repurchase authorization. Let's look now at our capital structure. We remain committed to maintaining a prudent capital structure and preserving significant financial strength and flexibility.

At September 30, 2026, our net debt-to-cap ratio was 50%, and our net debt-to-EBITDA leverage ratio was 2.1x. We believe these levels are both prudent and supportive of our growth strategy. We ended the quarter with $241 million of cash on hand and nearly $1 billion of total committed liquidity. Combined with no significant near-term debt maturities, this strong liquidity position gives us the flexibility to continue investing in our businesses, return capital to shareholders and pursue growth opportunities while maintaining a disciplined approach to balance sheet management. #3005. Intrepid Potash (IPI) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Trio output climbed 19% as Moab harvest timing shifted.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Intrepid Potash, Inc. (IPI +1.72%) reported its third quarter results, highlighting a strategy focused on operational execution and capital discipline. Management reported increased Trio production and improved segment gross margins despite seasonal weakness and a strategic delay in the potash harvest season. The company stated that it is prioritizing organic growth through investments in its existing assets while returning capital to shareholders through share repurchases. Additionally, the company noted that selection for federal matching funding for a new sulfate of potash facility represents a potential expansion into specialty fertilizer markets.

Operator: Thank you for standing by. This is the conference moderator. Welcome to the Intrepid Potash Q3 2026 Earnings Call. [Operator Instructions] The conference is being recorded. [Operator Instructions] I would now like to turn the conference over to Ryan Schultz, Investor Relations Manager. Please go ahead.

Ryan Schultz: Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us to discuss Intrepid's third quarter 2026 results. With me today are Kevin Crutchfield, our Chief Executive Officer; Zach Adams, our Vice President of Sales and Marketing; Rick Kim, our Vice President of Operations; and Jason Tremblay, our Chief Financial Officer. Before I turn the call over to Kevin, I want to remind everyone that today's discussion contains forward-looking statements about future business and financial expectations. The Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995 provides a safe harbor from civil litigation for such forward-looking statements.

Actual results may differ significantly from those projected in today's forward-looking statements due to various risks and uncertainties, including the risks described in our periodic reports filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission. Except as required by law, we undertake no obligation to update our forward-looking statements. Further, this call will include the discussion of certain non-GAAP financial measures. Reconciliation of these measures to the closest GAAP financial measure is included in our earnings press release and corresponding SEC filings, which are available at intrepidpotash.com. With that, I'll turn the call over to Kevin.

Kevin Crutchfield: Thank you, Ryan, and good morning, everyone. On last quarter's call, I discussed the 3 pillars of our own value creation strategy: execution, opportunity, and capital discipline. We made good progress on all 3 of these fronts during the third quarter. With respect to our execution, we increased production in Trio during the third quarter. This operating performance supports a 10,000 ton increase in our full year 2026 Trio production guidance and positions us to target annual production of more than 300,000 tons, a goal we didn't expect to achieve until sometime in 2027. We're acutely focused on the reliability and predictability of our operations, and we're pleased with the improving track record that we're establishing.

Rick will discuss the operating drivers behind this progress shortly. With respect to opportunity as the only U.S.-based producer of muriate of potash and 1 of only 2 global producers of langbeinite, the mineral we market as Trio, we see significant potential to unlock value through organic growth investment. In potash, our opportunity lies in our ability to increase production while moving down the cost curve through operational excellence. We expect to lower production costs, and combined with our transportation cost advantage in our core markets and the diversity of our offerings, we expect to drive profitable growth in our potash business. Trio's combination of low chloride potassium, magnesium, and sulfate gives us a differentiated platform for growth.

We believe that potential growth in demand for sulfur nutrition could support this opportunity over time. Zach will discuss the market backdrop for the products in more detail shortly. We believe consistent execution, rigorous cost controls, and strategic organic growth investments can unlock the earnings potential of our existing assets, driving profitability and improved free cash flow conversion. While cyclical, the domestic market for agricultural nutrient is large, and third-party industry forecasts generally anticipate growth over the coming years. Importantly, as we've seen in recent years, the fertilizer market is highly sensitive to global geopolitical disruptions and thus, a matter of national security.

As such, Intrepid is a well-positioned domestic critical mineral producer with attractive production assets in close proximity to key U.S. growing regions. Against this backdrop, as we announced on October 26, the USDA selected our proposed sulfate of potash, or SOP, project near Carlsbad, New Mexico, for approximately $139 million and proposed matching funding through its FIELDS program. Based on current project plans, the facility would have annual production capacity of approximately 220,000 tons. If constructed, it would introduce a new product to our portfolio, complementing Trio and potash and expanding our presence in specialty fertilizer markets. This could be a pivotal project for Intrepid, allowing us to turn American minerals into a fertilizer product American farmers need.

The project is in the advanced stages of planning and evaluation but remain subject to completion of the award process, fulfillment of all applicable grant conditions, and a final investment decision, which we're currently targeting for late 2027. As we continue our evaluation, we remain focused on disciplined capital deployment and generating attractive long-term returns on invested capital. We look forward to sharing additional details as the project advances. Our operational improvements and the constructive selling price environment contributed to the solid cash flow we generated through the first 9 months of this year.

The cash flow, combined with the proceeds from the sale of our South Ranch asset in the second quarter, puts us in a financial position that gives us multiple pathways to shareholder value creation. Jason will review our capital allocation framework in more detail shortly, but I want to outline our two-pronged returns-focused strategy for deploying capital. First, we've identified several attractive organic growth opportunities within our existing asset base. We're in the final stages of evaluating capital investments designed to increase production volumes and lower unit costs across both segments. Jason will discuss the 2 principal projects we plan to pursue in 2027.

We believe these investments can generate attractive returns and support sustained growth over the next several years. Also on the organic growth front, our development partners continue to advance the Wendover Lithium project as they work to complete their definitive feasibility study. Discussions with potential off-takers are also in the advanced stages. In addition to growth investments, we're returning capital to shareholders through share repurchases. During the quarter, we repurchased shares under our $50 million share repurchase authorization. We would plan to accelerate the utilization of this program depending on how far below intrinsic value we believe our shares are trading.

While we purchased a limited number of shares in the third quarter, over the past 5 weeks, we've accelerated our repurchasing activity, bringing the total shares we bought since the end of June to approximately 88,000. Share repurchases are a core component of our returns-focused approach to capital allocation, not a residual use of cash. We'll continue to weigh every dollar of capital, whether directed to share repurchases, improvements to our existing operations, or potential new growth projects, against the returns it can generate for shareholders, with a clear priority on compounding per share value over time.

Before I turn it over to Zach, I'd like to extend a special thanks to all of our employees for the work they've led over the last couple of years. By working together, we've increased our production in both Trio and potash. We substantially reduced our cost in Trio and are working similarly on our potash cost structure. As a result of their good work, our assets are performing much better, more predictably and reliably as demonstrated by increasing levels of profitability over the last several quarters. I'll close by noting the 3 pillars I outlined earlier, execution, opportunity, and capital discipline are converging into a cohesive value creation framework for Intrepid.

We're making good progress formulating that framework, and we see an opportunity to generate significant returns on invested capital through organic growth investment, share repurchases, and operational execution. As that work matures, we look forward to sharing more detail with you over the coming quarters. Now I'll turn the call over to Zach for a closer look at demand, pricing and the market outlook for potash and Trio.

Looking ahead, we are monitoring factors that could tighten the supply/demand balance in early 2027. In particular, congestion at Baltic ports is currently constraining exports from Russia and Belarus. If these shipping constraints persist into next year, they could limit available supply and provide further support for the market. We are also monitoring reports that negotiations have started on the next annual potash contract in China, where another early settlement could support continued shipment activity and help keep global supply and demand balances tight. Global sulfur supply constraints are contributing to higher input costs for some sulfate of potash producers, putting pressure on operating rates and availability. A tighter market for low-chloride potassium fertilizers could support demand for Trio.

With sulfur constraints extending beyond sulfate of potash to other sulfur-containing fertilizers, Trio's sulfate sulfur component provides an attractive value proposition for growers heading into spring. Trio is a unique product that supplies 3 essential plant nutrients, low-chloride potassium, magnesium, and sulfate in a naturally occurring mineral. Current supply conditions could enhance the appeal of that combination. Importantly, the sulfur in Trio occurs naturally within the langbeinite mineral we mine and is immediately available to the plants in the sulfate form. We do not need to purchase sulfur feedstock to produce Trio, reducing our exposure to the feedstock constraints affecting some other fertilizer producers.

We believe this positions Trio well to serve growers seeking low chloride potassium, magnesium, and sulfur nutrition. Looking ahead to spring 2027, grower sentiment has improved although elevated input costs continue to encourage disciplined purchase decisions. Demand will be subject to regional and seasonal variation but we believe growers remain focused on maximizing yields and making effective nutrient investments. Against that backdrop, we expect continued healthy fertilizer demand through the first half of 2027. With that market context, I'll turn the call over to Rick to discuss our operating performance and the initiatives supporting our increased production outlook.

Richard Kim: Thanks, Zach. I'll focus on the operating improvements behind our third quarter production gains and the work underway to improve reliability, efficiency, and unit costs across our operations. At our potash operations, we are focused on continuing to increase production while maintaining spending discipline. On a year-to-date basis, we have held total spending approximately flat while increasing output allowing us to spread fixed costs across more tons resulting in lower unit production costs. In our HB Mine, improvements in pond management and mill recovery have contributed to increases in production volume. These changes are helping us recover additional product that otherwise would have been lost as waste.

We deliberately delayed the start of the harvest season at Moab to allow additional evaporation in our ponds. After evaluating several operating scenarios, we determined that this timing would better support long-term production by adding more tons of inventory. While this decision reduced third quarter production, we expect to recover those tons by year-end. We also expect the additional evaporation to support higher production at Moab in 2027 and a corresponding reduction in unit costs. Decisions like this reflect our focus on optimizing performance across our operations. Turning to our Trio segment. Production increased 19% year-over-year during the third quarter, driven largely by continued improvements in operating performance at the East mine.

Our team is increasing equipment availability, reducing downtime and improving productivity. Year-over-year, operating hours increased by 5%, while tons per operating hour increased by approximately 20%. We believe these improvements provide a sustainable foundation for further production growth. Higher production improved operating leverage and contributed to a 13% year-over-year reduction in cost of goods sold per ton. Together with higher realized selling prices, these improvements supported stronger segment gross margin. As Kevin noted, we are increasing our full year 2026 Trio production guidance by 10,000 tons. Our focus is on sustaining these gains while continuing to improve reliability, productivity, and unit costs. With that, I'll turn the call over to Jason to review our third quarter financial results and outlook.

Jason Tremblay: Thank you, Rick. Third quarter sales from continuing operations were roughly flat with the prior year quarter at $51 million. Gross margin from continuing operations was also in line with last year's third quarter at $10 million. Net income from continuing operations improved to $1.7 million or $0.13 per diluted share from $700,000 or $0.05 per diluted share last year. Adjusted EBITDA from continuing operations increased to $11.7 million from $10.7 million last year. As a reminder, the third quarter is typically 1 of our seasonally weakest quarters, given that it follows the spring planting season and precedes the fall fertilizer application season. In Potash, segment sales were $31.3 million compared with $32.5 million in the prior year quarter. We ended the third quarter with $152.3 million in cash and cash equivalents, $9.1 million in short-term investments and no debt.

As part of our capital allocation framework, we invested $14.5 million in short and long-term investments during the third quarter to earn additional returns on cash we've set aside to support our long-term balance sheet strength. Going forward, we'll continue to manage our liquidity carefully, balancing opportunities to earn returns on our cash with seasonal working capital needs investments in our growth projects and ongoing share repurchases. As Kevin mentioned, our balanced approach to capital allocation prioritizes a safe and reliable operations balance sheet flexibility and investments evaluated against the risk-adjusted return potential, execution readiness, and strategic fit. Within this framework, we will also return capital to shareholders.

As I discussed during last quarter's call, balance sheet preservation is the foundation of our capital allocation strategy. We plan to maintain approximately $50 million in liquidity to navigate cyclical downturns plus roughly $35 million for working capital needs and cash flow variability. Beyond these amounts, we will deploy capital through disciplined growth investments, particularly within our existing operating footprint and share repurchases. As Kevin indicated, we have increased our repurchase activity over the past several weeks. Since the Board approved the $50 million authorization in June, we repurchased 88,000 shares for a total of $3 million. Turning to growth investments.

Our planned spending in 2027 will focus on Trio production growth and potash mine development within our existing asset base. The Trio and Potash investments I'll discuss are separate from the proposed SOP project, Kevin outlined. For Trio, we plan to invest approximately $16 million in 2027 as part of a broader initiative to increase annual production by approximately 30,000 tons above the first half 2026 run rates.
