We anticipate realizing some of the production benefits in late 2026 and expect full year 2027 Trio production in the range of 320,000 to 330,000 tons. We believe there is potential to increase annual Trio production by an additional 100,000 tons with modest incremental investment beyond 2027. For Potash, we plan to invest approximately $20 million in mine development in 2027 to sustain annual production at levels approximately 20,000 to 30,000 tons above prior years, with expected rates of return of approximately 30%. To support these initiatives, we are increasing our full year 2026 capital expenditure guidance to approximately $45 million from approximately $40 million previously.

In summary, we executed well during the third quarter, making meaningful progress toward our stated goals. We increased Trio production and significantly improved the segment's margins. Our strong balance sheet and disciplined capital allocation strategy position us to invest in organic growth while returning capital to shareholders through opportunistic share repurchases. The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 12 â 0 vote:

The Committee decided to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point to 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a solid pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. Today's policy action will support a timelier return to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

#2997. A Major Neocloud Stock Rally May Be Coming. These 3 Stocks Could Be the Biggest Winners.

The impacts of tariffs and trade wars brought neocloud stocks to a low point in 2025 before potent rallies put them back in the spotlight on Wall Street. The end of this year may follow that same pattern.

The past month was a bloodbath for neocloud stocks, no matter where you look. CoreWeave (CRWV -9.90%), Nebius (NBIS -6.67%), and Iren (IREN -11.24%) are three of the most popular names in the space, and it was ugly for all of them.

Nebius was the top performer out of those three tech stocks, relatively speaking, with a 42% loss over the past month. Neocloud companies that use the colocation model didn't do much better. TeraWulf is down by roughly 30%, while Cipher Digital has plunged by almost 60%.

It might not look like it now, but a neocloud rally may start to take shape soon. Here are three stocks that stand to benefit.

Among CoreWeave, Nebius, and Iren, it was Iren that took the hardest hit in October. While each neocloud is well positioned for a rally, Iren is the poster child of how quickly fortunes in this niche of the tech sector can change. It's trading at around a third of its all-time high, but it was just last year that Iren marched from $5 per share in April 2025 to almost $77 per share in November 2025.

That's the type of march neocloud stocks can enjoy once sentiment changes, especially if macroeconomic conditions improve. For now, Iren is flirting with a $20 price tag ahead of its fiscal 2027 Q1 report, due out on Nov. 5.

Its annual recurring revenue growth highlights how it can experience a parabolic move, like it did in mid-2025. Iren has a 5.8-gigawatt pipeline and is set to bring 500 megawatts online in 2027, which would boost its total to 800 megawatts delivered. At $20 million per megawatt, those 800 megawatts can produce $16 billion in annual recurring revenue.

Late-stage discussions for 2027 capacity and discussions progressing for 2028 capacity suggest that demand remains strong, despite the stock's steep decline.

Nebius hasn't been beaten up as much as Iren -- in fact, it's still up year to date. However, the stock is still well below its all-time high despite showing material revenue growth tied to AI.

In Q2, its revenue surged by 454% year over year as more long-term deals got delivered. It's still unprofitable, but total operating expenses only rose by 251% year over year, so revenue growth was outpacing its growth in total costs. That type of setup eventually translates into profitability.

Nebius' compute is also commanding high prices per megawatt; it mentioned $40 million to $50 million per megawatt for short-term deals in its Q2 shareholder letter. Total contract values quadrupled sequentially, and its recent introduction of live spot prices for GPU compute implies that demand is still surging.

Prepayments are financing 50% to 60% of its capital expenditures, and Nebius is still able to tap into financial markets for the remainder of its needs. It secured a $3.5 billion financing facility from Citi in October, showing that access to loans has not entirely dried up for neoclouds.

CoreWeave is my least favorite of these three stocks due to its debt. It has an undesirable 0.46 current ratio, which means it has more current liabilities than current cash. It will have to refinance and postpone the retirement of some of that debt, and given the current macroeconomic conditions, that will result in the company paying higher interest rates on it.

Furthermore, the company does not own all of the land upon which it is building. Some of its business relies on leasing property from colocation providers like Applied Digital (APLD -6.74%) and Core Scientific (CORZ -10.39%).

CoreWeave may not have full ownership of the land or the power supplies its data centers require, but this business model helps it scale its AI cloud services with less initial overhead. AI processors are still incredibly expensive, which explains why CoreWeave is still sitting on a lot of debt that is coming due soon.

In the second quarter, CoreWeave's revenue more than doubled year over year to $2.6 billion. It also almost achieved a positive operating margin. It has 1.5 gigawatts of active power along with approximately 4.2 gigawatts of contracted power, most of which isn't online yet.

Notably, CoreWeave is sitting on a $104.2 billion backlog, and its backlog has more than tripled year over year. This signals how rapidly AI is growing and what type of revenue potential awaits neoclouds after they are able to execute on more of their contracts. However, all of that potential revenue will take several years to be fully realized. If CoreWeave can refinance its debt on reasonable terms, the bullish thesis for its stock gets a lot louder.

Citigroup is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Marc Guberti has positions in Iren and has the following options: long May 2027 $20 calls on Iren and long May 2027 $28 calls on Iren. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

#2995. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2991. FTC Solar (FTCI) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Revenue climbed 26% as the company eyes positive adjusted EBITDA by 2027.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

FTC Solar, Inc. (FTCI -4.83%) reported sequential revenue growth and an improvement in gross margins, driven by increased product deliveries and operational efficiencies. Management highlighted a strategic focus on 'constructability,' a design philosophy aimed at reducing site labor requirements and accommodating challenging soil and terrain conditions. The company is expanding its international presence, specifically in India, Australia, and the United Kingdom, while maintaining its domestic manufacturing capabilities to leverage local content requirements. Management indicated that the company is on a path to reach positive adjusted EBITDA by the first quarter of 2027, supported by a contracted backlog of $640 million and an accelerating revenue ramp expected in the final quarter of 2026.

Operator: Good day, and thank you for standing by. Welcome to FTC Solar's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please be advised that today's conference is being recorded. I would now like to hand the conference over to your first speaker today, Bill Michalek, VP of Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

Bill Michalek: Thank you, and welcome, everyone, to FTC Solar's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. Before today's call, you may have reviewed our earnings release and supplemental financial information, which is posted earlier today. If you haven't reviewed these documents, they are available on the Investor Relations section of our website at ftcsolar.com. I'm joined today by Anthony Carroll, the President and CEO; Cathy Behnen, the company's Chief Financial Officer; and Patrick Cook, the company's Head of Capital Markets and BD. Before we begin, I remind everyone that today's discussion contains forward-looking statements based on our assumptions and beliefs in the current environment and speaks only as of the current date.

As such, these forward-looking statements include risks and uncertainties, and actual results and events could differ materially from our current expectations. Please refer to our press release and other SEC filings for more information on the specific risk factors. We assume no obligation to update such information as required by law. As you'd expect, we'll discuss both GAAP and non-GAAP financial measures today. Please note that the earnings release issued this morning includes a full reconciliation of each non-GAAP financial measure to the nearest applicable GAAP measure. With that, I'll turn the call over to Anthony.

Anthony Carroll: Thank you, Bill. It's really great to speak with you all again. I appreciate the opportunity to tell everyone about FTC's exciting progress, which I might summarize as more revenue, more margin, more awards and consistent improvement in a true trend towards profitability. So on to the numbers. Our third quarter revenue grew 25% sequentially, and our results were again in line or better than our target ranges. Revenue was also above the midpoint of our guidance and gross margin and adjusted EBITDA were better than the top end of our ranges. We continue to make substantial progress improving the business in terms of efficiency, productivity, product and customer positioning.

Based on that progress, we expect to see accelerating revenue growth in the fourth quarter with the midpoint of our guidance range equating to 46% sequential growth and the highest absolute level in almost 5 years. And we expect 2027 to be an even stronger year with profitability in the first quarter. So let's jump into it. I recently passed the 6-month mark as CEO. While it's still early in my tenure, I'm very pleased with the progress we're making. We're strengthening the fundamentals of the business. We're positioning ourselves for continued growth, and we're accelerating the transition into profitability. Customers do love our product.

Our factories are producing and delivering on time and on budget, and we're protecting our margins. We all know how challenging this industry can be. And because of this, I'm very proud of the great people in FTC that are achieving those results. If you remember, last quarter, I outlined several key areas of focus, expanding our customer base, driving immediate bookings, ramping second half revenue, optimizing cost and breakeven level and something you know I'm very passionate about, robotics and AI. Today, I wanted to keep that structure and ensure that I can share all the great progress in these areas. Okay. So let's talk about the progress with our customers first.

We're making great moves in this area, both in the U.S. and globally. Let me start with North America. On the new customer front, I'm very pleased to share that we just recently received a 750-megawatt project award from a well-regarded developer with a high-profile backer for a project to start in 2027. This is a customer we have an installed base with, so it means continued business and trust that proves our business model. We also received another 175-megawatt project with a well-known U.S. customer.

As you may have seen this past quarter, we also announced an agreement with Linxon and Hitachi Energy, a company that I hold very close to my heart, establishing a strategic collaboration framework to jointly pursue and deliver utility-scale solar projects across North America and other markets. They are outstanding partners, and we're excited about the opportunity to combine our strengths to pursue new projects and deliver true value for our customers. As part of that collaboration, we're actively working on multiple large-scale projects in the U.S. as well as a portfolio of at least 700 megawatts along with ETCM in Puerto Rico that we will be sharing more about soon.

Our engineering support has been key in these promising projects. We also continue to make progress with the top 10 U.S. EPCs, and one of them has confirmed 5 more projects in their '27 and '28 pipeline are designed with our solution. Getting this repeat business is based on our successful execution and customer support. I also wanted to mention that, as you know, 232 tariffs have challenged some projects. And at FTC, we provide solutions, not problems. We were able to move one of these projects for a top developer to a 100% domestic supply during execution to add that advantage and keep the customer's ultimate time line on track.

This type of service flexibility in U.S. manufacturing is part of what makes FTC unique. Now moving to Europe. We announced today a new multiyear MSA for 500 megawatts of projects in the U.K. This is with AGR, a really great developer that I know very well and I've worked with for more than 15 years. I'm looking forward to partnering with that team again and growing our relationship and our business together. Along with the signing of the MSA, we've received a contract for initially 30 megawatts. And we've also continued to hire great talent in Europe to support that growth. Over to Australia, where I was last week, at the All Energy Conference.

We continue to successfully execute on the 400 megawatts of projects that we shared with you last quarter. And we've also dramatically expanded our project pipeline, adding multiple gigawatts, some of those expecting to close in the near future. Also, to further support the 100% local content requirement needed for some of these projects, we recently signed an MOU with 6 significant material suppliers in Australia to deliver that local content capabilities and put us in an even stronger position in this key market. And finally, India. As I announced last quarter, we have strategically expanded into the fast-growing renewable energy market.

And I'm very pleased to share that we have recently secured new contracts with 2 premier blue-chip customers in the region. The first is a 118-megawatt project with Tata Power Solar, the solar subsidiary of India's largest power and energy company. Initial production for the project is already underway. The second is a 121-megawatt project with Evren, the Indian renewable development firm backed by Brookfield. Initial production for the project is expected to begin in the first quarter of 2027. These are outstanding wins with high-quality customers and reflect the tremendous growth we're seeing in our pipeline across India.

FTC has had presence in India since the earliest days of the company, and we have an impressive foundation in place, including global capability and training center, R&D facility and a software development hub supported by a talented team of almost 140 professionals. So overall, more projects, more new customers and more global presence, just what we said we would do when we last connected 3 months ago. Since the last call, we have received more than $100 million of new awards, essentially all of them with Tier 1 customers, and our commercial positioning in the market has never been better. So now let's talk about revenue. In terms of our revenue growth priority, our revenue ramp continues.

We grew over 50% sequentially in the second quarter, 25% sequentially in the third quarter and at the midpoint of our target range in the fourth quarter, we will grow 46%, and it will be our highest quarterly revenue level in almost 5 years. The growth rate may be variable and project execution progress determines some of these movements, but the direction and the trend are clear. And we expect to continue to see significant revenue growth in 2027.

Another key priority I highlighted last quarter is improving our cost structure and lowering our breakeven revenue, including through greater use of software and AI to automate workflows, boost productivity and identify savings while also transforming our contracting process and our integrated supply chain strategy. We have negotiated better contracts to provide greater stability to our customers amid volatile input and logistics markets. We have improved our terms with our key suppliers as they believe in us, and they see the growth, and we will continue to win together. We are now able to bid more projects faster, and we have been able to substantially increase our revenue without seeing an increase in our OpEx.

We have also been able to further show the financial advantages of using our constructability to speed up install times, but also our terrain following solutions to save customers a great amount of cost. All of this helps us protect the margins. Let's not forget about our manufacturing capabilities in the U.S. that allow not just vertical integration and shorter lead times, but access to a pool of more profitable projects that require local content. We remain disciplined as we accelerate towards profitability. We have met our recent commitments and with our current customers and projects, I'm very confident in our continued growth and profitability in Q1.

Last quarter, I promised to talk about numbers and savings when it comes to product installation and robotics. Before that, I want to share a thought, something that has really resonated from my now hundreds of meetings since I joined FTC. Across the globe, the energy revolution is not just about electrons. It's about construction and dirt. Yes, dirt. Easy square flat sites are rare nowadays. Sites for PV plants or data centers are now often in challenging terrain with complex soils, hard weather conditions and in areas where labor is usually a challenge. Hyperscalers and developers and investors are coming to us to ask, can we even build on this site?

This is why I think FTC's value proposition really resonates. If you give superior engineering support long before the contract, faster install times, less training required, superior terrain following solutions and advanced readiness for automation, combined with great software to analyze land, construction and performance, you will win. This is why I believe in this company and this team. Going back to my promise to deliver numbers, DNV, a leading global technical adviser and assurance firm, recently validated the installation performance of our Pioneer 1P tracker, just over 5 hours per tracker or approximately 81 labor hours per megawatt. That's 30% to 50% less installation labor than reported in competing product trials. This isn't just a model or a simulation.

It's real-world performance independently validated and demonstrated in the field. How do we achieve it? It's our DNA. Our teams have always developed the product as if we were the installers and owners. Engineering always focuses on constructability, simpler connections, bolt-free rail attachments, slide and glide module placement, color-coded components, better packaging, better sequencing and delivery, optional preassembly, all of that really matters. Every feature is designed to make installations faster, simpler and more efficient, and customers do care. Developers and hyperscalers need speed without sacrificing quality or reliability. As one of the largest global developers recently said to me, if we can build solar projects in half the time, we can deploy twice the gigawatts.

Another customer also recently selected us after testing showed our solution could reduce daily labor requirements by 400 people compared with competing alternatives. In a market increasingly constrained by labor availability, that's a powerful differentiator, helping customers improve productivity, reduce costs and build more projects with the same resources. That's why FTC exists. That's the power of constructability. Better engineering translated into measurable customer value. It drives project wins, repeat business and stronger customer partnerships. And now on to robotics, a natural extension of our leadership in constructability and a key priority I highlighted last quarter. We believe robotics will be a major driver of productivity in solar, and FTC intends to help lead that transition.

Our strategy is to partner with leading companies to develop open platform-agnostic solutions to make installations faster, safer and more automated, reducing costs and enabling the industry to scale more efficiently. We reinforced that commitment at RoboDay, bringing together more than 100 industry leaders and sharing promising results from our tests and pilots. We've run hundreds of these tests, and we've signed multiple agreements with companies that automatically install modules, execute robotic pile installation, automatically install our Cinch clip and especially we've connected those companies to the top EPCs that are using us as their testing platform to validate that data to later incorporate these companies into their innovation plans. That's the secret.

It's not just about saying we believe in robotics. It's about designing the product that is best for automation, investing in the people that are driving that innovation, spending thousands of hours in our Austin lab with our solar PV field and hundreds of trackers installed that we keep testing again and again and again with all types of robots. The biggest advantage starts with product design. Unlike systems that require robotics to be retrofitted onto manually oriented installation processes, our tracker is inherently automation-friendly. Modules hang automatically when placed, reducing manual handling and alignment. Our Python and Cinch clips have also been adapted for robotic installation, eliminating the need to align bolts and turn nuts.

In other words, we're not adapting our tracker for the future of automation. We're advancing one that already supports it. The opportunity is significant for everyone. Combine smarter engineering with automation to reduce labor, improve safety and consistency and lower installed cost. Constructability is delivering value today. Robotics will allow us to extend that value tomorrow. So overall, we continue to make solid progress across the business. We do have a great product and great customers. We committed to increasing our global expansion, growing our revenue, transforming our supply chain and our contracts, protecting our margins and improving our EBITDA. And we are delivering to that commitment.

I could not be prouder to continue to lead this company and drive our path to a very successful future. And with that, I'll turn it over to Cathy.

Cathy Behnen: Thanks, Anthony, and good morning, everyone. I'll provide some additional color on our third quarter performance and our outlook. Overall, third quarter results reflected continued execution of our growth strategy with strong sequential growth, improved gross margins and better-than-expected adjusted EBITDA. Revenue came in at $32.8 million, just above the midpoint of our target range for the quarter. This revenue level represents an increase of 25.4% compared to the prior quarter and an increase of 26.1% compared to the year earlier quarter. GAAP gross profit was $1.9 million or 5.8% of revenue, compared to gross loss of $2.2 million or 8.5% of revenue in the prior quarter.

Non-GAAP gross profit was $2.8 million or 8.4% of revenue, representing an improvement over non-GAAP gross loss of $1.3 million or 5.1% of revenue in the prior quarter and a gross profit of $2 million in the year ago quarter. GAAP operating expenses were $10.4 million. On a non-GAAP basis, operating expenses were $8.1 million at about the midpoint of our target range. This compares to non-GAAP operating expenses of $8.5 million in the prior quarter and $8 million in the year ago quarter.

GAAP net income, which, as a reminder, includes a noncash accounting adjustment each quarter to adjust warrants to the fair value based on the change in our stock price was $6.7 million or a loss of $0.54 per diluted share, compared to a loss of $27.1 million or a loss of $1.69 per diluted share in the prior quarter and a net loss of $23.9 million or $1.61 per diluted share in the year ago quarter. Adjusted EBITDA loss was $5.2 million, which was better than our target range. Adjusted EBITDA excludes approximately $11.9 million net for the change in fair value of the warrant liability, certain transition costs and other noncash items.

This quarter's results compared to adjusted EBITDA loss of $9.8 million in the prior quarter and $4 million in the year ago quarter. With that, let us turn our focus to the outlook. We have indicated over the past couple of quarters that we expected to see sequential growth for the remainder of the year, and that continues to be our expectation. While full year revenue growth will be shy of our prior 40% target, it will still represent a strong 25% growth for the year with accelerating revenue growth of 46% in the fourth quarter.

Specifically, our targets for the fourth quarter call for the following: revenue between $44 million and $52 million; non-GAAP gross profit between $5 million and $8.3 million or between 11.4% and 15.9% of revenue; non-GAAP operating expenses between $8.8 million and $8.2 million; and finally, adjusted EBITDA between a loss of $3.9 million and breakeven. Looking ahead to the first quarter of 2027, we expect to see continued revenue growth and to achieve profitability on an adjusted EBITDA basis. And while we're not providing specific guidance for 2027, we currently expect to see significant revenue growth and profitability for the full year. With that, we conclude our prepared remarks, and I'll turn it over to the operator for any questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our first question comes from the line of Sameer Joshi from H.C. Wainwright.

Sameer Joshi: My first question is just about the actual results for this quarter. The service revenues are relatively low compared to the product revenues and also over the last several quarters when you compare those. Is there something unique that happened this quarter on the service side?

Anthony Carroll: Thanks, Sameer. Appreciate your question. No, it's seasonal. It depends on different services related to different projects and different warranty and non-warranty-related interventions, but we do expect to continue to grow our services and our software as part of the strategic plan.

Sameer Joshi: Understood. And then on the outlook, meaning, yes, you talk about a global progress in Australia, Europe, India. If we get at your midpoint, we will be at around $50 million or $45 million to $50 million in the December quarter. Should we expect that run rate through 2027? Because I think you also -- I also heard that 1Q 2027 is going to be sequentially higher than 4Q 2026. Should we expect this quarter-over-quarter growth or maybe compare year-over-year growth into 2027?

Anthony Carroll: Yes, absolutely. We did guide to a stronger Q1, you are correct. And we are guiding to a strong 2027 with significant revenue growth. So you should see those numbers continue to increase.

Sameer Joshi: And it is being driven by global increase or North America?

Anthony Carroll: I would say it's a combination of things, Sameer. One, our current customers in the U.S. continue to give us business. So we continue to ramp up with our top-tier customers that already trust us. Two, as you saw, we have incremental projects now. So we're also counting in the growth in Australia and hoping to announce new projects there soon, and we're diversifying into Europe. So it's a combination of U.S. strengthening with current customers and new customers, definitely some of that growth happening also in Australia and then opportunistic markets like the U.K. and other ones like Mexico.

Sameer Joshi: Got it. Just moving to operations. I'm happy that you have consistently talked about robotics and AI and are showing improvements in efficiency. Is there more room for efficiency going forward on this front? Or have you achieved all the cost improvements and efficiencies for installation? I think -- yes, go ahead.

Anthony Carroll: No, finish, Sameer, please.

Sameer Joshi: Yes. I was wondering if you are seeing this across all projects? Or is it unique to certain geographies or certain localities?

Anthony Carroll: Great question. So I'll break it down into 3 different parts. One, we are definitely using AI, automation and efficiency to improve our supply chain and to improve our costing. If we're able to maintain or increase pricing of projects, thanks to constructability, and we're able to continue implementing those efficiencies on the cost side, on the supply chain side, that's why you see protection and increase of margin. The other lever is the one around automation for bidding and just leanness across the organization, right? We see all over the world, companies utilizing AI to create automated systems inside of the company. So we're not just using AI and robotics for the trackers.

We're using AI in the finance department, in the supply chain department. We use it to track the cost of steel better. We're using it to be able to bid more projects faster, bid better because you know that if you have a better design on the engineering of a tracker project, you're actually able to maximize margin while you keep the same quality to the customer. So that's another area where we're definitely seeing that advantage. And then on the constructability, absolutely. When customers ask us to bid a project, what they're looking at is cost and also time and efficiency because if they're able to build faster, that cost of the project is going to reduce.

So using our software tools, especially PathFinder, to give them an analysis of that site very quickly for them to see the savings, not just in the CapEx, but also in the installation time is one of the reasons why we're winning projects. And we see it across the board, not just in one region. However, regions that have challenging situations with labor, or challenging terrains that I mentioned in my remarks, the terrain following solutions, we're seeing more and more implementation because of difficult land where people are trying to install solar. That's definitely a way to maximize upside and protect our margin.

Sameer Joshi: Congrats on the good progress.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our next question comes from the line of Sameer Joshi again from H.C. Wainwright.

Anthony Carroll: We're aiming and we have a plan to increase our liquidity while we increase our volume. And there's a few different ways of doing that, Sameer. One, obviously, are the typical capital raise and financing options that are available to all public companies. But mostly, we're going to focus on the better payment terms and the better contracts and the better cash flow on our projects. There is room to continue to improve that because with volume, you have better negotiation leverage. It's an automatic factor where you have more volume and more projects. Obviously, your vendors continue to trust you and continue to want your business.

With this growth, we're going to be able to not just improve payment terms with customers, but also with vendors, improve the cash flow of projects, which will automatically result in better liquidity. And also as you improve the margin of your projects, something that we showed today, that's going to improve our liquidity position. So we expect that improvement to continue along with the growth.

Sameer Joshi: It seems in addition to operational efficiencies, we are also having working capital improvements. So that's good to see. Once again, congrats on the project.

#2985. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2981. Blood banks targeted in latest European fridge cyberattacks

French blood banks were on Monday affected by the recent wave of cybersecurity attacks on European refrigeration systems, the French Blood Establishment confirmed on Wednesday.

The organization said that while it wasn't the direct target of the attack, cold storage units operated by external providers containing the EFS' blood products were compromised across three of its sites, with hackers cranking up temperatures.

"Specifically, temperature and defrost thresholds were set to excessively high levels," the EFS said in a statement translated from French to English by CNBC.

"These cold storage units—located at EFS preparation and distribution sites—are used to store blood products prior to their delivery to healthcare facilities, as well as samples intended for EFS biobanks."

They also stored frozen plasma used for transfusions the manufacturing of medicinal products.

The EFS said that its surveillance systems were triggered rapidly, and depending on the situation, affected equipment was either brought under control with no impact on the products or samples, or they were taken offline. No samples or blood products were lost during the attack, with samples quickly transferred to other facilities, the EFS added.

The EFS is now coordinating with the French National Cybersecurity Agency (ANSSI) and the Ministry of Health and has increased monitoring of its equipment

Hundreds of supermarkets across France and other European countries, including Belgium, Sweden and Finland, had their fridges remotely hacked and temperatures raised, resulting in potentially millions of euros of spoiled food.

"Several regional facilities of the French Blood ​Establishment (EFS) were affected by a cybersecurity incident ​that impacted certain cold storage room monitoring and control ‌systems,"

The situation is now under control and is being closely monitored, the French Health Ministry said in a statement, according to Reuters. They added that the safety of the blood supplies was ​preserved "thanks ​to the ⁠facilities' monitoring and alarm systems, as well as precautionary measures immediately ​implemented."

#2979. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#2974. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2973. AI data center stocks and Musk's SpaceX decline as Texas governor result raises risks of a slowdown

AI data center stocks came under pressure as Democrats — seen as proponents of moratoriums on the buildout — won big in midterm elections and in key technology hubs like Texas.

Markets were already anticipating the Democrats to win the House of Representatives and possibly take Senate control. But the Texas governor mansion going to Democrats was a big blow, analysts said. Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who wants to enact an immediate moratorium on data centers in the state, won over incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott. According to Wells Fargo, 27% of planned data center gigawatts is in the Lone Star State.

The midterm elections will slow the pace of data center buildouts across the U.S., Dan Ives, partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said in a note on Wednesday morning.

"There is no way to sugarcoat that Democrats taking control especially over the Senate will create some major hurdles for AI and tech legislation in the Beltway over the next 2 years," he said.

He added that the election in Texas was the "biggest worry" of the night for tech investors and the AI trade because that's where around 25%-30% of data center gigawatts and capacity will come from.

Shares of Elon Musk's SpaceX were down 4% after the open, and Tesla shares were down 5%. The Democrats' victory in Texas is a big setback for Musk, where he heavily backed Republican candidates, and where his companies Tesla and SpaceX are based. Musk spent a record of at least $474 million on the midterms and funneled $224 million into Texas alone, where Republican Senate nominee Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott lost to Democrats.

SpaceX is currently building out multiple gigawatts of data center capacity, including projects in Texas.

"Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now," Musk said in a post on X in the early hours on Wednesday, referring to his speculative plans to build AI infrastructure off-planet.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Iren fell by 4% in morning trading on Wednesday. Peer Nebius Group was down about 0.5% and Oracle was up 2%. Nvidia shares were slightly in the red.

Tech giants Alphabet, Meta and Microsoft mostly bucked the trend and were higher. Alphabet was up 3%, Amazon was up 7%, Meta was up 2%, and Microsoft was up 5.5%.

The Democratic win in Texas adds to existing pressure on the bull-market-leading AI trade. The Nasdaq-100 index fell into correction territory to end October as concerns mounted that the growth of data center spending by big hyperscalers and private AI juggernauts OpenAI and Anthropic was slowing.

"We believe the equity market reaction from the midterms mostly depends on the outcome of the Texas governor's race — a Dem win could potentially be a left-tail event to the AI trade, warned Ohsung Kwon, Wells Fargo's chief equity strategist, in a report this week.

Democrats won or retained the governorships in other key data center states Georgia, Pennsylvania and Ohio. Georgia represents 7% of all the planned U.S. datacenter gigawatts, while Ohio and Pennsylvania account for 5% apiece, estimates Wells Fargo.

Notable investment strategist Michael Hartnett of Bank of America warned earlier this year that Democrats winning the Senate, combined with taking the Texas governorship could lead to a "big slump" in stocks of more than 10% into year-end.

"Data centers went from being a popular economic development tool in 2024 and 2025 to something of a pariah by the time the midterm campaigns gathered steam," wrote the Washington policy team at Optimal Advisory this week. "The midterms are likely to send a message to local and state officials that there is a political upside to blocking, delaying and/or raising the cost of data centers."

#2966. Uber beats on revenue, CEO tells CNBC the company is 'investing significantly' to add drivers

Uber reported revenue for the third quarter that topped analysts' estimates, but the company missed expectations in its two key businesses. Shares of the ride-hailing service rose more than 7% on Wednesday.

Here's how the company did versus analysts' estimates compiled by LSEG:

Revenue increased 10% from $13.47 billion a year earlier. The company reported net income of $512 million, down 92% from a year earlier, when the company had a $4.9 billion benefit from a tax valuation release.

Uber's core mobility unit, which is its ride-hailing service, saw a 2% decline in revenue to $7.52 billion, missing the average estimate of $7.71 billion, according to StreetAccount. Revenue in the food delivery business increased 23% to $5.49 billion, trailing the $5.51 billion average estimate.

Total bookings of $59.82 billion were about in line with the $59.83 billion average analyst estimate, according to StreetAccount.

Uber's revenue beat was due to growth in its freight division. Sales jumped 34% to $1.76 billion, topping the StreetAccount consensus of $1.49 billion,

Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi said, in prepared remarks ahead of a scheduled call, that the company's core mobility segment performed slightly below expectations overseas.

"Elevated fuel prices and tighter driver availability weighed on supply growth in several international markets," he said, adding that Uber is making "targeted investments to strengthen driver supply."

In a CNBC interview, Khosrowshahi emphasized that Uber has "more demand" than supply, especially for its mobility services.

"We don't want to raise prices on our customers," he said, adding that the company is also doing what it can to avoid longer wait times. Uber is "investing significantly" in bringing more drivers on board in India, Brazil, and the U.S. to meet demand, he said.

Uber has also committed about $10 billion in capital to bring more driverless vehicles to its platform, the CEO said, through an array of partnerships and investments in companies including Rivian, Nuro, AVRide and others developing robotaxis and driverless trucks.

Near-term, Uber plans to have over 2,000 automated vehicles available on its platform by the end of 2027, half operating in the U.S. and half internationally. It expects to have around 700 available on its platform by the end of the year, adding Amazon's Zoox and Motional robotaxis to its platform in Las Vegas this quarter.

Uber is also growing its delivery business through acquisitions of Delivery Hero in Germany and ezCater in the U.S.

In its Uber Eats business, a recently expanded partnership with Costco in the U.S. is "tracking ahead of expectations," Khosrowshahi said. The company said Costco is now one of its top merchants in terms of gross bookings.

For the fourth quarter, Uber sees overall bookings of $64.5 billion at the mid-point of its forecast, in line with the average StreetAccount estimate of $64.26 billion.

#2962. Elon Musk’s record election bet backfired. The backlash may reach beyond one billionaire

Democrats' sweeping midterms victory marked a major rejection of Elon Musk and the artificial-intelligence industry, as a broad backlash against U.S. President Donald Trump and his tech allies put their political influence — and the debt-fueled data center buildout — under sharper scrutiny.

Musk, who spent a record of at least $474 million on the midterms, funneled $224 million, or nearly half, into Texas alone, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records, only to see his favored candidates, Republican Senate nominee Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott, trounced in a Democratic wave.

The defeats rattled markets overnight, sending SpaceX and Tesla shares lower, both of which have long flourished under Abbott's business-friendly policies. Meanwhile, broader tech stocks came under pressure as Democrats secured control of Congress.

Across the country, voters pushed back against billionaire influence and the growing power of technology companies, electing critics of the AI industry and approving restrictions on data centers fueling its expansion.

"We must get big money out of our politics, so no billionaire or trillionaire can buy our elections," Democratic Senator-elect James Talarico said in his victory speech, without naming Musk. "We must unrig this economic system that only benefits the top 1%."

J.C. Lintzenich, a visiting senior fellow at the German Marshall Fund, pointed to Musk's massive spending in Texas, telling CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday that the results were "clearly a rejection of the Elon Musk aspect that has found its way into the Trump orbit."

Texas has grown to be homebase for much of Musk's Empire. Tesla operates its largest U.S. vehicle factory outside Austin, SpaceX launches rockets from its Starbase complex in South Texas, and Musk is pursuing additional investments in AI and semiconductor manufacturing.

These operations rely heavily on state decisions regarding electricity, water, environmental permits, infrastructure, and tax incentives. Democratic Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa — who campaigned against generous corporate subsidies, and Musk himself — will wield significant influence over those decisions.

Hinojosa has called for a moratorium on new data centers, an end to industry tax breaks and requirements that developers cover their full electricity costs. In her victory speech Tuesday, she vowed to take power from the "powerful people who have gotten very comfortable running this state."

Republicans still control the Legislature, limiting Hinojosa's ability to overhaul state policy. But her administration could complicate Musk's expansion plans with a tougher approach to permitting and business incentives.

Nationwide, Musk's America PAC spent nearly $87 million across 44 federal races, according to a CNBC analysis of FEC filings. By Wednesday morning, Republicans it backed had lost 29 contests and won just three, with 12 still uncalled. More than $80 million — or 93% of its spending — went toward races Republicans lost.

The outcome leaves Musk with fewer allies in a Democratic-controlled Congress that could investigate his federal contracts, his past leadership of Trump's Department of Government Efficiency and his companies' ties to Washington.

Congressional scrutiny may slow SpaceX's lucrative and fast-growing government contracts, but Trump still controls executive agencies and holds veto power, limiting Democrats' ability to reverse policies that benefit Musk's defense-tech businesses.

The defeats aren't the first time that Musk's political spending has failed to produce his desired result.

America PAC backed eight losing congressional candidates in 2024, and last year Musk spent over $20 million trying to elect a conservative in Wisconsin. Though Musk promised last year to spend "a lot less" on politics, he ultimately became the biggest known donor in the 2026 midterms.

Longtime Tesla investor Ron Baron warned in March 2025 that Musk's political activism risked driving away customers, telling CNBC: "I don't think it's a great thing to alienate half the population."

Others cheered Musk's close relationship with Trump, and his influence over everything from regulation to Pentagon tech spending.

Musk was not the only tech figure who spent big.

OpenAI President Greg Brockman and his wife, Anna, contributed $25 million to Leading the Future, a political operation seeking to shape AI regulation, according to FEC filings. Venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz contributed another $50 million.

While the industry secured some victories – such as defeating New York lawmaker Alex Bores, an advocate for tougher AI safeguards, in a Democratic congressional primary – winning over local communities proved far more difficult.

In Ohio, Democrat Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted after attacking his support for data centers. Democrats spent nearly $28 million on advertisements criticizing data centers, according to AdImpact.

In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews defeated Republican Sen. Darline Graham despite a late advertising push by Building the Future, a new data center industry super PAC that made Graham its first candidate to back. The group reserved $1.5 million in ads supporting Graham, while Andrews campaigned for a two-year moratorium on new data centers.

Nationwide, campaigns and outside groups spent $173 million on ads about data centers during the 2026 general election, with the ads almost entirely negative, according to AdImpact. Democratic advertisers accounted for $140 million of that spending. Voters also considered 41 local data center measures Tuesday, with at least 10 communities approving bans or restrictions.

The financial stakes are enormously high.

JPMorgan Asset Management estimates the AI infrastructure buildout could cost roughly $5 trillion through 2030, including about $2 trillion financed through investment-grade debt markets.

Projects cannot generate anticipated returns until fully operational. Permitting delays can postpone completion and revenue, while higher utility and borrowing costs can raise project expenses and make expected returns harder to achieve.

#2958. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2956. SpaceX signs deal with Qualcomm to provide network technology for U.S. mobile service

SpaceX announced a deal with chipmaker Qualcomm to provide key network technology for Elon Musk's Starlink Mobile service, a challenger to Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile.

Qualcomm said on Wednesday that the terrestrial network it will help SpaceX build will "complement Starlink's satellite capabilities," with a planned rollout to begin next year. Financial terms of the multiyear deal weren't disclosed.

"Together with SpaceX, we are building world-leading cellular infrastructure in the United States, advancing 5G today, with an opportunity to lay the foundation for AI-native 6G in the years ahead," Durga Malladi, an executive vice president at Qualcomm, said in the statement.

Starlink is a critical piece of SpaceX's business, accounting for 36% of the company's revenue in the third quarter and 85% of its operating profit. But to date, the company's consumer business has been limited when it comes to mobile direct-to-device services as its satellite network can't provide all the voice, video and data coverage that competitors offer on popular smartphones.

Now SpaceX is snapping up spectrum and forging partnerships to take on the major mobile carriers. Last month, the company announced an agreement to purchase a nationwide spectrum portfolio in the 800 MHz band. The news sent shares of U.S. carriers reeling. Earlier this week, the Washington Post reported that SpaceX is in talks to acquire EchoStar's Boost Mobile.

SpaceX went public in June in the largest IPO on record and is now valued at close to $2 trillion.

With Qualcomm, SpaceX will be using the chipmaker's Dragonwing cellular infrastructure hardware and software in base stations to enable high-capacity coverage for Starlink Mobile.

Qualcomm has a long history of making chips for cellular networks, but is best known for making the modems and radios that go inside devices, not base stations for running a wireless network. The company has targeted carriers and other infrastructure companies since it made a bigger push into the 5G infrastructure market in 2020.

SpaceX represents one of Qualcomm's highest-profile customers or partners in 5G infrastructure, following earlier deals with Verizon, Dish, and Europe's Vodafone.

While SpaceX plans to use Qualcomm's technology to establish tens of thousands of new cell sites, it hasn't announced partners among cell tower companies or said how many new towers may be required.

Mobile carriers generally lease space from companies such as American Tower and Crown Castle, which commonly host multiple carriers from one site.

SpaceX did not respond to CNBC's request for additional information.

-- CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed reporting.

#2952. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2949. The Celtics are showing progress attacking the rim, but there’s room to grow

In his season-ending press conference immediately after Boston's first-round playoff exit, Brad Stevens made a comment about the state of the Celtics that reverberated through every offseason decision leading up to the start of the season.

"I think one of the things that we've gotta figure out is how to have more impact at the rim," Stevens said. "I thought we struggled to get to where we wanted to go on offense."

He had a point. In the regular season, the Celtics were dead last in volume inside of 5 feet, averaging 22 attempts per game. But on low volume came high efficiency, with the team's 69% finishing on those attempts being second in the league. On top of that lack of attempts was a league-low in free throw attempts (18.7).

So, the Celtics made changes. They brought in Mitchell Robinson, one of the game's best offensive rebounders after the Celtics averaged the lowest amount of putback points among all playoff teams (4.1). Then they swapped Paul George for Jaylen Brown, adding a shot creator that would offer a different kind of off-ball influence to open up Jayson Tatum's own scoring creation.

As of Nov. 2, Boston's 7-game starting sample has shown early returns in this area. As far as just touching the paint, they're getting inside at an increased rate, jumping from 24.4 touches to 28.3. The Celtics entered their Rockets matchup 13th in field goal attempts inside of 5 feet, making a major leap of 28.3 attempts to last year's 22.

There's work to be done in their finishing on those attempts — they're currently working at a 63% clip (17th in the league) — but the results are showing a team that's making an effort to get more looks at close range.

This back-to-back showed two sides of that progress, with slight variations in personnel and significant variations in Joe Mazzulla's lineups.

On the first night of their back-to-back, the Celtics got an Orlando team that's missing key pieces and is in the process of developing a new identity under coach Sean Sweeney, yet still retains some of that gritty defensive identity with their available personnel.

For Boston, only Mitchell Robinson was absent from the rotation, and in that absence, we saw more of the freeflowing, fast-reacting movement of last year's offense with Neemias Queta as the lone big on the floor.

It's no surprise that Robinson and Queta lead the team in paint touches, but behind them stand Boston's two most important players in Tatum (3.5) and George (3). In this matchup, they shined both in their self-creation and in their playmaking inside the paint.

As a team Boston was 72% inside the restricted area, and Tatum's gravity as a pick-and-roll ballhandler was a significant factor in creating looks for himself and for the roller.

Tatum's high free throw rate has also been a major benefit of his driving aggression. The Celtics are currently averaging 28 attempts from the line, another significant jump from last year's number. Tatum is currently at 10.2 attempts from the line.

Like Tatum, George is creating off the dribble, feeding the roll man, and comfortably playing off-ball.

It's two players who have figured out how to put the ball into space. But for that to happen, you need good cutting instincts from those around them. That wasn't an issue last year, and it's definitely not an issue now. The Celtics are currently scoring 1.35 points per cut possession, nearly identical to last year's 1.36 PPP.

This game was a good indicator of this team's ceiling attacking the rim. You're missing Robinson, who has struggled to adapt to his new role closer to the action on the perimeter, but you're seeing Boston's other two bigs look comfortable in their roles, while the team's two stars acted as high-running motors to an efficient offense. When you can space the floor with shooters around Tatum and George and add in a rolling threat like Queta, the space to attack and the decisions you can make feel more abundant.

On the second night of their back-to-back, the Celtics were without their other primary offseason addition with Paul George getting a night off.

On a sluggish shooting night, the primary theme for this story's subject was as follows: No Tatum=No Paint Pressure.

Without George's primary option lineup, when Tatum stepped off the floor, getting inside the arc became a chore for Boston. In a shade under 16 minutes with Tatum resting, they were just 1-for-8 on shots inside the paint, with a single shot generated at the rim. With Tatum, they had 20 shots at the rim and were 70% on those shots, much more in line with what we've seen so far this season.

Along with his ability to create in isolation, Tatum's gravity as a pick-and-roll threat opened up rim-runs for Mitchell Robinson and Queta, while his vision finding cutters particularly flashed early on.

The Celtics trailed by five when Tatum checked out with 1:39 left in the third quarter. When he returned with 7:02 left to go in the fourth quarter, it was a deficit of 16. With JT off the floor in general, the Celtics were outscored 35-18.

When he returned, it didn't take long for the offense to get back into its previous flow. Tatum quickly assisted on a drive-and-kick three to Derrick White in the corner, hit Queta on a roll, and scored himself on a drive-and-finish within his first six possessions after checking back in. But by then, the Rockets had comfortably separated enough to bury them.

With a lack of paint pressure coming from the perimeter, Mazzulla experimented heavily with double-big rotations throughout the game, which in spurts gave Boston some more putback and lob options but wasn't nearly enough to overcome poor shooting from the outside and a tough night on drives. The Queta-Robinson combo was featured most prominently, finishing the night with 4:42 on the floor together. With those two sharing the floor, the Celtics were 3-for-3 at the rim, two of which came from Queta and the other from Robinson.

Even without an interior enforcer like Steven Adams, the Rockets are an exceptionally difficult team to get a step on. They have a collection of lengthy wing defenders like Amen Thompson, Tari Eason and Kevin Durant and feisty point-of-attack guards in Marcus Smart, Fred VanVleet and Reed Sheppard. Add to that disciplined help on the nail, and even if your last line of defense is Alperen Sengun, the defense is designed to make it drudgerous just to get to that weak link. And it just so happens that their weak link still finished with a pair of blocks and steals on Monday night.

If the Orlando game was a sign of what they could be, Houston was a sign of how far they have to go to get there. Missing George's ballhandling was a predictable blow, but a lack of influence in the paint from those not named Jayson Tatum against a quality defense does show that they have a lot to work on.

The road ahead is long to figure out what kind of impact this team can have from close range. They can't all be against a Chicago, and they won't all be against a Houston or Detroit, but it's important that every step of the way, we keep tabs on the progress of this team's still-developing interior identity.

#2948. A massive trade just happened in a leading mortgage lender

For more options-trading reaction to the rally, click the link at the top of the page for Options Action LIVE featuring CNBC contributors Michael Khouw and Brent Kochuba alongside host Oliver Renick.

Stocks are surging after midterms brought a Democratic blue wave and options traders are responding by selling volatility and embracing a rotation away from big-tech trades.The S&P 500 added just shy of 2% by midday Wednesday, recovering losses from Friday and Monday. The move in its corresponding options prices was even bigger, with the Cboe Volatility index falling about four points to below 17, the biggest one-day drop in the volatility gauge since at least July.Crude oil prices fell as WTI traded below $90 and energy stocks dropped 5.6% for the biggest decline in the S&P 500. Options on the State Street Energy Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLE) jumped to more than three times the 30-day average, with 57% puts by volume and the big-money trades concentrated in bearish put-buyers. The top trade in XLE was the 62.5-strike put expiring Jan. 15, suggesting it could be a slow rollover for what has been the best-performing sector of 2026, SpotGamma data show.With the drop in crude oil, a handful of inflation and interest-rate related trades got some bullish attention. Consumer discretionary stocks rallied almost 5% but options volume in the SPDR sector fund XLY was below average and volume was split between puts and calls, according to Cboe LiveVol data.Instead, there was some big action in a few housing trades such as Rocket Companies, the mortgage lender and Redfin owner that fell almost 50% this year before turning around last month. Options volume on Rocket was double the average with a strong bullish bias. Of the 110,000 options traded, 90% were calls and more were likely initiated by buyers than sellers. Bulls traded the bulls like the 14 and 16-strike calls expiring Nov. 20 the most.Opposite the drop in crude oil was a rally in solar energy companies, extending recent gains and extending unusual options activity that preceded the midterms.The Invesco Solar ETF (TAN) added another 9%, trading the highest since July. Options volume was more than five times the average and 80% of the volume was in calls. The most popular trade in TAN was the 48-strike call betting on a 6% rally through Nov. 20th.On the tech side, options flows looked bearish in the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which fell 2.6% to the lowest since May. Options volume was twice the average, 70% of it puts, with the 475-strike put expiring Nov. 13 the most popular contract bought by volume.
