#2942. NBA power rankings: Rockets and Sixers shoot to top, Knicks drop

The NBA paused its action to encourage Americans to vote.

So with no games scheduled for Tuesday, Nov. 3, it offered a bit of a reset to take in the lessons learned from the first two weeks of the 2026-27 regular season.

The good news for teams is that the spate of injuries that ravaged rosters across the league have (mostly) calmed. And while it's still so very early in the season, the early action has led to sweeping changes across the board. Already, there's a new No. 1 team, and a new No. 2 for that matter.

Here are the Week 2 USA TODAY Sports NBA power rankings:

They're second-to-last on net rating (-13.2) and are prime candidates to sell off their assets before the trading deadline.

No team plays faster than the Bulls, who lead in pace (105.86), but the offense has been wildly inefficient, with Chicago shooting just 41.6%, second-worst in the NBA.

The addition of Julius Randle hasn't been enough to stabilize the offense, and 3-point defense (15.0 conversions allowed per game) has been another Achilles heel.

The Jazz, who were looking to take a step forward, have been one of the more disappointing teams. Utah doesn't force enough turnovers and its transition offense is basically nonexistent.

Tyler Herro has been pretty solid, but too many Bucks players have been far too inconsistent, starting with Kel'el Ware.

The Clippers still are the only team to average at least 20 turnovers per game.

They're the only NBA team to have only played five games, but Phoenix has been getting killed on the glass, where they rank 29th in rebounding (39.6 per game).

Indiana is banged up, but without Tyrese Haliburton, this offense lacks a catalyst and identity.

Winners of three consecutive, the Pelicans have been a nice story so far and are dominating down low; New Orleans leads the league with 62.6 points in the paint per game … which is 7.5 points more than the next team.

Since Paolo Banchero (ankle) and Jalen Suggs (knee) have been out, Orlando has been unable to find consistent offense and turnovers (17.3 per game; tied for 25th) are a massive issue.

No team is allowing more 3-pointers per game (17.3). The defense has been a nightmare, with players even saying they're not trusting each other on the court.

They've lost four consecutive and the warning sign is an offense that has is drastically underperforming; Charlotte ranks dead last in offensive rating (100.4), which is nearly four full points below the next closest team.

Are the Wizards finally a player in the East? The early returns on this group look good, but the schedule hasn't exactly been a slog.

They look older and slower, but the more curious issue is the sweeping dip across Nikola Jokić's stats: 22.0 points, 9.7 rebounds and 9.0 assists per game.

They've won three of four, but became the first team to lose to the previously winless Kings. The Cooper Flagg-Kyrie Irving partnership, however, has been a fun watch.

They've had five different leading scorers through eight games and lead the NBA in assists per game (30.6) and assist-to-turnover ratio (2.08). The results have nonetheless been uneven.

Are we in championship hangover territory? The Knicks are a bit of a rarity, ranking dead last in pace (96.5). They've struggled against marquee opponents.

No team has been hauling in more offensive boards per game (15.3), but losses in three of their last five have stunted their progress up the standings. And they got good news: Kawhi Leonard (knee swelling) might be back soon.

They share the ball well and protect it, but the Cavs have had an uneven start to the season. A loss against an extremely short-handed Thunder team was a missed opportunity.

They've won five consecutive, some against top-level teams (Lakers, Timberwolves, Nuggets). And Stephen Curry, at 38 years old, is playing his way into the MVP conversation.

Minnesota's two victories this week were against the Clippers and the Bucks, which is fine. But the Timberwolves will need to show that they can beat some of the league's better teams without Anthony Edwards. The saving grace will be the defense.

The overtime loss against the Pelicans was a setback, but Memphis remains the biggest surprise in the NBA, and Ty Jerome (28.2 points per game; seventh in the NBA) has been a revelation.

Losing Ja Morant (hamstring) is certainly a blow, but Portland has been doing a great job on the glass, limiting teams to an 10.0 second chance points per game.

The move to add Mitchell Robinson is paying off so far, as pairing him alongside Neemias Queta has Boston ranking third in the league in opponent points in the paint per game (39.4).

Recent competition hasn't been as robust, but the Spurs have won five consecutive and are taking care of the ball, ranking second in turnovers per game (13.4).

Luka Dončić is flaming opponents, averaging an absurd 38.3 points per game and orchestrating the NBA's best offense (121.9 points per 100 possessions).

They've dropped two of their last three, as the injuries keep piling up. The latest, to Chet Holmgren, means OKC will be without three starters for the foreseeable future.

It's rather unfair to drop a team one spot even though it won all three games this week. This is more about how good the 76ers have been and the quality of opponents Detroit faced. But can the Pistons sustain this level with Duncan Robinson (a career-high 17.9 points per game) as their second-leading scorer?

Interestingly, it's the defense that has been Philly's strength. The Sixers have the league's third-best defensive rating (105.1 points allowed per 100 possessions).

They own the NBA's top net rating (+12.8), Alperen Şengün is anchoring the offense and Jabari Smith Jr. is breaking out.

#2939. Americans have said 'NO TO WAR,' Iran speaker says as Democrats take Congress

Iran's speaker of parliament Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf said Americans have said "NO TO WAR," as projections from U.S. midterm elections showed that Democrats have flipped both houses of Congress.

Posting on X after projections showed that Democrats will have a majority in the House and Senate over the coming two years, Ghalibaf said that "Conscience or the cost of living, Americans said NO TO WAR, and we endorse this message."

"If the [U.S. government] betrays their public will to serve international paymasters and blackmailers, it will learn our NO comes with a Nightmare."

The X account of Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei posted: "The process of the US's collapse has accelerated."

"If there are still those who entertain the slightest hope that the hegemonic system may change behavior or shifts in its political parties may bring about change, or that by making concessions to the US, they can persuade it to abandon its hostility, they're gravely mistaken," the account added.

Last Saturday, Khamenei had called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

The Iran war has been a central issue for voters in the midterms, after U.S. President Donald Trump launched joint strikes with Israel against the Islamic Republic in February. The conflict has pushed up energy prices across the U.S., which has led to cost of living concerns.

Trump is also blaming the Iran war in part for the Democratic sweep of both chambers of Congress, telling the Wall Street Journal that "Iran was a piece of it" when asked if the war contributed to the Republicans losses.

However, he maintains that prosecuting the war was the right decision, saying, "I don't think about it. I did the right thing. I would have done it again."

Washington has not explicitly stated it plans to resume attacks on Iran, but reports from The Wall Street Journal and The New York Times indicate the U.S. is preparing for possible renewed military actions after the midterm elections.

With their new majorities, Democrats might be able to push through a War Powers Resolution, which will direct Trump to end the conflict with Iran should Congress not authorize it.

In June, both chambers — which were then Republican controlled — passed a concurrent resolution to end the conflict, although a concurrent resolution is not binding and does not have the force of law. Previous attempts to impose a binding War Powers Resolution had been defeated.

#2936. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2934. Patrick Dumont purchases Miriam Adelson’s share of Mavericks, now owns 69% of team

In 2023, Mark Cuban sold what was then his majority stake in the Dallas Mavericks to Miriam Adelson and her son-in-law, Patrick Dumont. At the time, the sale was purported to be to the tune of $3.5 billion. The transaction gave Adelson and Dumont majority ownership of the franchise, with Dumont serving in the role of Governor while Cuban maintained a minority stake in the team.

It is now being reported that another sale took place last month, though this one does not involve any new parties entering the fold. Adelson has instead sold her stake in the Mavericks to Dumont and his wife (and Adelson's daughter) Sivan Ochshorn. As a result of the deal, Dumont now holds a 69% ownership stake.

Along with Dumont are minority owners Cuban (27%) and Mary Stanton (4%). As part of the terms of the original sale in 2023, Dumont retains the right to purchase as much as 20% of Cuban's remaining portion as well. "Our plan is to look at that and to do that over time," Dumont suggests. Should he chose to do so, he would then hold a whopping 89% of the franchise which Forbes values at $5.8 billion.

Since taking over in 2023, Dumont has overhauled much of the front office. He hired Rick Welts as CEO, Ethan Casson as President (and successor CEO come the turn of the calendar to 2027), Masai Ujiri, Mike Schmitz, new head coach Dusty May and a number of other senior-manager level employees. Dumont is also hard at work on a new basketball-only area for the Mavs, with plans to make the former Valley View Mall an all-out entertainment destination.

"The most important thing every day is just trying to work harder to get better." Dumont clearly has big plans on where he wants to take the Mavericks behind young phenom Cooper Flagg and this purchase demonstrates his long-term commitment to the team

I invite you to follow me @_80MPH on X, and check back often at Mavs Moneyball for all the latest on the Dallas Mavericks.

#2930. 5 midterm election takeaways: Tech billionaires suffer as Democrats drub Republicans

Democrats decisively took control of Congress Tuesday night, delivering a stinging rebuke to President Donald Trump and his economic record as voters voiced deep frustration with rising prices, government corruption and the AI data center build-out.

The party scored an extraordinary pair of victories in traditionally Republican Texas. James Talarico defeated Ken Paxton, becoming the first Democrat elected to the U.S. Senate from the state in nearly four decades. Gina Hinojosa defeated three-term Gov. Greg Abbott, making her the first Democratic governor of Texas since Ann Richards left office in 1995. Texas became the clearest measure of how far the blue wave reached: Democrats swept the state's marquee races in a Republican stronghold long considered beyond their grasp.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The victories were part of a broader wave of Democratic gains. In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews upset Darline Graham in a race Republicans had expected to win. In Ohio, Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted. Democrats also flipped Senate seats in Iowa and North Carolina and won key races in Georgia and New Hampshire.

The results marked a striking collapse for Trump's candidates. Paxton, Graham and Husted were among the president's prominent picks who lost after he made dozens of endorsements.

The election also sharpened the political fight over data centers and artificial intelligence infrastructure. Texas is a major center of planned data-center construction, and Hinojosa campaigned for a pause on new projects until the state adopts tougher rules. Her victory gives Democrats more influence over Texas' approach to the build-out, though the state Legislature remains in Republican hands.

The results also delivered a blow to the tech billionaires and corporate interest groups that poured hundreds of millions of dollars into backing Republican candidates who might be friendly to the massive AI data center projects they hope to build around the country. Elon Musk, the world's richest man and the cycle's largest individual political donor, spent record sums in a failed effort to help Republicans retain power, including in Texas.

Musk's spending may have become a liability as voters raised concerns about government corruption and the growing influence of billionaires in exit polls. Talarico repeatedly attacked Musk on the campaign trail, accusing him of trying to buy "his very own personal United States Senator."

"Tonight's results are a wake-up call for corporate special interests and billionaires who have been rigging the economy," said Rep. Chris Pappas, who defeated former Republican Sen. John Sununu to win an open Senate seat in New Hampshire.

The outcome is poised to reshape the technology sector's relationship with Washington. A more adversarial Congress is likely to intensify scrutiny of tech companies and the AI data center build-out. Markets reacted as the scale of Democratic gains became clear: Dow futures fell and S&P 500 futures slipped Tuesday evening.

Economic frustration was also central to Democratic gains. Many voters blamed Trump's tariffs and the war with Iran for worsening their economic situation, while the cost of living remained a persistent strain.

Democratic control of both chambers gives the party a powerful check on Trump in the final two years of his term, including the ability to block parts of his agenda and intensify congressional oversight. The results sent a clear warning: promises to bring down costs did not overcome voters' frustration with prices, corruption and the country's direction.

Silicon Valley spent record sums trying to shape the election. Instead, it got a Democratic House promising to haul its CEOs before congressional investigators.

Many of the winners ran against not just Trump, but the billionaire donors who helped bankroll Republicans. In North Carolina, Cooper told supporters that North Carolinians "will always matter more than the billionaires and the insiders."

In Texas, Talarico defeated Paxton despite more than $200 million from SpaceX CEO Musk to stop him. On the same ballot, Hinojosa defeated Abbott after blaming him for the rapid buildout of data centers across Texas, saying the governor for putting Big Tech interests ahead of local communities.

The GOP had a commanding advantage among the election's biggest donors. According to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records, 14 of the 20 largest donor groups backed Republicans exclusively or nearly so. Crypto, AI, and online gambling interests poured more than $377 million into races.

Not all of Silicon Valley's money ended up on the losing side. LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, one of Democrats' biggest donors, cast the results as "an overwhelming wave against Trump and his cronies" and took a direct shot at Musk.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote on X late Tuesday. "And while people like Elon are spending outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power, those of us who believe in the ideals of America cannot yield."

With control of the House comes the power to launch investigations, issue subpoenas and examine the Trump administration and its allies. That's going to be a challenge for tech companies to navigate as they find themselves in Democratic crosshairs.

Democrats are already calling for AI executives to testify under oath.

Among the first to seize on the results was Rep. Greg Casar, the Texas Democrat who chairs the Congressional Progressive Caucus.

"The House Democratic majority must make it a top priority to protect Americans' jobs, freedom, and lives from AI billionaires," Casar said in a statement after Democrats were projected to win the House. "AI company CEOs should be called to testify under oath."

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the presumptive next chair of the oversight committee, put it more simply: "Lawyer up," he said in a statement.

The results also present headwinds for the infrastructure undergirding AI.

The Texas results appear to be a warning sign for the AI industry in a state projected to overtake Virginia as the world's biggest data center market by 2030, according to JLL. Abbott had long courted data center development before shifting his position this year as the backlash grew. Talarico and Hinojosa both seized on that anger, winning over Republican voters fed up with data centers going up in their communities. Hinojosa has promised to force lawmakers to regulate the industry.

In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision, with Democrats ranking the issue higher than Republicans.

Trump personally pushed states to redistrict their congressional seats mid-decade to cement a Republican majority in the House. The effort largely flopped.

Many of the GOP's redrawn districts ended up falling to Democrats, despite the unprecedented effort to defend or flip them by redrawing boundaries mid-decade.

That includes seats in Texas, Florida and North Carolina, which Democrats won despite Republicans making them safer. In Texas, where Republicans began their redistricting war that then spread to other states, Democrats ended up winning most of the gerrymandered seats.

The redistricting push was always risky because no recent census was available when the maps were drawn.

Trump rolled out the red carpet for Paxton, only for Republicans to lose a Senate seat in Texas for the first time in over three decades.

Talarico's victory in Texas came after Trump endorsed Paxton over the incumbent Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, who held the seat for decades. In endorsing Paxton, Trump said Cornyn had not been sufficiently loyal.

Paxton prevailed in a bitter primary, and Trump's move to tip the scales spurned Senate Republicans, who saw Cornyn as a better and less expensive candidate than Paxton — who had a laundry list of scandals that plagued his campaign.

Sen. Darline Graham, R-S.C., who Trump handpicked to replace her late brother Lindsey Graham, fell to Democrat Annie Andrews. And Republican Michael Whatley lost to former Gov. Roy Cooper in North Carolina.

Democrats linked voter concerns over the cost of living to corruption in government in the lead-up to their colossal victory. The mix of the two helped propel corruption to be a top voter issue rather than a downballot concern.

Public polling indicated before Tuesday night that the issue of corruption was landing with voters. CNBC's All-America Survey conducted in mid-October found corruption was voters' top concern. Exit polls on Tuesday also tied the two issues: 69% said benefits flow to the wealthy in CNBC's Voter Poll.

The issue of corruption has exploded into public view during the second Trump term, as reports of Trump family business deals and the president's own ballooning wealth while in office dominate headlines.

In victory speeches across the country, Democrats made clear they are committed to battling a "rigged" system — a line reminiscent of once-radical Bernie Sanders presidential campaigns.

"We must get big money out of our politics, so no billionaire or trillionaire can buy our elections. We must unrig this economic system that only benefits the top one percent,"  Talarico said. "The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom."

#2926. 15 players to watch amid NFL trade deadline buzz

Take a look at the NFL standings. There's no sure thing this season.

Once the Los Angeles Rams got off to a rocky start, it opened things up around the league. There is no runaway team this season, and there is also a group of teams already looking ahead to 2027. That could lead to a ton of activity before the NFL's trade deadline, which is Nov. 10. There has been plenty of action well ahead of the deadline, with the most recent deal involving the Arizona Cardinals sending recently demoted quarterback Jacoby Brissett to the QB-starved Baltimore Ravens.

Before that players like guard Quenton Nelson went to Seattle, defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons landed in Chicago, defensive tackle DeForest Buckner headed to San Francisco, cornerback Joey Porter Jr. was shipped to Dallas and receiver Marvin Harrison Jr. being was dealt to Houston.

That's already a lot of big trades and we still have plenty of time before Tuesday's deadline. Here are the 15 names to watch between now and the deadline:

If we take the Browns at their reported word, Sanders is not likely to be traded at the deadline. You can never say never until the deadline passes, but for now it appears Cleveland will continue to carry four quarterbacks, which includes Sanders. He has been QB2 behind Deshaun Watson and has replaced Watson for a handful of snaps after Watson got hurt. Watson was sacked eight times Sunday, and the 5-3 Browns might fear a more serious Watson injury as they sit just outside the current playoff picture after Week 8's defeat to the Steelers.

Given his cost-controlled salary, low draft pedigree and Cleveland's crowded QB room, Sanders might normally be considered a higher-likelihood trade candidate. So far there has been little apparent traction. We'll see if that changes over the next week. Perhaps there's even a world where Sanders is in the mix to start in Cleveland if the free-agent-to-be Watson is not brought back in 2027. – Eric Edholm

The Eagles reportedly won't trade Barkley. That also doesn't make much sense. Philadelphia is going nowhere, Barkley is turning 30 years old in February, his production has declined since 2024 and the Eagles have never been overly sentimental about player moves. Barkley brought great memories and was the superstar on a Super Bowl champion, but the Eagles should listen to calls. They could pass if teams try to get Barkley cheap, given his current injuries, but it seems like he'd have more value to a contending team this season than the Eagles going forward.

Maybe the Eagles are serious and Barkley is indeed off the table. That won't stop speculation. It makes a ton of sense for Philadelphia to move Barkley now. That's why we're still keeping an eye on Barkley as the deadline approaches. If they don't move him, that decision will be discussed not only at the deadline but beyond. – Frank Schwab

It would be a surprise if Taylor isn't traded, after the Colts sent off guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Buckner. The question is who lands Taylor and his 8,208 career rushing yards. He's still one of the NFL's best runners and plenty of teams have a need. The Giants, Broncos, Packers and Vikings top the list. While other backs are likely available, nobody else would pop like Taylor. Other players might be more prudent from a football aspect (Jeffery Simmons to the Bears, most notably) but no player acquisition would generate more buzz than nabbing Taylor. – Frank Schwab

Pollard has said he doesn't want to be traded, and the Titans reportedly have rebuffed offers, but a 1-7 record and a reasonable proposal could change their minds. He's in the final year of a three-year deal, makes fairly modest starting RB money and would provide the Titans with some salary cap relief if they moved him.

Pollard has averaged a robust 4.5 yards per carry, has back-to-back 100-yard rushing outings (and five TDs in two games) and remains in his relative prime at age 29 with fewer than 2,000 career touches. On paper, he checks every box for a sale candidate if the Titans want to add more draft assets before the deadline. And for teams looking for RB help, he might come far cheaper than some of the other players mentioned above. – Eric Edholm

The memories of Thomas' rookie season might last forever. They could lead to some team thinking it can revive his career. Thomas was a first-round draft pick in 2024 and had 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns. He looked like a future star. In the season and a half since he has fewer than 1,000 yards. He has fallen well out of the Jaguars' hierarchy at receiver, leading to plenty of trade speculation. The Jaguars, who look like Super Bowl contenders, could keep Thomas just in case there are injuries at receiver. But teams like the Seahawks, Chiefs or Commanders would likely be interested, hoping Thomas rediscovers his rookie form in a new situation. – Frank Schwab

Interestingly, Jennings was rumored to be a target of the 49ers, trying to bring him back into the fold, but declined the offer, saying he didn't want to go back to San Francisco. That's especially interesting considering his best production came with the 49ers, notching a 975-yard season in 2024 and a nine-TD campaign a year ago before leaving on a one-year deal in Minnesota. Jennings has been almost a ghost with the Vikings, with only six catches in his six games and only modest upticks in his activity lately.

The Vikings need all the offense they can find, but Jennings hasn't been a great fit. If they can include him in a deal for a running back or defensive depth, the Vikings might consider it. – Eric Edholm

Downs has requested a trade, saying he's "open to whatever" when it comes to moving on from the Colts. The team has already kick-started its offloading efforts with Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, and don't appear to have strong intentions to keep Downs. The 25-year-old receiver is in the final year of his rookie deal and has stepped up as the Colts' leading option with Alec Pierce hurt. Downs doesn't fit a WR1 profile but could be a quality complement for a team seeking an upgrade to its unit, similar to the Seahawks' deadline trade for Rashid Shaheed last year.

Downs is a smaller slot receiver and might be a fit for only certain teams, but with his trade request and the seeming league-wide need for WR help, he figures to be a candidate who possibly moves on soon. – Eric Edholm

Jeudy has always been mercurial. He came into the NFL as a first-round draft pick and was fine early on with the Broncos, but wore out his welcome and was traded to the Browns. With the Browns, he had a stellar 2024, with 1,229 yards and a Pro Bowl nod. But he has dipped again and with the Browns turning toward rookies Denzel Boston and KC Concepcion, Jeudy has just 171 yards this season. Still, Jeudy has one of the biggest plays of the Browns' 5-3 start, a 33-yard catch against the Ravens to set up a game-winning field goal.

Jeudy, 27, could be an intriguing gamble for a team that needs juice at receiver. He's no sure thing, but the talent is there. – Frank Schwab

Goedert has been consistent. Over the past seven seasons he has posted between 496 and 830 yards each season. He's not a superstar at the position but he is reliable, even at age 31. Goedert is on a one-year deal and it's not like the Eagles are going to be competitive this season. That makes him a perfect trade target, and a few teams could use a solid pass-catching tight end for the stretch run. – Frank Schwab

Mack isn't the same player he was when he was a first-team All-Pro three times in four years. His peak might lead him to the Pro Football Hall of Fame. He's 35 years old now, but still is an effective player. And with the 0-7 Chargers going nowhere, it would make sense for the team to move on and allow Mack a chance at playoff success in what might be the final season of his career. Mack is in his 13th season and has never been part of a playoff win.

Mack would add leadership and a strong edge defender down the stretch for a contender. Mack has 8.5 sacks in 19 games over the past two seasons, including three in seven games this season. Given his age, he might not cost that much. A reunion with the Bears seems like the perfect fit. – Frank Schwab

When the Eagles drafted Smith in 2023, draft experts lost their minds. They gave the Eagles a consensus grade point average of 4.12 — better than an A. While the Eagles' first pick, defensive tackle Jalen Carter, worked out very well, Smith has not.

Smith has just 14.5 career sacks but still has the draft pedigree and enticing athleticism. The Eagles picked up Smith's fifth-year option for 2027, which means they won't be looking to give him away. But for a floundering Eagles team, the idea of recouping some value on Smith during his fourth lukewarm season has to be a possibility.

There are a lot of Eagles on this list. Philadelphia becomes the seller to watch as the trade deadline approaches. For some contender looking for a shot on a sleeper, taking a stab at Smith at age 25 could pay off nicely. – Frank Schwab

Sweat reportedly requested a trade in August, and the Cardinals very publicly said they had no interest in honoring that request. Could they have a different mindset now? Arizona has won two of three and is a fairly respectable 3-5, albeit in a very tough division the Cardinals are unlikely to win. Sweat's play has not been as impactful as a year ago, when he notched 12 sacks and four forced fumbles, but he had a huge sack in overtime of Dak Prescott in Dallas on Sunday.

Speaking of Dallas, the Cowboys figure to be among the teams that could have interest in Sweat, along with the Packers, where Jonathan Gannon (his former coach) is defensive coordinator. Sweat's contract kicks up to $23.6 million salary cap hits each of the next two seasons, which puts him outside the top-10 edge rusher deals, but there have been rumors of Sweat wanting a new deal after this season, which could complicate things. – Eric Edholm

There are conflicting reports on the 25-year-old Ojomo and whether the Eagles might consider dangling him. Normally, they'd want to build around an emerging defensive tackle who has started the past three games and been more productive recently than highly paid DTs Jalen Carter and Jordan Davis. But Ojomo is set to hit free agency, and it's unlikely the Eagles can afford him after what they paid Carter and Davis. If the Eagles have designs on re-signing Ojomo, they'll have to be creative. They are 2-6, losers of six straight, including back-to-back thumpings in prime time, and QB Jalen Hurts (along with others) is on injured reserve. The Jeffery Simmons trade to Chicago had to be an eye-opener for Philly, value-wise, and might cause the team to consider moving him before the deadline. – Eric Edholm

Baker has been tremendous for the Cardinals over his 10 seasons with the team, making eight Pro Bowls. But it might be time for a rebuilding club to move on. Baker can provide playmaking at secondary, as he has for a decade. He is signed through 2027, with a reasonable salary of a little more than $16 million for next season. So he wouldn't be a short-term rental for a team.

It would sting for the Cardinals to move on from Baker, one of the team's best players and only positive lights in the post-Larry Fitzgerald era. But it's prudent for a team looking for the future. And it would be a big get for a contending club that needs a difference maker in the secondary. – Frank Schwab

Following his three-pick game Sunday against Joe Burrow, Hooker is the NFL's INT leader with five, and his 114 return yards off those picks also is first. The 28-year-old veteran has quietly carved out a nice career over eight seasons with the Titans and is enjoying one of his better years, tackling and covering well. The Titans signed him to an extension last summer through the 2028 season with a fairly reasonable salary outside the top 10 safeties.

Given their 1-7 record at the bye, the Titans could offload more vets after trading Jeffery Simmons. – Eric Edholm

#2925. YouTube Gold: The Greatest Players Of The ABA

The ABA lived from 1967 to 1976. It was a short, chaotic, but also exuberant and fantastic era.

The management in the ABA was generally horrible, but there were some brilliant coaches and some unbelievable players.

Julius Erving was, is, and always will be the face of the ABA, but the league had some other immense talents. Guys like David Thompson, Spencer Haywood, Rick Barry (who started in the NBA but spent several years in the upstart league), Connie Hawkins, Dan Issel, Artis Gilmore, George Gervin, and the first great three-point shooter Louie Dampier, come to mind, along with Charlie Scott and Mel Daniels.

This video looks at some of the best players in ABA history.

The league was a race between chaos and brilliance. The merger with the NBA happened in 1976, just in time, because of mismanagement and the lack of a TV contract; the ABA was on borrowed time.

It's really too bad the league couldn't sustain itself, but it changed basketball forever, and its legacy should not be forgotten.

#2924. Voters rebuff data centers in every ballot measure except one during the 2026 midterms

The artificial intelligence data center boom hit a roadblock on election night, further complicating the tech industry's historic expansion plans.

There were 41 local ballot measures in 10 states up for a vote on Tuesday that would place restrictions on the construction of data centers in different ways, according to Ballotpedia.

Voters sided against data centers in each measure except one in Harwood, North Dakota, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday. The lone exception was Harwood, North Dakota, where voters rejected an added review of industrial projects, including data centers.

Ohio alone accounted for more than half of the measures, with 21 on the ballot. The issue also played out at the top of the ticket, where Democrat Sherrod Brown ousted Republican Sen. Jon Husted after casting the incumbent as "the face of data centers."

The Buckeye State, which was named America's Top State for Business in 2026 by CNBC, has become a key battleground for the issue.

OpenAI announced in August that it secured 8 gigawatts of computing capacity at the PORTS-Pike Technology Campus in Ohio, the largest planned AI data center campus in the world. The first phase of construction is slated to begin this year, and capacity is expected to come online in phases starting in 2028, according to the project's website.

SoftBank and SB Energy are developing the Ohio data center campus in partnership with the U.S. Department of Energy. SoftBank is a major investor in OpenAI, and the two companies announced plans to invest $1 billion in SB Energy earlier this year.

Nvidia is backstopping the project, which could cost more than $500 billion in total.

Those plans could now run into resistance from the governor's mansion. Ohio's top office flipped blue on Tuesday, as Democrat Amy Acton, a physician, defeated Republican businessman Vivek Ramaswamy. Acton supports a "conditional moratorium" on data centers to ensure they cover 100% of utility costs and "exclusively build with union labor."

Heading into the election, the AI trade was already faltering on Wall Street, with chipmakers, neoclouds and other data center-related companies selling off in October on concerns about rising interest rates and overspending. The hyperscalers — Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft and Meta — are each spending hundreds of billions of dollars a year on AI infrastructure to meet what they say is insatiable demand. All of those companies except Microsoft have turned cash flow negative in the process.

Getting data center projects off the ground may have just gotten a lot harder.

Communities in California, Florida, Illinois, Kansas, Maine, New Jersey, Nevada and Wisconsin also went to the polls to weigh in on the issue, according to Ballotpedia, and data centers were a key motivating factor for voters in matters up and down the ballot.

In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision, with Democrats ranking the issue higher than Republicans.

Democrats who campaigned on pausing or regulating new projects won key races in Ohio, Wisconsin, Georgia and Texas, which is on track to become the world's largest data center market by 2030.

In one of the most significant results of the night for Democrats, Gina Hinojosa unseated Gov. Greg Abbott after blaming the Republican incumbent for the rapid buildout of data centers across Texas, saying he put the tech industry's interests ahead of local communities.

The losses came despite record spending from Silicon Valley to shape the 2026 midterms, with the GOP boasting a commanding advantage among the election's biggest donors. According to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records, 14 of the 20 largest donor groups backed Republicans almost exclusively. Crypto, AI and online gambling interests poured in more than $377 million.

Elon Musk, the cycle’s largest individual donor, spent at least $474 million, nearly half of it in Texas, and lost both of the state’s marquee races. It was a warning sign for the wave of tech companies and founders who left California for Texas in search of a friendlier business climate.

"Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now," Musk wrote in a post on X.

Within hours of the polls closing, Democrats were already calling for AI executives to testify under oath.

Among the first to seize on the results was Rep. Greg Casar, the Texas Democrat who chairs the Congressional Progressive Caucus.

"The House Democratic majority must make it a top priority to protect Americans' jobs, freedom, and lives from AI billionaires," Casar said in a statement after Democrats were projected to win the House. "AI company CEOs should be called to testify under oath."

Casar also accused Republicans of being "bought and paid for by AI billionaires," arguing that voters had rejected that influence and warning Democrats to "hold them accountable instead of cashing their checks."

He said the data center fight is only the beginning.

"The revolt against data centers was one of the top issues of the 2026 election," he said. "And in a few years we will look back on it as just the early tremor of a much bigger AI earthquake upending American politics."

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the presumptive next chair of the House Oversight Committee, summed it up in two words: "Lawyer up."

— CNBC's Ashley Capoot contributed to this article.

#2921. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2915. Is Mitchell Robinson washed? (and other Mailbag answers)

Ok, for full disclosure, the question in the title wasn't actually asked. But that is the kind of vibe this mailbag has, so I felt it was representative of the overall feel. And as a reminder, I did ask people to provide their hot takes and questions. So don't be mad that people delivered. Don't hate the playa, hate the game. Which, would honestly be fair in this case.

Hot take 1: Big Country looks out of shape. Over the first few games he's playing well below how he has performed in prior seasons. I will be interested to see if this continues or if this is a product of the team getting used to Big Mitch and him getting used to the team.

The boring/optimistic answer is to preach patience and give him time to acclimate to his new surroundings and role. The concern is that these things don't always work out as we would hope. He had a specific role in New York and thrived in that system. What if he doesn't work within the Celtics structure? We haven't even hit the first time missed due to injury that we all expected.

At the moment I'm not overly worried (once again, small sample size) but it is something to keep an eye on.

Hot take 2: Derrick White should be traded. There are many trade approaches that could be taken, his contract could be used to grab either another center or forward in an effort to lean into Peyton, Baylor, Hugo, and Ron stepping up to fill his role/shift the ball handler role more onto JT and PG13, the other would be to find a way to make a trade exception, have the aforementioned bench players absorb some of those minutes and also create minutes for Chris Cenac. Ultimately Derrick White is close to his athletic downturn. His playstyle, though not nearly as reliant on his athletic ability, does operate on the right side of a thin margin that contributes of his ability to stay in front, react defensively, and generally use what athletic ability he does have to keep up with the more athletic players he usually is tasked with guarding. Losing a half step, which I believe he is in the process of doing, will put him on the WRONG side of that margin and we need to deal him before he is on the wrong side of that margin.

I love, love, love Derrick White. I do not want to trade Derrick White. I will fight for Derrick White.

With all that said, it is hard to argue that he might be a logical option to move. Not necessarily this year, but perhaps by this coming offseason. Smart GM's tend to move on from players before they lose a step and White's shooting woes last year indicate that he might be close to that downturn you are talking about.

He has a large (but not max) contract that could be paired with picks or players to bring back another star or an impact long-term piece.

With all that said, I'd be very reluctant to move White until one or two of the younger players has firmly established themselves as consistent performers. Not just "shows us something occasionally" but contributing on both ends on a nightly basis. You need consistent, effective adults on the floor to win games.

Hot take 3: we should flip Garza and give his minutes to Chris Cenac. Right now Garza is the much better player by a decent margin. I agree that the current minute allocation where Garza is the third big in the rotation will lead to the most wins throughout the season. However, Chris and Luka fill very similar niches when they are on the floor. Giving Chris those minutes now will lead to more winning further down the line. The one drawback is that Cenac starts to click early which will increase his value when his rookie contract ends which may cause an unnecessary increase in his first contract negotiations.

I legitimately don't think either of those last two things will happen, but for the first one I am optimistic that we will see an improvement of Mitch's fit within the next few games…

I support the idea of finding minutes for Cenac, but I don't think we necessarily need to bench Garza to get them. First of all, I think he could use the reps in Maine. Secondly, I think there will be plenty of scheduled (or sometimes unscheduled) games off for Mitchell or Queta and there will be opportunities for Cenac to play on the 2nd night of a back-t0-back or in garbage time.

I should probably be writing in all caps, cus that's how people seem to talk on ESPN, but I'll restrain myself.

Hot take 1: Luka Garza is the Celtics best offensive rebounder.

Hot take 2: Mitchell Robinson retires mid-season and joins Monster Jam, a place where his truck actually fits in the parking garage.

I wonder if Joe has had any thoughts about "borrowing" the truck to use as a getaway vehicle for his next theoretical ballpark heist.

Queta earned the starting spot last year, and Mitch is did not come in prepared to take over the starting spot. Queta should play the start and ends of the quarters, staggering his rest to be able to play 24-30 mpg. Mitch should play shorter stints in the middle of quarters to about 18-24 mpg; and Luka plays as needed. Refs are morons and they have a quicker whistle for backups. Let Queta's experience and reputation grow as a starter.

Mitch's minutes are about the same, points steady, rebounds way down, fouls up, blocks down. He's a career backup that should be beating up on other backups. Nobody is doing hack-a-mitch at the beginning of quarters. He's not playing like a starter, and Queta is thrown into a completely different environment from where he was a steady performer last year.

I will admit, I haven't had a strong take on which big should start. I can see the argument for both of them. Both of them are what I would consider starting quality bigs (based on performance in previous seasons). Both could and should thrive in a bench role. Both should get about the same number of minutes.

The rest is just about fit and chemistry with the units that they play with. I tend to think that the worries about hack-a-Mitch are a bit over-stated. It seems more important to me that he knows where to be on the court and how his teammates are going to be delivering the ball (or how their shots come off the rim). That will take time to develop and the coaching staff is going to need to see what downstream impacts occur in various lineup combinations.

#2911. What Trump losing power over Congress means for Iran, Ukraine and foreign policy

With Democrats taking control of both houses of Congress, U.S. President Donald Trump could intensify his focus on foreign policy in his final two years in office, while facing greater scrutiny over the wars in Iran and Ukraine, experts told CNBC.

Democrats will have a Senate majority and won control of the House, giving them greater scope to scrutinize the administration, shape spending and challenge Trump's foreign-policy agenda.

"We are going to see a president that is somewhat constrained on matters of oversight and investigation, but on foreign policy, he does still retain a lot of latitude," Laurel Rapp, director of Chatham House's U.S. and North America Programme, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Rapp said the Iran war was a "major issue" in this election cycle amid soaring fuel prices, food costs, and inflation. The war began when Trump launched joint strikes with Israel on Feb. 28.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage. We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter," Rapp added.

Democrats could use Congress' spending powers to try to restrict funding for the war, she added. Any bill directing Trump to end U.S. involvement would need two-thirds support in both chambers to override a presidential veto.

In a Wednesday note, Berenberg's chief economist, Holger Schmieding, predicted a final round of bombing in Iran, with Trump ultimately striking a deal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz by 2027, as voters feel the sting from high prices and fuel costs.

Mitchell Reiss, a fellow with defense and security think tank RUSI, predicted that Trump would replace Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth following the results.

"Traditionally, presidents do replace cabinet officials. Certainly, after midterm results like this one, if you remember, George W. Bush replaced Donald Rumsfeld as Secretary of Defense after midterm losses, so that wouldn't be a surprise," Reiss told CNBC on Wednesday.

J.C. Lintzenich, visiting senior fellow at the German Marshall Fund, said that the president is not a "lame duck."

"Remember that this election changes the Congress; it does not change the president," Lintzenich told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday.

"The President of the United States still has lots of power, lots of authority. We still have pending action in Iran. We still have an unresolved situation with Russia and Ukraine."

He added that while Congress has changed, foreign policy is still executed by the president and his appointed officials.

"What we'll now see is a little bit more congressional oversight, the application of funding, for example, Ukraine, that's required by statute and law," Lintzenich said.

Lintzenich said the outgoing Congress was "more willing to let the White House slide," but Democratic control of both chambers would mean a more assertive Congress, using its Article I powers over legislation, spending, and oversight to hold the White House more closely to existing law and congressional requirements.

Berenberg's Schmieding warned that Trump could face "more trouble ahead" as Democrats take control of the Senate.

He said Congress was unlikely to fund "an expensive campaign similar to the Iran war, but added that Trump, "as a perennial attention seeker without a consistent long-term strategy," may be "even more eager to pursue dangerous geopolitical adventures."

Speaking as voting took place on Tuesday, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said the U.S. midterm elections would "shape the future."

"The world will be watching," he said in Berlin. "Right now, things are deeply uncertain. Many hopes may once again end in disappointment."

Meanwhile, the Kremlin said it hoped the outcome of the midterms would not impact Trump's efforts to broker a peace deal between Ukraine and Russia, Reuters reported, citing local media.

The results could complicate Trump's ability to secure congressional backing or funding for a deal, but they do not prevent him from pressing Kyiv diplomatically. Berenberg's Schmieding said Trump could try to push Ukraine toward a deal favorable to Russia, though he considers that a "tail risk."

Schmieding said Trump's influence over Republicans could weaken after the midterm defeat, noting that Republicans, including Secretary of State Marco Rubio, are warier of Putin. "That can help to contain the risk of Trump trying to impose an unacceptable deal on Ukraine," he said.

If Trump did pressure Ukraine, Europe would likely increase its support, Schmieding said, but it could not fully replace critical U.S. military and intelligence assistance.

Christel Schaldemose, a vice president of the European Parliament, said the result "could benefit the support for Ukraine" but added: "The EU must continue the course of standing stronger and standing on its own two feet."

#2909. The most terrifying part of losing is having nothing to gain

We currently find ourselves in the trenches, five games into the season, with the Phoenix Suns sitting at 1-4. And when you're down in the trenches, you typically don't have the luxury of looking at things from 30,000 feet. There's too much happening around you, and the moment you start thinking too big or too far into the future, something changes that demands your immediate attention. Such is life. Ultimately, what defines quality strategy is the ability to balance the immediate challenges in front of you with the health and viability of your franchise over the long term.

But there's a realization slowly creeping into the frontal lobes of our brains, something we've been actively trying to avoid thinking about because we know that once we accept it as reality, the outlook becomes increasingly frustrating and dystopian. Last year's team saved us from these thoughts. This year's team may not be able to. The Suns don't own their own draft capital, and no matter what happens, sucking as a basketball team provides them absolutely no benefit.

The reality of professional sports is that teams ebb and flow. The advantage of possessing your own draft assets is that when an inevitable down season arrives, you have an opportunity to turn that adversity into something useful. Down seasons can happen for a multitude of reasons, with injuries often being the primary culprit. You struggle for a year, acquire a valuable asset through the draft, and get back to work. If you're a perpetually bad team, the draft provides a pathway toward rebuilding your roster.

There's no perfect formula for constructing a championship roster, or even one that's consistently competitive. Throughout NBA history, organizations have employed numerous strategies with varying degrees of success. But as we look at the season ahead, we're staring at a Suns team that appears disjointed and poorly constructed. The roster features expensive players with duplicative skill sets, an abundance of undersized contributors, and injuries that only complicate matters further.

Things can get really bad in Phoenix, really quickly. The most unsettling part? There's no eject button.

You might believe that trading Devin Booker is ultimately the answer, but it's not. Perhaps Booker is overvalued as an asset within the Phoenix market because of everything he's meant to this organization and how long he's been its centerpiece. His value on the trade market may ultimately produce a return that falls short of what Suns fans envision. If that return includes multiple first-round picks, there's a very real possibility those selections end up being far less desirable than what Phoenix would need to meaningfully rebuild over the long term.

Booker is now 30, which isn't what you would classify as "old". At least, you wouldn't have 10 years ago. Kirk Golderberry of The Ringer, however, recently delved into the decline in players over 30 and their impact on the game:

During the 2025-26 regular season, players 30 and older made up just 17.5 percent of the NBA. That's the lowest share in at least 30 years. Meanwhile, for the seventh straight season, more than half the league (54.6 percent) was 25 or younger. In 1997-98, that number was just 36 percent.

Yeah, that wasn't exactly my favorite read of the week. Nothing quite like being reminded that the guy who's supposed to be in his prime and leading this team to victories is rapidly becoming antiquated in a league that increasingly values youth and durability over sustainability and longevity. Great. Fantastic. Love that for us.

I had someone suggest trading Devin Booker to the Detroit Pistons for Ausar Thompson and four first-round picks. As unlikely as that scenario might be, let's play it out.

The Suns acquire Ausar Thompson, meaning they're now building around Jalen Green, Thompson, Dillon Brooks, Miles Bridges, and Khaman Maluach. How many games is that team winning? My guess is they're a .500 team at best. Let's say, in a best-case scenario, Phoenix somehow ends up with the sixth seed in the Western Conference, although reality probably suggests they're a Play-In team at best. Regardless, because the Suns don't control their own draft pick, they're handing that selection over to Houston. No, the Suns get the worst pick between Cleveland, Minnesota, and Utah. For thought exercise purposes, let's say Phoenix receives the 25th overall selection.

But wait! You traded Devin Booker to Detroit for four first-round picks! Except now Booker is playing alongside Cade Cunningham and Jalen Duren, and suddenly the Pistons look fantastic because Booker is operating in a role that better suits him. Detroit hypothetically ends up with the 24th pick.

So Phoenix walks into next year's draft holding the 24th and 25th overall selections. Cool. No high-end prospects. No obvious game changers. Instead, you're selecting a pair of developmental projects, hoping they possess enough transferable skills to become viable rotation players three years down the road. You get a couple of Ryan Dunns.

Then it becomes rinse and repeat. Every year, the Suns are struggling to assemble a competitive roster, attempting to do so with Detroit's draft capital while their own selections remain outside their control.

Why am I bringing this up five games into the season? Why am I looking at the bigger picture when I should be focusing on Portland tonight? I think it's because the potential worst-case scenario is beginning to unfold before our eyes. And while the fan base desperately rushes to trade machines, searching for some magical combination of transactions that will fix everything, the reality is becoming increasingly difficult to ignore. This is a very hard situation to fix.

Perhaps it's because I'm seeing the suggestions people are putting out there, and while I understand the strategy behind many of these proposed deals, I ultimately find myself asking one question. How the hell did we get here?

To me, the answer is simple: Bradley Beal.

I know plenty of people will argue that acquiring Kevin Durant was the beginning of the downfall of the Phoenix Suns, and I don't completely disagree. That move was a step in the wrong direction, one that could ultimately take this organization a decade to recover from. The Suns had what makes teams successful in today's NBA: youth, connectivity, chemistry, and a veteran presence to stabilize it all. It's reminiscent of the Seven Seconds or Less Suns, a team that was ahead of its time. By the time the rest of the league caught up to that philosophy, Phoenix had already moved on to something different, never fully capitalizing on the strategy it helped pioneer.

Through that lens, I can accept the argument that Durant's acquisition was the root cause of where we find ourselves today. As for the extracurricular drama he brought to the organization, and the notion that his presence was ultimately more detrimental than productive, I politely disagree. Sure, there were extracurriculars with Kevin Durant. Nobody knows that better than I do. But he was also an elite scorer, someone you could rely on at virtually any moment to get you a bucket. It was the moves made around him that ultimately sunk this team, and none was more damaging than acquiring Bradley Beal.

The Suns acquired a depreciating asset whose skill set was largely duplicative of Devin Booker's, sacrificed the remainder of their available draft capital to do so, and inherited a no-trade clause that made an already difficult situation even worse. Ultimately, they had to buy Beal out and stretch the financial consequences across multiple seasons.

Think about this team right now and how differently you might view it with an additional $19.3 million in payroll flexibility. Need a point guard? How about another big man who can actually rebound? That money could help address those issues, but it's unavailable because the Suns waived Bradley Beal. And the ancillary draft capital that could facilitate additional transactions? That's gone, too, because they shipped it away to acquire Bradley fucking Beal.

That's the deal we're going to continue paying for through 2030. That's the transaction whose consequences continue to suffocate this franchise, even after the player himself is long gone. And that's the draft capital we sacrificed, capital that could have been used to facilitate the very transactions this team so desperately needs today.

The reality of what the Phoenix Suns might become if they aren't a competitive basketball team is something we accepted at the beginning of last season. The thought process at the time was that eventually we'd trade Grayson Allen and Royce O'Neale, Dillon Brooks was a throw-in from the Kevin Durant trade, and Jalen Green had enough upside to warrant seeing what he could become. Expectations were low as we prepared for this dommsdat scenario to play out. Phoenix surprised us, however, winning 45 games.

The team did eventually trade Grayson Allen and Royce O'Neale, and in return acquired Miles Bridges, who thus far has probably been the most consistent player on the team. But we also surrendered our 2033 first-round pick to make it happen. Why? Nobody fucking knows. Because the Suns "expect to be good", right? They operate as though they'll never need a contingency plan, and when circumstances inevitably demand one, they're screwed.

Jalen Green has upside. He has athleticism. And he absolutely does not fit on this team. If he were making $16 million a year, perhaps I wouldn't think much of it. But he's making $36.3 million this season and another $36 million next year, with a player option he's almost certainly going to exercise because he's unlikely to command that kind of money on the open market. So the Suns are left holding the bag. Again.

When was the last time the Suns made a trade and actually won it? When was the last time you looked back three years after a transaction and thought, you know what, "Slam dunk for Phoenix"? The Barkley trade? KJ?

That's why I'm feeling so much doom and gloom right now. That's where the frustration comes from. The reality we managed to put off last season because the team overperformed is beginning to show signs of coming to fruition this year. Now another year has passed, Devin Booker's trade value isn't getting any higher, and even if it were, it wouldn't change the fundamental problem. This franchise simply cannot benefit from being a losing basketball team.

I'm writing this because I see all the solutions people are desperately trying to come up with to save the Suns, and every time another proposal surfaces, I find myself asking the same questions: How? Why? Is Dejounte Murray going to be the guy who saves this franchise? Domantas Sabonis? Are those really the answers to the problems we're facing? No.

The fact that we've reached the point of entertaining those possibilities only five games into the season says plenty about the long-term outlook of this organization. And perhaps that's what I'm really doing here. Trying to mentally prepare myself for a reality we've spent the past year hoping we'd never have to confront.

The Suns can improve their execution, find better lineup combinations, and perhaps salvage something from this season. But none of that changes the underlying reality. This organization has sacrificed its flexibility, its financial resources, and its ability to benefit from failure. Five games into the season, we're already contemplating the consequences of decisions made years ago. The Suns aren't necessarily doomed to lose. They're trapped in a situation where losing doesn't help them win tomorrow.

#2905. Hyperscalers and Apple surge after election results remove investor uncertainty

Many of the biggest technology companies are surging on Wednesday, after Democrats scored a seismic victory in the midterm elections, flipping both the House and Senate.

During trading on Wednesday, Amazon surged 8%, Google rose over 6%, Microsoft rose 4%, Meta was up 3%, and Apple — which faced Trump's tariffs and recently got refunds — climbed nearly 7%.

Tuesday's results remove uncertainty in the market, David Kelly, chief global strategist at JPMorgan Asset Management, said on "Squawk on the Street."

He said the divided government would lead to "moderation in growth, moderation in inflation, less uncertainty, and I think that's all helping the stock market move up today."

However, some stocks, particularly those tied to Trump advisor Elon Musk as well as the AI data center buildout, fell on Wednesday.

As results came in last night, some observers saw bad news for big technology companies because of their bets to build large AI-focused data centers. There were 41 local ballot measures on Tuesday to place restrictions or clear the way for AI data centers, and voters sided against data centers in 40 of them. In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their voting decision.

Analysts said that Democrats who proposed data center moratoriums won gubernatorial races in Texas, Georgia, and Ohio, raising fears that projects could get snarled in regulation or blocked by politicians.

"Recall that backup power emissions, zoning, water, interconnect/grid, and tax considerations are often overseen by state regulators," Macquarie analyst Paul Golding said in a note on Wednesday.

Tesla stock fell 2%, and SpaceX was down 3%. Nvidia, which makes the chips for most of these AI data center projects, slipped just over 1%.

Many of the technology stocks rising on Wednesday are what are known as hyperscalers, or the largest cloud providers that also pair massive capex spending with robust free cash flow and have the most experience and capability to construct AI data centers.

"We believe higher regulatory barriers entrench incumbents, and increase risks (and raise required ROICs) for new entrants," wrote Needham analyst Laura Martin in a note on Wednesday. "Hyperscalers are AI incumbents."

Smaller AI cloud providers, often called neoclouds, got whacked on Wednesday. CoreWeave stock fell 10%. Baird analysts said in a note that the election results create a "sentiment headwind" for neoclouds.

Golding, the Macquarie analyst, wrote that he remains bullish on AI infrastructure, and that well-capitalized, high-quality operators stand to benefit the most if projects in some states slow down.

"Neoclouds may pay more to lease powered shells for GPUaaS commitments (and pass on costs), while hyperscaler/AI lab capacity needs could intensify competition for even more limited supply," Golding wrote.

Tuesday's results could signal restrictions on new data center developments in some states, but it doesn't necessarily signal a nationwide slowdown in construction, Chris Miglino, CEO of Axe Compute, said in an email. Companies are still raising capital, enterprises are spending, and there's still a need for more capacity.

Miglino said that increased regulation in some regions could cause investors and customers to look more closely at different cloud computing providers, and opt for those with the capability to deliver capacity, or which offer AI inference to a specific industry niche.

"If you've already secured power, have the financing in place and understand how to deploy and operate compute infrastructure efficiently, you're in a much stronger position than a company that's still trying to figure those things out," Miglino said.

#2903. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.
