#2902. Snap shares fall over 10% on third-quarter revenue miss

Snap shares sank over 10% when the Snapchat parent missed on revenue and daily active users in its third-quarter earnings report Wednesday.

Here's how the company did compared with analysts' expectations:

Sales in Snap's third quarter jumped 14% year-over-year while net income came in at $56 million, compared to the $104 million net loss it logged the prior year.

Snap's third-quarter adjusted earnings came in at $383 million, topping the $337 million that analysts were projecting, according to StreetAccount.

The Snapchat parent said fourth-quarter revenue should come in between $1.95 billion and $1.99 billion. The midpoint of that figure is higher than Wall Street's estimates of $1.95 billion.

Fourth-quarter adjusted earnings are expected to come in between $490 million and $525 million. Analysts were expecting that figure to be $498 million.

Snap executives said in an investor letter that the company plans for globally monthly active users to become its "primary audience metric" starting in its first quarter for 2027. The company also said it has 977 MAU for the quarter.

"Daily engagement has historically been stable as a percentage of monthly engagement, but we do not intend to optimize Snapchat around daily use at the expense of long-term value for our community," the investor letter said. "A longer-term horizon better aligns our reported metrics with the product and safety decisions that strengthen relationships, create lasting utility, and support broader, more durable audience growth."

Snap chief commercial officer Ronan Harris told analysts during an earnings call that lower spending by unspecified consumer packaged goods, or CPG, companies impacted third-quarter sales. Harris attributed the curbed CPG spending to "macro pressures in the U.S. and also the Middle East.""As we look ahead, we're seeing very positive signs of growth from diversified verticals like financial services in the U.S.," Harris said. "So part of our strategy here is to look outside of the pressures in CPG and find traction in these other verticals."

#2901. Milwaukee Bucks vs. Boston Celtics Preview & Game Thread: Road warriors?

The Milwaukee Bucks do battle with the 5-2 Boston Celtics tonight as they seek their first road victory of the season. On Monday in Minnesota, the Bucks entered the fourth quarter down just four points after a back-and-forth affair, only to see the rim shrink and, once again, go cold to close out the game. Meanwhile, the Celtics were in Houston and lost to the Rockets in a similar fashion, with their offense running out of steam in the fourth. Milwaukee will look to avoid a Beantown beatdown and hope to see Burries, Jakucionis, and other young Bucks continue to find their stride.

Boston was without Paul George Monday evening for a planned rest day, and George is expected back in the lineup, while Jayson Tatum will likely be on the bench for a scheduled rest day himself. In typical Boston fashion, though, they have been a model of consistency with almost any lineup they have thrown out there. The Celtics currently rank 10th in point differential, 13th in Offensive eFG%, and 12th in Defensive eFG%, while playing effective team defense that forces turnovers through help-side rotations. However, when the Celtics struggle, it often happens in the 4th quarter: they are -29 on the season in the final quarter of games, and it nearly cost them in their game against Brooklyn.

For this game, then, it will be about who maximizes their strengths first. Boston runs an effective half-court offence but is 26th in pace; meanwhile, the Bucks, for all their flaws, are fourth in pace. Both teams shoot a lot of three-pointers but are below league average in makes, with the Bucks shooting 33.3% and the Celtics shooting 34.5%. So if the Bucks can get more possessions by winning the offensive glass, they will have a good chance of walking away with an upset win (provided Kyle Kuzma doesn't have another -22 net rating in 21 minutes of a 12-point loss, of course). This means that Kel'el Ware will need to have his presence felt. The Celtics are a bottom-five team in offensive rebounding; whether that means they aren't prioritizing the O-boards or don't have the personnel to get them, Ware has a real opportunity to prove himself as a difference-maker in that area.

Payton Pritchard: Questionable (right shoulder contusion)

Jericho Sims. We saw Sims get his first real minutes against the Wolves, and he was impressive. Was that a blip on the radar, or will we get another impressive performance? Also, the Celtics like to hunt for mismatches, so if the Bucks can deploy a big who has no problems switching, it will do a lot to mitigate that.

Who: Milwaukee Bucks (1-6) vs. Boston Celtics (5-2).

In his second NBA start, Brayden Burries will find his rhythm and come up in a big way for the Bucks, posting a double-double with 18 points, 10 rebounds, and six assists!

#2900. Miami vs Notre Dame Prediction: Odds, expert picks, team and player news, betting trends, and stats

The game of the week is in South Bend! The #1 Miami Hurricanes (8-0) go to #11 Notre Dame (7-1) on the biggest Saturday of the year. In the initial College Football Rankings, Notre Dame is 11th; presumably, if the Irish lose to Miami, the Irish are out of the playoffs, even with Boston College, SMU, and Syracuse looming. Miami beat Notre Dame 27-24 on a field goal with 1:03 remaining to open the 2025 season, so this is also a revenge spot for Notre Dame. The Irish opened as a -3.5 point favorite, but big bettors have moved this line to Notre Dame -7!

Both Miami and Notre Dame showed struggles last week. Notre Dame trailed 13-7 to Navy before going on a 28-0 run to end the game, while Miami was spotted playing tennis with North Carolina, going back and forth in a 38-35 shootout. It was the second time Miami was pushed to the brink of their first loss (Florida State), but the Darian Mensah and Malachi Toney connection seemingly can't be stopped, unless there was an injury. Rumors are Toney hurt his ankle at practice on Wednesday and his status is questionable ahead of this game.

Mensah and Toney are the frontrunners for the Heisman, while Notre Dame's CJ Carr is fourth in terms of odds. This game will shape the Heisman market as well as Georgia versus Ole Miss and Ohio State against Oregon. The matchup in Notre Dame and Miami that everyone will be locked into is Toney versus Leonard Moore. Notre Dame and Moore haven't faced a passing attack all season quite like this, and those one-on-ones between the two will be highlighted every drive.

The biggest question for a lot of people in this game is: can the Miami defense slow down Notre Dame's offense? That's a fair point. My biggest question is, can CJ Carr win a big game? He's 0-3 against top 16 teams and 17-0 outside that range. His best win would be over #20 USC or unranked Arkansas? This is a major test for Carr, just as much as it is for the Miami defense.

Let's dive into the schools on both sides of the ball, look at a few key players, and see where the numbers take us.

Moneyline: Notre Dame Fighting Irish (-258), Miami Hurricanes (+210)

He will have to find receivers outside of Toney if Moore is lined up specifically on him, but the offense is dynamic enough that he can be a decoy and they can still move the ball.

Miami's defense is top 25 in the country with 17.7 points per game allowed, but they've been disregarded as a top-tier unit because of 43 points to Florida State and 35 to North Carolina. The Hurricanes will have a lot to prove, especially in the secondary. Miami gave up 298 passing yards and four touchdowns to Ashton Daniels (FSU) and 341 yards and four more touchdowns to Billy Edwards Jr. (UNC) in two of the last three games. CJ Carr could certainly have a day if the Irish want to be aggressive and work downfield. However, that's not how the Irish operate now. Miami's rush defense is formidable, but it will have a massive challenge ahead trying to contain Notre Dame's three-running-back committee. Miami's defense, on paper, looks outmatched against Notre Dame, which is one of the reasons money is coming in on the Irish.

Last week, Marquise Lightfoot had two sacks at North Carolina, tying his season-high, plus the second-most tackles in a game of his career (7). Lightfoot has seven sacks on the season now and also earned his second forced fumble last week. Miami ranks 14th in pressure created on defense, and Lightfoot on the edge is a big reason why.

#2899. Lions promote LB Joe Bachie to active roster, sign two other LBs

The Detroit Lions announced three linebacker-related roster transactions on Wednesday: signing Joe Bachie from the practice squad to the active roster, signing K.J. Britt from the Patriots' practice squad to the active roster, and signing Owen Pappoe to the practice squad.

No immediate roster moves were necessary, as the Lions previously opened two spots on their 53-man roster on Tuesday when they placed rookie linebacker Jimmy Rolder on injured reserve and waived rookie safety Bishop Fitzgerald. Bachie's promotion from the practice squad opened a spot for Pappoe.

Bachie joined the Lions in April and provided veteran experience throughout training camp at MIKE linebacker and on special teams. He performed well enough in the preseason to be firmly on the roster bubble, and while the Lions released him in cutdowns, they signed him back to the practice squad the next day. When it became clear Rolder couldn't start the season due to injury, the Lions signed Bachie to the active roster for their first two games, then released him in late September and re-signed him to the practice squad.

With Bachie operating as the team's LB7, the injuries to Jack Campbell and Rolder have created a need for him to step into a larger role moving forward. While he will almost certainly be a fixture on special teams, his experience and positional versatility could put him at LB4 behind Derrick Barnes, Trevor Nowaske, and Malcolm Rodriguez, the three presumed starters.

As for Britt and Pappoe, it will likely take time for them to acclimate to the Lions' defensive scheme. That said, Britt has plenty of special teams experience, and with the Lions' preference to keep six linebackers active on game days, he could be active as soon as this Sunday against the Dolphins.

#2898. Former NFL Player Doesn’t Hold Back on Criticism of Dallas Cowboys’ Defense

Former NFL linebacker Emmanuel Acho delivered a scathing assessment of the Dallas Cowboys' defense after its collapse against the Arizona Cardinals in Week 8. His criticism followed a 33-30 overtime defeat that turned a comfortable lead into another damaging loss for the struggling team. The analyst said the performance was difficult to comprehend.

The defense appeared to be making progress early Sunday, limiting Arizona to a field goal in the first half and helping Dallas build a 17-3 advantage. The unit recorded two takeaways before halftime and generated pressure, giving the Cowboys a chance to control the game after the break. The first half offered a brief sign of progress.

Nov 27, 2025; Arlington, Texas, USA; Dallas Cowboys defensive tackle Quinnen Williams (92) warms up prior to the game against the Kansas City Chiefs at AT&T Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Kevin Jairaj-Imagn Images

That momentum disappeared in the second half, when the Cardinals erupted for 30 points and 286 yards. Allgeier finished with 147 rushing yards and a touchdown.

#2897. Game Preview #9: Wolves at Rockets Game Thread

We at Canis Hoopus usually try to get game recaps out a full day before the games so everyone can get their jokes and thoughts going in the comments. It's what makes this community so fun for so many of us, myself included. From GIFs to shitposts to real analysis and everything in between, this is where to find it.

However, some of us were a little busy tracking some election day results. We were watching a blue wave instead of watching a wave of blue at Target Center. We were celebrating the left wing, not analyzing the wings left with Anthony Edwards out. We were… You get the point, but I have a ton of these.

Now, with the Minnesota Timberwolves heading down to Texas to face the 7-1, conference-leading Houston Rockets, it's time to flip red to blue, just as the state just did.

It's a pretty great day to wake up and rep the Wolves, and to completely destroy now-jobless Greg Abott's favorite team.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (5-3) vs Houston Rockets (7-1)

When: Wednesday, November 4th at 7:30 PM CT

So far this season, the Houston Rockets have played fast. After being a team largely defined by defense, Houston is now winning their games the way people have been doing for decades: by simply scoring more points than their opponents.

Getting Fred VanVleet back after his season-ending injury has done a lot to organize the Rockets, but it is their ability to turn defense into offense that ultimately has turned them from a Kevin Durant shooting clinic into a well-balanced, well-oiled machine.

To their credit, the Timberwolves have been aiming to do a similar thing in the absence of Anthony Edwards. Without Ant, the Wolves have been relying on the talent and speed of LaMelo Ball.

"I like to play fast, and he's faster than no other," Ball said earlier this week.

While his driving (not the on-court kind) has gotten most of the focus in regard to his speed, Ball has been the fulcrum Minnesota needs to stay in games without their superstar wing and primary half-court shot creator.

However, it has also been Ayo Dosunmu's incredible play filling in for Edwards that really should get more attention. So much of the conversation around Ayo was about this feigned outrage over a perceived overpay, but the Illinois guard is more valuable for the Wolves than any other team. He can fill in for either Ball or Edwards when needed, and can certainly fit with anyone else in the rotation.

The Twin Turbos of Dosunmu and Bones Hyland have been the two main beneficiaries of Edwards' absence, and it's their pursuit of transition opportunities that has kept Minnesota alive.

The team is coming off a win against the Milwaukee Bucks that showed both of them off, and will now hopefully carry that momentum into Houston.

I want all of you to take a look at this clip for a moment. While on the surface it looks like Kevin Durant is once again getting irritated with what seems like a relatively innocuous question, there's a moment when Durant shifts from being annoyed to actually respecting the reporter's questions and thoughts.

That's because this is a really good goddamn question.

For years, the balance for stars has been all about involving teammates while also not shrinking under the lights. Many years ago, 538 put out an article about not just ball dominance but emotional dominance within a team structure. That piece determined that Durant was not an alpha.

It's been the same argument for much of LeBron James' career, and it is an argument that continues to be completely asinine.

The best players use their gravity to create for others as they do for themselves. How often they pull off those different things is what we use to define players as scorers or creators.

Durant has been an underrated playmaker for the entire duration of his career because of how good he is as a scorer. Just a few games ago, Jabari Smith Jr. had a 30-point game largely off of attacking closeouts that Durant put him in position to beat.

The Wolves will have to do a good job of not just defending Durant but also minimizing the ways he can enable everyone around him. That's so much easier said than done, and may not even be possible in general.

With Alperun Sengun at center, the Wolves can't move Rudy Gobert to guard Durant, so they will have to ask Jaden McDaniels and Jonathan Kuminga to guard him while not sacrificing their rebounding that has been so vital recently.

Answers, however, are easy to think of and hard to implement, so watch out for a big Durant game, even if the box score doesn't necessarily show it.

#2896. The NFL has a quarterback problem. The Ravens just became Exhibit A

The precise organizational term for the current state of the Baltimore Ravens is: screwed. It's a fate that likely awaits every other team that doesn't already have a quarterback succession plan in place, right this very moment. But Baltimore is uniquely beset by injury.

The Ravens lost all-everything quarterback Lamar Jackson on the very first play of Sunday's game against the Buffalo Bills to a thumb injury that will likely cost him the remainder of the season. Making matters infinitely worse, Baltimore subsequently lost backup Tyler Huntley to a toe injury that ended his season.

Oh, and just to add to the joy, undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano, who leaped from emergency No. 3 quarterback into the lineup on Sunday, is himself dealing with an ankle injury. It rains, it pours, and sometimes, it Biblically floods.

As for Baltimore's quarterback options now, well, Wednesday they dug through the scrapheap and pulled out Jacoby Brissett … who was just benched in Arizona in favor of Gardner Minshew.

The Ravens are in the teeth of the most vexing conundrum in the NFL right now: how to even survive, much less thrive, when your quarterback goes down. It's the most important position in team sports, by a wide margin, and consequently the most challenging one to fill. More complex offenses, more ferocious defenses, more pressure and more expectation all combine to forge a few elite quarterbacks, and shatter the rest.

You don't need any kind of advanced metrics to know what your eyes tell you: There are more teams in the NFL than there are good quarterbacks to lead them. The numbers back this up: ESPN's Total QBR stat, in this instance, is a useful benchmark. QBR doesn't simply total up yardage and touchdown passes; it incorporates a range of game situations and quarterback attributes, making it a solid starting point for discussions of elite, serviceable and unusable quarterbacks.

In the Total QBR system, a ranking of about 50 is average, and 25 is replacement-level. Players around the 50-point mark include Michael Penix Jr. (56.2), Sam Darnold (49.4) and Aaron Rodgers (49.2). So far, so good, right? At the low end of the 30 quarterbacks who have played enough to statistically qualify for the QBR leaderboard, you'll find Jameis Winston (41.4), Deshaun Watson (40.9), Daniel Jones (39.4) and Justin Herbert (32.6) — again, no real surprises there.

The Ravens had to face Josh Allen with undrafted Joe Fagnano under center after both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley went down.

But when you look at the overall performance of all players who have taken snaps or thrown passes this season, that's when you really get a sense of how bad quarterback play can be. Fagnano (who will start Thursday while the Ravens wait for Brissett's arrival) posted a QBR of 7.4 in his emergency stint on Sunday. Tanner McKee is at 30.7 after starting, and losing, three games for Philadelphia. (He's now been benched in favor of Joe Milton, who has never started an NFL game.) And Cooper Rush posted an almost incomprehensibly bad QBR of 4.7 — again, 25 is replacement-level — in his two losing starts for Atlanta earlier this season. The Falcons might have been better off just direct-snapping to Bijan Robinson every play, and the Ravens might need to consider the same route with Derrick Henry.

We can also determine the teams at most risk of a falloff due to quarterback injury by using a less scientific but more financially-motivated metric. Yahoo Sports' Ben Fawkes has created a list of NFL starting quarterbacks' value in terms of point spreads vs. their backups — in other words, the amount of value that a single starting quarterback brings to a team's odds — and, again, the results won't surprise you.

Per Fawkes' metric, the quarterback most important to his team's point spreads is — shocker — the Bills' Josh Allen, who's responsible alone for 7.23 points against the spread. Put another way, if Allen were injured and backup Kyle Allen needed to come in, any scenario where Buffalo was favored by fewer than +7.5 would immediately flip the Bills from huge favorite to pick-em status. That's pretty damn significant! Wrap Josh Allen in bubble wrap, Buffalo!

The next most important quarterbacks: Patrick Mahomes (backup: Justin Fields), Matthew Stafford (backup: Ty Simpson), and Drake Maye (backup: Tommy DeVito). Jackson comes in at fifth, responsible for 5.89 points against the spread, but that's with Huntley as his backup. Now? The gap might be double digits.

So you can see the problem here. There already aren't enough quality quarterbacks in the NFL, and the teams that have more than one in-house — looking at you, San Francisco — aren't parting with them for anything less than obscene ransoms.

Look upon the list of backups this year in the NFL, and despair. All due respect to Trey Lance, Quinn Ewers, Sam Howell, Davis Mills and the rest of the "hey, I remember that guy" guys holding tablets on sidelines across the league, but there's just not much there there.

Which is how the Ravens have ended up turning to Brissett who, in fairness, has a QBR (48.9) only slightly worse than the aforementioned Rodgers.

The one hope that teams have is the Sam Darnold Scenario. Darnold, of course, sputtered from starter to backup, buried on San Francisco's depth chart until Minnesota resurrected his career and Seattle cemented his legacy with a Super Bowl title.

We have two extremely small sample size cases already this season: J.J. McCarthy, who seems rejuvenated with the Giants after flaming out in Minnesota, and Gardner Minshew, who checked in from 2019 to lead the Cardinals to a win over Dallas on Sunday. (McCarthy's QBR this year is 60.5 and Minshew's is 71.3, in case you were wondering.)

Beyond that, teams have to either mortgage their future with draft capital — say, overpaying for Kirk Cousins, if the Raiders were even interested in trading him — or grit their teeth and weather the storm until the offseason. When demand for quarterbacks far outstrips the supply, it's only a matter of time before someone — today, the Ravens; tomorrow, who knows? — ends up with another team's reject. That's the reality in a league now centered on, and entirely dependent on, elite quarterbacks.

#2895. Figma sinks after earnings beat as company prioritizes long-term growth with AI changes

Figma shares tumbled 7% in extended trading after the company reported third-quarter results and guidance that surpassed Wall Street's predictions.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Figma's revenue grew about 43% year over year in the quarter, according to a statement. Net loss narrowed to $81.3 million, or 15 cents per share, compared with a loss of $1.10 billion, or $2.72 per share, a year earlier.

The company's 12% adjusted operating margin exceeded the 6.1% consensus among analysts polled by StreetAccount.

Executives lifted the full-year forecast. Figma now anticipates an 11% operating margin, up from 9% as of August, and $1.497 billion to $1.499 billion in revenue, compared with $1.463 billion to $1.467 billion before. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $1.471 billion in 2026 revenue.

The company sees fourth-quarter revenue growth slowing to about 33%. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted 28% growth.

Figma's announcement comes a day after highly valued design software startup Canva said it would cut 22% of its employees.

Shares have come down about 41% so far in 2026, while the S&P 500 index has gained 12%. Earlier this year, investors became concerned that artificial intelligence models could pose a competitive risk to Figma. Across much of software, AI panic has largely subsided as incumbents have posted persistent growth.

Figma wants to benefit from the rising adoption of AI to build websites and apps. During the quarter, the company started giving more AI credits to clients while maintaining prices.

"What that means is you're potentially trading off near-term revenue, investing in your customers, and then allowing yourself to then grow durably over the long term," Figma's finance chief, Praveer Melwani, told CNBC in an interview on Wednesday.

The number of weekly users working at organizations that spend over $10,000 on Figma who used AI credits increased by almost 50% during the quarter, Figma said in the statement. The share of work the company's own AI model performs doubled, and the rate of people undoing AI-generated work has fallen.

"If there's certain actions that we can power for our customers at lower latency, lower cost, or similar quality to third party, we're open to that as well," Melwani said.

Figma is increasing its focus on cybersecurity, said Dylan Field, Figma's co-founder and CEO. In recent months, AI agents have infiltrated systems at Hugging Face and other companies.

Clients aren't asking Figma to become a security vendor, but the company must scrutinize "the security of all of our vendors right now," Field said.

#2893. UFC Vegas 124: Waldo Cortes Acosta confident he will KO Johnny Walker

The Dominican fighter not only has extreme confidence in his punching power and striking, but he also has little confidence in his opponent's ability to take a hit. Cortes Acosta (17-4 MMA, 10-4 UFC) fights veteran Johnny Walker (23-10 MMA, 9-7 UFC) in the main event of Saturday's UFC Fight Night 293, which takes place at the Meta APEX (Paramount+).

This will be Walker's second fight at heavyweight, as he recently debuted in the division with a spectacular knockout of Mick Parkin. Although the Brazilian impressed many in his heavyweight debut, Cortes Acosta is not too high on his rival's potential in the new weight class.

"I think him trying to be better now at heavyweight because at light heavyweight he didn't do nothing at all," Cortes Acosta said at Wednesday's UFC Fight Night 293 media day. "He didn't have a lot finishes at light heavyweight because everybody would knock him out. He doesn't have a chin. It's like a doorbell; it's easy to hit it. Now he thinks he can be at heavyweight and have a better chance to move forward and one day get a title shot, but before that he needs to pass me and a lot of people, real heavyweights."

Some might caution Cortes Acosta about Walker's speed and explosiveness, but Cortes Acosta doesn't think there's much to be cautious about when facing Walker this Saturday.

"I don't need to measure anybody," Cortes Acosta said. "I go after him and look to break his head. I don't think I need to play with anybody. I know now how my chin is, it's like metal, and my punches, if I move my feet, you're going to sleep."

#2891. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs. Dallas Mavericks

The Spurs will look to keep their winning streak alive the Mavericks pay a visit. The two teams faced each other once already in this young season, but Dallas was without its two best players and simply couldn't keep up in the fourth quarter against a more talented opponent. Things could be different this time around.

Cooper Flagg has looked like a superstar in his second season, averaging 25-6-6 and showing the type of maturity that is not common in players his age. He's coming off a poor performance in a loss against the Kings, but his ceiling seems unlimited, and his production makes him a problem on a nightly basis. Kyrie Irving missed the game against Sacramento, as he's sitting out occasionally while he recovers from knee surgery, but the veteran point guard is averaging 21 points and five assists, and seems like the perfect partner for the developing Flagg. Both should be available on Wednesday, so it should be a competitive matchup.

While the Mavs have been solid this season, currently boasting a winning record, the Spurs are the hottest team in the league right now. After dropping their first two games, they have prevailed in their next five despite still trying to figure out their rotations, in part thanks to a fantastic stretch by Victor Wembanyama. They clearly have the talent to contend and should be considered the favorite over Dallas, but Flagg could present problems for a San Antonio team that starts a really small unit. On the other hand, the three-guard lineup could give the Mavericks fits on the perimeter while Wemby, looking for a bounceback performance after looking mortal against Utah, controls the paint en route to another victory.

Spurs Injuries: Tobias Harris – Out (calf), Jayden Quaintance – Out (knee).

Mavericks Injuries: Santi Aldama — Questionable (Knee), Tarik Biberović — Out (Heel), Max Christie — Out (Hip), Sergio de Larrea — Out (Knee), Dereck Lively II — Out (Foot).

Flagg is just in his second season, but judging by his recent performances, he seems on his way to superstardom sooner rather than later. The 19-year-old forward can do it all already, except consistently shoot from outside, and should only continue to get better. He has as good a case as anyone to be considered Wemby's biggest challenger for best player in the world in the near future, so it will be interesting to see what he can do against the most dominant defensive force in the league.

As mentioned, the Spurs don't have someone who can match up well with Flagg, at least physically, in their starting lineup, but Stephon Castle has shown he can bother bigger guys, and the big guy will be behind him to back him up.

San Antonio can win without Wembanyama playing at his highest level, but everything is easier when their best player dominates. He could do that against the Daniel Gafford – Morez Johnson Jr. duo, and he could definitely take advantage of Dusty May going small, like he did a couple of times, to terrible results, in their first matchup.

Wemby won't likely be guarded by Flagg for long, if at all, and vice versa, but whoever outplays the other could determine who emerges as the winner on Wednesday.

While the halfcourt battle will be interesting to watch, whichever team can control the transition game will have a huge advantage. The Spurs play at an average pace, but they lead the league in fastbreak points. Whenever San Antonio gets to run, the offense gets supercharged, and even lineups lacking shooting become viable.

It might be hard to get a huge advantage on the break, however, since the Mavericks allow the fourth-fewest fastbreak points in the league despite turning the ball over at a high rate. Dallas also prefers to play at a leisurely pace, so making sure they are forcing San Antonio into a halfcourt battle will likely be a priority for them.

In the first matchup between the two teams, the Mavs actually outscored the Spurs in transition, but it seemed like it was in part because, missing both Flagg and Irving, they just took every opportunity they could to run, as it was the only way they could score consistently. The Silver and Black also seemed to play down to their opponent at times, with some bad transition defense showing their lack of focus. That shouldn't happen again, or it could cost San Antonio a win.

The Spurs are more talented than the Mavericks overall, but they have at times found themselves lacking a spark outside of their main guys. The Mavericks, meanwhile, recently had great performances off the bench by Walter Clayton Jr., who dropped 20 on the Kings, and Morez Johnson Jr., who had 13 on just five shots against Sacramento. They have also had good showings from former No.1 overall pick Zaccharie Risacher and Naji Marshall this season.

One of those players will likely have to take Max Christie's starting spot, but they still have a bunch of guys who might not be dependable every night but can make an impact on any given game, as the Spurs learned while they were getting torched by the now-injured Sergio de Larrea in their first game against the Mavs.

The goal, clearly, will be to limit Irving and Flagg, but San Antonio will also have to keep an eye on the supporting cast to prevent someone from becoming an unlikely hero.

If you'd like to, you may follow along with the game on our Twitter profile (@poundingtherock) or visit our Game Thread!

#2890. Qualcomm stock drops 6% on weak earnings forecast as memory shortage weighs on smartphone sales

Qualcomm reported quarterly results on Wednesday that topped Wall Street estimates, but the chipmaker's earnings forecast came in lighter than expected. The stock slid 6% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

For the current quarter, Qualcomm said it sees earnings per share of between $2.15 and $2.35 on revenue of $10.4 billion to $11.2 billion. Analysts expected earnings per share of $2.27 on revenue of $10.48 billion.

Most of Qualcomm's chip sales come from system-on-chips and modems for smartphones. It's a market that's currently in turmoil as rising memory costs force handset makers to raise prices, hurting demand. Adjusted company revenue fell 7% during from a year earlier.

Qualcomm raised prices in September. Still, handset chip sales during the quarter fell 26% to $5.14 billion, trailing a StreetAccount estimate of $5.21 billion. Qualcomm said it's starting to see sequential growth in the category, and that Android shipments in China have bottomed, even though the company predicts the handset market will be flat in 2027.

Under CEO Cristiano Amon, Qualcomm has tried to turn investor attention to other kinds of chips. The company is looking to enter the fast-growing market for AI data center infrastructure. It had $650 million in data center revenue during the quarter, mostly from customizing and connectivity work, Amon said.

Amon said Qualcomm expects $5 billion in data center revenue in fiscal 2027. He said the company recently signed a customer for optical connectivity and had engaged a global hyperscaler.

"We don't have a demand issue; it's a supply issue," Amon said. "If I had more supply, I would probably be doing better."

Qualcomm also sells chips for cars and other automotive uses. Revenue in that division soared 89% on an annual basis to $1.99 billion, beating StreetAccount expectations of $1.66 billion.

Internet of things revenue includes low-power chips, smart glasses SoCs, laptop chips, as well as the company's bid to provide cellular infrastructure. Earlier on Wednesday, Qualcomm announced a deal to provide technology to SpaceX's cellular buidlout. IoT revenue increased 4% from a year earlier to $1.88 billion, slightly higher than estimates.

Qualcomm's QTL division includes revenue from licensing its intellectual property for 5G and other technologies to other companies. Qualcomm said during the quarter that it had renewed licensing agreements with Apple, Huawei, and Transsion.

"QTL will maintain its scale going forward," Amon said.

QTL revenue sank 7% to $1.3 billion, in line with a StreetAccount estimate.

#2889. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

"He was throwing deli meat out there," Rams safety Quentin Lake said on the St. Brown Podcast, sparking laughter from co-hosts Amon-Ra St. Brown and Equanimeous St. Brown. "He's seeing ghosts. ... Now 0-7, the Chargers have gone 27 consecutive offensive possessions without scoring a touchdown.

Chargers coach Jim Harbaugh shrugged off the comments in his Wednesday news conference.

"As far as anyone's opinion, I don't see that," Harbaugh said. :Everybody's entitled to their opinion, that's not what I see."

He added: "Pass game, I thought he did his job exceptionally well. Two throws he'd like to have back."

Herbert, however, took accountability for the continued turnovers.

"It's bad decisions, poor mistakes by me," Herbert said Sunday in a postgame news conference. "It's something I have to clean up."

#2885. Mike Vrabel addresses Patriots rumors ahead of NFL trade deadline

A pair of New England Patriots veterans found themselves in trade rumors ahead of the NFL's trade deadline.

This week, defensive tackle Christian Barmore and cornerback Carlton Davis were mentioned as potential trade candidates by Sports Illustrated's Albert Breer. According to Breer, trading either player would give New England an opportunity to add needed draft capital while getting younger.

Speaking on Wednesday, head coach Mike Vrabel responded to the report.

"I think sometimes national reports have the tendency to do that. I have not shared that information with anybody," Vrabel said. "You know, this time of year, I think that all these things kind of get drummed up. So we're focused on trying to prepare for Green Bay, make the corrections from the game. I mean, this is business as normal here. So, I don't know where a lot of these things come from. Certainly not any conversations with me."

Davis, 29, has played in four games this season while dealing with a neck injury. When healthy, he has occupied his usual starting role opposite Christian Gonzalez, with Vrabel highlighting his physicality and competitiveness.

Barmore, 27, has seen his playing time decrease slightly, playing just 50% of New England's defensive snaps this season after logging 65% last year. The defensive tackle has been productive at times, recording 3.5 sacks, his highest total since 2023.

Neither player has guaranteed money remaining on his contract beyond this season, making a potential trade a realistic possibility. However, would dealing away two defensive contributors make sense for a 5-3 Patriots team with aspirations of competing?

"I don't know. There's probably a bunch of ways we can make this team better," Vrabel responded. "You know, coach better, execute better, whether that's [adding] guys on the streets or — there's multiple ways. Again, we'd have these conversations each and every day."

Vrabel said he doesn't feel the need to discuss trade rumors with players, instead keeping conversations centered on football and the team's preparation.

The NFL trade deadline is set for Tuesday, Nov. 10, at 4 p.m. ET. With less than a week to finalize any moves, Vrabel's focus remains on the present.

"Right now, there'll be another week before the trade deadline," Vrabel said. "We'll be leaving for Munich next week. We're focused on today and how we get better and how we can run the ball better, how we can affect the quarterback. All these things that if we don't do, it's going to be tough."

#2884. How Shai Gilgeous-Alexander learned ‘personal energy’ matters amid Thunder’s injury-laden season

How Shai Gilgeous-Alexander learned 'personal energy' matters amid Thunder's injury-laden season appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

After the Oklahoma City Thunder suffered their worst regular-season loss — a 120-96 blowout by the Dallas Mavericks — All-Star guard Shai Gilgeous-Alexander was at a loss for words when the Houston Rockets handed them another 20+ point demise three days later. He looked defeated watching from the bench throughout the fourth quarter of a 119-98 rout in Houston.

It almost felt like the reality of the Thunder's 2026-27 season set in amid long-term injuries to Jalen Williams, Cason Wallace, and most recently, All-Star Chet Holmgren, who underwent biological treatment for a right knee injury and could miss up to two months. However, Gilgeous-Alexander didn't bring that same energy into the third stop of Oklahoma City's three-game road trip.

Instead, he put together his best performance of the young regular season, scoring a season-high 43 points and finishing with eight assists, two steals, and one block, while guiding the Thunder to a hard-fought 117-112 win against the Cleveland Cavs. After the game, Gilgeous-Alexander addressed his attitude amid the Thunder's injury-laden first two weeks.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

"The way the past week has gone, I needed to have better personal energy," Gilgeous-Alexander said. "It obviously was very emotional, and a lot really fast, but I think I had to get my mind to what I could control, and where we are as a team, and where my feet were. And I think my head wasn't where it should have been. I just tried to stay in the moment tonight more than anything, and I guess it paid off."

Thunder All-Star Shai Gilgeous-Alexander doesn't surround himself with yes men or people who tell him he's doing the right thing. Instead, the two-time MVP is supported by a group of people who aren't afraid to tell him when they don't agree with his approach, which is what Gilgeous-Alexander was most likely told after sulking on the bench during a second consecutive blowout loss.

"It's a little bit of what I was taught. Also, my inner circle, the people I have around me on a day-to-day, we don't sugarcoat things," Gilgeous-Alexander said. "We tell each other how it is and what it is. When you have people that are brutally honest around you, you get a chance to get better at things, ultimately, in life."

Thunder head coach Mark Daigneault isn't afraid to reprimand Gilgeous-Alexander, as SGA revealed after beating the Cavs. It's helped shape his mindset on how to guide the Thunder, coming off three consecutive seasons with the best record in the Western Conference standings, through their most trying campaign in recent years.

"My head now is just trying to figure out what winning will look like with this team, and this group, as myself, and the rest of the group have come so accustomed to what winning will look like with the group that we've had," Gilgeous-Alexander said. "Just trying to figure that out — that's all that matters. Trying to win games and figure out what it looks like.

"In the NBA, it's a team full of NBA players. Even when you might not have "enough talent" on paper, you still do. You just have to do it a different way, and we found that way tonight. And that's going to be the challenge going forward for the rest of the season."

While some have already ruled Shai Gilgeous-Alexander and the Thunder out as championship contenders, Gilgeous-Alexander says the team's goal of winning a second title in three years hasn't changed despite the untimely setbacks.

"For me, they don't change," Gilgeous-Alexander stated. "The same expectations we've always had as a group. I think the challenge has just changed. And how we're going to be able to get it done is going to be a little bit different. And that's the hard part, and the figuring out. Same expectations; it's just the path is going to look a little different now."

The Thunder will host the Cavs in a rematch at the Paycom Center on Wednesday.

#2883. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

#2882. Roundtable: What would it take for YOU to trade Kyrie Irving?

The scuttlebutt over the weekend was a series of quasi-nonsense reports about the Dallas Mavericks being open to trading Kyrie Irving. On Sunday, the Mavs Moneyball Slack was on fire at the ridiculousness of such an idea, particularly with the Mavericks sitting pretty at 4-2. Then national media outlets started chiming in on the subject so I asked anyone who really had a take to answer this: what WOULD it take for you to be happy if the Mavericks traded Kyrie Irving?

Tyler: Obviously, the Mavericks shouldn't trade Kyrie just for the sake of trading Kyrie, but there are plenty of scenarios where they should listen. The two teams in particular that I'd be interested in dealing with are Houston and Detroit. Houston has a treasure trove of draft assets from Brooklyn and Phoenix, and combined with plenty of young talent on the roster, you could easily put together a deal with a couple of those attractive first-rounders and a young player. Detroit is such a perfect Kyrie team, and they have all of their draft capital plus young guys in Ebuka Okorie, Ron Holland, and others. Again, some firsts, and Okorie and Holland and I'd probably go ahead and do that.

That's not an indictment of this Mavericks team. They've gotten off to a very nice start amid a very tough schedule. However, one of the fatal flaws of the Luka Doncic era was accelerating the timeline with the Porzingis deal. The Mavericks in keeping all of these vets around, with a severe lack of assets to properly build around Cooper when he reaches his prime, is far more of a pressing issue than being the eighth seed this year. This is a five-year plan, not a one-year deal. They need to be prudent about this.

Ben: I'm ready to fully move on into the next era of Mavericks basketball. They shouldn't trade for the sake of trading, but should also be realistic about the value of a soon-to-be 35 year old small guard coming off knee surgery.

If they get an offer of a first-round pick and an expiring salary, they should take it immediately.

Michael: I fully agree we should not trade Irving. I have never been on board with trading the veterans for a bunch of picks and full-on rebuilding in that manner. There is just far too much unknown in doing so. Getting younger and cheaper could just as easily mean "getting much worse" as it could mean "getting better." NBA teams need veterans, especially Hall of Famers who are willing to play as the second option to a 19-year-old. The Mavs have that almost-impossible-to-obtain scenario, and I don't think they should let it go.

But that doesn't answer the question. The question was, "if we do" trade Irving, what does it take for me. My answer to that question is "a lot." It would take a younger player that is a direct replacement for Irving in terms of production (or a year removed from all but certainly making that jump), along with at least two FIRST-round picks in consecutive drafts no more than one year removed from this offseason. That is simply something I don't see happening.

Irving is critically important to this team, and I think he gets underestimated by a big margin when it comes to assessing his value. I understand he is currently 34 years old and coming off a major injury, but we're not in 1996. In this modern era, we have Steph Curry at almost four years older to the day than Irving. Kevin Durant is three-and-a-half years older with a major injury under his belt. LeBron James is another one… Irving is playing at a high level and clearly helping this team win. Also, Irving has a player option next year, then is likely gettable for a reasonable contract thereafter. I don't feel as though he is hurting the team or the cap in such a way that can't be solved via other moves (plus the team will drop Klay Thompson's $17M cap hit after this season by simply doing nothing).

The only way I personally would be willing to trade him is basically if it was the most obvious no-brainer trade imaginable where Dallas is getting back a young but ready player and draft capital. It can't be something that ushers in a "hope for the best" youth movement and obviously hurts the team right off the bat with hopes of the team improving over time. If the Mavs make moves, it should be with the goal of improving immediately and going forward.

Bryan: To give up Kyrie, the Mavericks would need to be either flailing around the December 15th mark (when most players in the league become available for trade) or to be bowled over with an offer worth 3 or more first-round picks in value if they are above .500 and in good seeding position. Unless Cooper Flagg's production slows down tremendously or Kyrie gets hurt, there is no reason to tear any of the core pieces of this team off of it for a paltry trade return when they could simply carry a team performing well into the play-in/offs and allow the young players to gain incredibly valuable postseason experience. Everyone is clamoring for Kyrie Irving to be shipped off to a playoff team that could use him more. After a hot early start, the Dallas Mavericks are pounding on their chests asking, "Why not us?"

Salvator: I think for this year barring an obvious overpay, I think they should keep Kyrie at least until the offseason. There is still value in making the postseason and seeing how our guys play in that environment, kinda similar to the one year CP3 played in OKC.

Kirk: I can't fathom trading him. Any contender making an offer to Dallas has to come through with picks that are from a team that projects badly, like, I'm not interested in a Detroit 2028 first-round pick (well… I guess I am a little, what a draft class). There has to be something there for me to sink my teeth into as a person looking ahead. Right now, Kyrie is too valuable and too fun to consider otherwise. But if I was forced? Two first-round picks, and ones that look good right now.

#2881. KC Concepcion, Rome Odunze headline the most-targeted pass-catchers still without a TD

Every week at this time I do some data hunting to try to find some interesting fantasy angles. Let's pop open the spreadsheets and see what's interesting as we get ready for Week 9.

The Diontae Johnson Award is a silly little thing I coined back in 2022, when Johnson somehow collected 147 targets for the Steelers but didn't score a touchdown. The drought was especially surprising because he scored 15 times in the two previous seasons. Normally we chase the opportunity in fantasy football, and we expect the scores to come one way or another. But there are always usage trends and play-calling patterns to consider, too.

So who's at the top of the DJA leaderboard through eight weeks? We're looking at the pass-catchers (receivers and tight ends) with the most targets without any touchdowns.

Concepcion, Denzel Boston and Harold Fannin Jr. all have similar target and efficiency stats in Cleveland, but the focus is on Boston from in close; Boston has eight red-zone targets (and three overall touchdowns) while Fannin has four inside-the-20 targets (and three overall touchdowns). Concepcion is stuck on three red-zone targets and no scores yet.

I'd like to bet on talent, so let's stick with Concepcion. Alas, it would be nice if the Browns had more talent in their QB room.

Talent isn't a problem in Chicago, where Caleb Williams is a star and Ben Johnson runs a smart offense. But note how the team handles its red-zone targets: Luther Burden 11 (eight catches), Colston Loveland 10 (six catches), Kalif Raymond 5 (five catches) and Rome Odunze 5 (one catch). Sure, there have been missed connections, with Odunze dropping at least one goal-line chance. But he hasn't been the first read here, either.

I don't have much to say on Schultz, and Bourne is sadly done for the year after fracturing his tibia. But what the heck is going on with the Buccaneers? Forget passing touchdowns, they've thrown for just 64 yards the last two games — including zero yards in the rain last week against Atlanta.

Although the Buccaneers are now the favorites to win the NFC South, I'd be worried about all of their passing components. Obviously the last two games are screaming outliers, but this team is good at a few specific things — playing defense (first in DVOA), run-blocking and power running. There's no reason why the Bucs can't rely on 1970s football — and surging Bucky Irving and Kenneth Gainwell — as they look to wear down opponents.

And even when the Bucs do look downfield, what does Godwin have left in his ninth season? His 6.9 yards per target is a career low and he's averaging just 30.4 yards per game. Maybe we can leave the light on for Emeka Egbuka when he heals up, but the train might be gone for Godwin.

I don't include running backs in the Diontae Johnson fun, but I suppose it is interesting to note the four backs who have the most targets without a touchdown catch:

It doesn't feel as life and death when running backs aren't turning their targets into touchdowns. We just like to see them find ancillary ways to score fantasy points, and ways to stay relevant to game script even when their teams are in hurry-up mode and/or playing from behind.

McCaffrey's had plenty of years where he ran hot with receiving touchdowns — he had seven such scores in both 2023 and 2025. But it is interesting to note that his yards per catch has dropped to 7.4 this year, and his yards per target (6.0) is the lowest it's been since his rookie year.

With Brown, we're just hoping the Cincinnati carnival doesn't leave without him as it continues the barnstorming tour. But unless the Bengals make a trade for a back (Taylor could make sense here), Brown is probably fine. Samaje Perine and Tahj Brooks are not major threats to his playing time.

Taylor is actually averaging a career-best 25.3 receiving yards per game, so his managers can't be unhappy here. He should be fine moving forward, be it with a new and contending team, or still on the Colts but aiming at a friendly schedule in the second half.

Swift leads the NFL in rushing touchdowns — he did his Jerome Bettis short-yardage imitation Monday at Seattle — so his managers are smiling. It will be interesting to see how the Bears handle the backfield workload when Kyle Monangai is healthy. When the fantasy season is complete, you'll be happy you drafted some Bears proactively.

#2880. Colin Cowherd trolls Republicans after Democrats’ blue wave on election night

Colin Cowherd couldn't begin his Wednesday Fox Sports Radio show without needling Republicans over what was a big election night for Democrats.

Four years ago, Cowherd was roasted for predicting a "red wave" in the 2022 midterm elections, only to see the result end up vastly different than many expected. Cowherd opted against making any bold predictions ahead of the 2026 midterm elections. But when Democrats obliterated Republicans on election night to take control of the House and Senate, Cowherd appeared to take some pride in poking the right.

"I was gonna wear a red shirt today, but I woke up and just felt like blue was the more appropriate color," Cowherd said with a chuckle. "I told the people in the back, I'd get some blue shirts ready for the next two years, it's just a theme."

After losing the presidential election two years ago, Tuesday night was a big rebound for Democrats, winning control of the House and Senate, and the majority of governorships for the first time in 16 years. The blue wave that occurred might not relegate Trump to being a powerless president, but it strongly limits his agenda for the remainder of his term. Perhaps worse than that, Republican fans of The Herd were forced to suffer through seeing Cowherd in blue.

Cowherd largely avoids politics on his FS1 and Fox Sports Radio show, but he loves to drop little quips that are just enough to cause a stir. Whether it's announcing why he's wearing a blue shirt, praising Kamala Harris for being pleasant, chiding Democrats for losing the presidential election, ripping Donald Trump as a "con artist" or crediting him for being an "appealing" leader, Cowherd routinely tries to spark a response from both sides of the aisle.

Maybe Cowherd would have been a successful cable news provocateur if he landed at CNN or MSNBC as he has previously discussed. Or maybe Cowherd is just weighing in on the topic of the day more than he is offering an informed political opinion. But he's good at sparking a response. Just as he says things that tend to provoke sports fans, his little political quips and comments tend to get a reaction on social media.

The post Colin Cowherd trolls Republicans after Democrats' blue wave on election night appeared first on Awful Announcing.
